{"posts": [{"title": "21 Testing Mistakes - Part 4", "custom_excerpt": "The fourth part of my Testing Mistakes series", "published_at": "2023-04-07T06:25:14.000+00:00", "plaintext": "💡This part focuses on16. Ignoring mutation testing and not verifying if the tests are covering all cases17. Creating objects in every test instead of having object mothers18. Not using explicit data structures in tests, but referencing previously created ones19. Not caring about test code quality20. Always adding tests into only one file21. Ignoring test automation in the CI process🔗First partSecond partThird partFourth part\n\n\n16. Ignoring mutation testing and not verifying if the tests are covering all cases\n\nSometimes when writing complex code, you want to make sure your tests cover every case. To do that, you follow the logic and verify that a test for this exists. This is good, but there is one additional thing you can do to make sure everything is tested: Mutation Testing.\n\nTo perform Mutation Testing, all you need to do, is comment out some line or change some condition and then run your tests. If some test fails, then that logic / condition is tested. If everything passes, then it probably means that a test is missing for that chunk of code.\n\nOf course, don't go over board with this and check every line, you should probably focus on parts:\n\n * That you're not sure are tested correctly\n * Complex logic that is hard to follow\n * Really important parts of the system\n\nApplying Mutation testing will give you more confidence, that the SUT is correctly tested and that any regression will be caught by your Automated test suite.\n\n\n17. Creating objects in every test instead of having object mothers\n\nThis topic is point borrows from the helper functions section. Basically, instead of repeating the same instantiation / creation logic every time, you could create a helper function which creates it for you.\n\nThese object mothers can create the SUT and its dependencies or data structures which the SUT uses. This way you increase re-usability and protect from code changes.\n\nSuch helper functions also allow you to use default values, making the tests only pass in relevant parameters for that test, which makes them shorter and more explicit.\n\nHere we have a class which we would like to test:\n\nclass SomeClass(\n val database: Database,\n val timestampProvider: TimestampProvider,\n val someConverter: SomeConverter,\n) {\n // ...\n}\n\nAnd an object mother for it:\n\nfun createSomeClass(\n database: Database = FakeDatabase(),\n timestampProvider: TimestampProvider = FakeTimestampProvider(),\n someConverter: SomeConverter = SomeConverter(timestampProvider),\n): SomeClass {\n return SomeClass(database, timestampProvider, someConverter)\n}\n\nThis object mother provides for us with some default values, which we won't have to provide when using it.\n\n@Before\nfun setUp() {\n systemUnderTest = createSomeClass(fakeDatabase, fakeTimestampProvider)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun hasCorrectInitialValue() {\n assertEquals(true, systemUnderTest.isInitialized)\n}\n\nIn most cases, you will probably want to pass in some dependencies from the test, so they can be verified. However, implementation details like \"SomeConverter\" can be ignored, because the object mother will pass it in.\n\nThese types of object mother come in handy when you have to create this class in multiple tests, or you want to test instantiation logic by re-creating the SUT inside a test case.\n\nThis type of function can also be used for data structures:\n\ndata class AwkwardDataStructure(\n val valueA: OtherStructure,\n val valueB: OtherStructure,\n val valueC: OtherStructure\n)\n\ndata class OtherStructure(\n val id: String,\n val type: String,\n)\n\nfun createAwkwardDataStructure(\n valueA: OtherStructure = OtherStructure(\"\", \"\"),\n valueB: OtherStructure = OtherStructure(\"\", \"\"),\n valueC: OtherStructure = OtherStructure(\"\", \"\"),\n): AwkwardDataStructure \n return AwkwardDataStructure(valueA, valueB, valueC)\n}\n\nThanks to this function, we don't need to repeat so much boilerplate every time we create the awkward data structure. If a test only focuses on valueA, we just pass in valueA the other ones use the default values. This makes the test easier to understand, because we know to only focus on this one value.\n\n\n18. Not using explicit data structures in tests, but referencing previously created ones\n\nTests should explicitly tell you what they're focusing on, without having to hunt down the answer why the test behaved the way it did. Hiding the important values in a global variable will make the test harder to understand.\n\nFor examples sake, we have an SUT that takes in an Article Schema and based on its enum type returns a different article:\n\nenum class ArticleType {\n Text,\n Image,\n}\n\ndata class ArticleSchema(\n val headline: String,\n val content: String,\n val type: ArticleType,\n val imageUrls: List?,\n)\n\ndata class TextArticle(\n val headline: String,\n val content: String,\n)\n\ndata class ImageArticle(\n val headline: String,\n val content: String,\n val imageUrls: List,\n)\n\nThe image schema property is nullable, so there might be a case, where we want an image article, but we don't have an image, then it should be converted to a normal text one.\n\nHere's a test which has all the data structures prepared beforehand:\n\nprivate val TEXT_ARTICLE_SCHEMA =\n ArticleSchema(\"Text\", \"Content\", ArticleType.Text, null)\nprivate val IMAGE_ARTICLE_SCHEMA =\n ArticleSchema(\"Image\", \"Content\", ArticleType.Image, listOf(\"url\"))\nprivate val IMAGE_ARTICLE_WITHOUT_URLS_SCHEMA =\n ArticleSchema(\"Image\", \"Content\", ArticleType.Image, null)\n\nprivate val TEXT_ARTICLE =\n TextArticle(\"Text\", \"Content\")\nprivate val IMAGE_ARTICLE =\n ImageArticle(\"Image\", \"Content\", listOf(\"url\"))\nprivate val TEXT_ARTICLE_FROM_IMAGE_ARTICLE =\n TextArticle(\"Image\", \"Content\")\n\n@Test\nfun `Article Schema with text type results in TextArticle`() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(TEXT_ARTICLE_SCHEMA)\n\n assertEquals(result, TEXT_ARTICLE)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Article Schema with image type results in ImageArticle`() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(IMAGE_ARTICLE_SCHEMA)\n\n assertEquals(result, IMAGE_ARTICLE)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Article Schema with image type but no urls results in TextArticle`() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(IMAGE_ARTICLE_WITHOUT_URLS_SCHEMA)\n\n assertEquals(result, TEXT_ARTICLE_FROM_IMAGE_ARTICLE)\n}\n\nUsing the already defined data structure in every test makes the tests shorter, however in order to understand them we have to look at the data structure definitions. Additionally, if we have an edge case (like image without URLs) we need to also hunt down what variable which is responsible for that behavior.\n\nA better approach would be to create the data structure in the test and only specify what is relevant for that test:\n\n@Test\nfun `Article Schema with text type results in TextArticle`() {\n val schema = createSchema(\"Text\", \"Content\", ArticleType.Text)\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(schema)\n\n assertEquals(result, TextArticle(\"Text\", \"Content\"))\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Article Schema with image type results in ImageArticle`() {\n val schema = createSchema(\"Image\", \"Content\", ArticleType.Image, listOf(\"url\"))\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(schema)\n\n assertEquals(result, ImageArticle(\"Image\", \"Content\", listOf(\"url\")))\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Article Schema with image type but no urls results in TextArticle`() {\n val schema = createSchema(\"Image\", \"Content\", ArticleType.Image, null)\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(schema)\n\n assertEquals(result, TextArticle(\"Image\", \"Content\"))\n}\n\nprivate fun createSchema(\n headline: String,\n content: String,\n type: ArticleType,\n imageUrls: List? = null,\n): ArticleSchema {\n return ArticleSchema(headline, content, type, imageUrls)\n}\n\nThis makes the tests a little longer, but a lot more straight forward. To help with repetitions, a parameterized test case could be created and to improve the readability additional string values could be extracted and maybe even more helper functions.\n\nGenerally, the more relevant information we have in the test body, the easier it should be to understand what's going on. However, too much information will obfuscate it, try to find a middle ground of verbosity and readability.\n\n\n19. Not caring about test code quality\n\nA lot of the times, test code is neglected, which makes it \"rot\", with time such rot will make the tests hard to change in order to fit the production code. Failing tests will be removed, and new tests won't be added. This causes our automated test suite to be a lot less reliable, and with time it can cause us a lot of issues.\n\nTo avoid this, it is important that you clean up / refactor your test code from time to time just as you would production code. Clean tests are easy to fix, understand and maintain, which in the long run speeds up our development because regressions are caught a lot faster.\n\nJust like with writing tests, it is an up-front investment, because writing tests is extra code. In the long run, these tests are a lot better at caching errors than humans are. They be executed in a fraction of what manual testing can achieve.\n\nKeep your tests clean because:\n\n * hard to understand tests will be hard to fix\n * hard to fix tests will be probably removed\n * without tests, more and more bugs will land in production\n * fixing these bugs will slow down development\n\n\n20. Always adding tests into only one file\n\nSometimes a class starts small, but more and more logic is added to it. If the class is not split up, or it doesn't make sense to do so, then tests will grow with it, but in a much faster rate.\n\nThis will result in a test file that has over 500 lines of code, which might seem good, because all edge cases are covered. However, big files are hard to reason about, they might have a lot of set-up or helper functions which are only used in a portion of tests.\n\nTo help with this, instead of having one a 1:1 relation for test file and SUT, you can split out the test classes and group them by relevant logic. If some group of tests only focus on one behavior, then it's a good candidate for a separate file.\n\nTake for example an SUT which manages comments with the following data structure:\n\ndata class Comment(\n val text: String,\n val postDateIso: String = \"\",\n val upVotes: Int = 0,\n val downVotes: Int = 0,\n val wasReported: Boolean = false,\n)\n\nThe system can vote, report, sort and also parses the text from HTML. The test could look like this:\n\nclass CommentsTest {\n \n @Test fun `Comment content is correctly parsed from HTML`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Up voting a neutral comment increases upVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Up voting an up voted comment does not update votes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Up voting a down voted comment reduces downVotes and increases upVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Down voting a neutral comment increases upVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Down voting an down voted comment does not update votes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Down voting an up voted comment reduces upVotes and increases downVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Initially comment is not reported`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Reporting comment sets the correct flag`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by newest works correctly`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by oldest works correctly`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by up votes works correctly`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by down votes works correctly`() { /**/ }\n // more tests....\n \n private fun setUpParsing() { /**/ }\n private fun setUpVoting() { /**/ }\n private fun setUpReporting() { /**/ }\n private fun setUpSorting() { /**/ }\n}\n\nThat's a lot of tests for a lot of edge cases, just by looking at their names we can see that they are grouped by functionality however they are still in one file which can grow exponentially and become really hard to manage.\n\nA better approach would be to extract these tests to specific test files:\n\nclass CommentsVotingTest {\n\n @Test fun `Up voting a neutral comment increases upVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Up voting an up voted comment does not update votes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Up voting a down voted comment reduces downVotes and increases upVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Down voting a neutral comment increases upVotes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Down voting an down voted comment does not update votes`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Down voting an up voted comment reduces upVotes and increases downVotes`() { /**/ }\n\n private fun setUpVoting() { /**/ }\n}\n\nclass CommentsSortingTest {\n\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by newest works correctly`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by oldest works correctly`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by up votes works correctly`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Sorting comments by down votes works correctly`() { /**/ }\n\n private fun setUpSorting() { /**/ }\n}\n\nclass CommentsMiscellaneousTest {\n\n @Test fun `Comment content is correctly parsed from HTML`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Initially comment is not reported`() { /**/ }\n @Test fun `Reporting comment sets the correct flag`() { /**/ }\n // more tests....\n\n private fun setUpParsing() { /**/ }\n private fun setUpReporting() { /**/ }\n}\n\nThis way, all the behavior specific set-up or helper functions are only present in that test file, which makes the other tests files smaller and easier to reason about. For behaviors which have only a small amount of tests, and they can't be sensibly grouped, they could stay in the original file which I usually name a MiscellaneousTest.\n\nIf you want to change something with sorting, you will probably only need to look into the Sorting test, which is smaller, and only focused on that behavior. The only problem with this approach is the tests, which are in the \"Miscellaneous\" are harder to find. However, this is only an issue, because the other tests are so nicely grouped.\n\n* An SUT object mother should be used for these test files to avoid repeating the same boilerplate.\n\n\n21. Ignoring test automation in the CI process\n\nSometimes we want to introduce a quick change to our project and forget to run our test suit. If the PR is approved, and we don't have any test automation, we will probably merge it. Sometimes these quick changes introduce bugs, and without automation it is really easy to introduce bugs to our main branch.\n\nTests should be run as often as possible to ensure that nothing is broken, we can't only rely on our human memory to remember about them. They should run automatically on every change we want to introduce to our codebase.\n\nThis is where a Code Review CI/CD process comes in handy, anytime we want to merge something to the main branch, the CI will run our tests and if nothing breaks it will allow us to merge the change. However, if we introduce a regression, the CI will let us know and hopefully block us from merging until we fix the issue.\n\nThe CI is an important tool in modern development, it helps us with eliminating the human error (i.e. forgetfulness) part of software development.", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", "slug": "testing", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#871807", "url": "/tag/testing/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/testing-mistakes-part-4/", "excerpt": "The fourth part of my Testing Mistakes series"}, {"title": "21 Testing Mistakes - Part 3", "custom_excerpt": "The third part of my Testing Mistakes series", "published_at": "2023-04-07T06:11:45.000+00:00", "plaintext": "💡This part focuses on11. Not focusing on boundary values12. Using \"private\" functions in tests13. Too generic test case naming14. Conditions in tests15. Regular loops instead of Parameterized / Table Tests🔗First partSecond partThird partFourth part\n\n\n11. Not focusing on boundary values\n\nSometimes when our System Under Test might be using numbers in some conditions. For example, checking if a number is greater or equal:\n\nclass SomeKindOfCalculator {\n\n fun isValueOverTheLimit(number: Int): Boolean {\n return number >= 5\n }\n}\n\nWe could write a passing tests using values which are far above or below the \"limit\":\n\n@Test\nfun sevenIsOverTheLimit() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.isValueOverTheLimit(7)\n \n assertEquals(true, result)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun twoIsUnderTheLimit() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.isValueOverTheLimit(2)\n\n assertEquals(false, result)\n}\n\nBut such tests will still pass even if we change the limit number, which is probably not a desired outcome. The number 5 is an important part of the SUT, so the tests should probably fail when it is changed by accident:\n\n@Test\nfun fiveIsOverTheLimit() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.isValueOverTheLimit(5)\n\n assertEquals(true, result)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun fourIsUnderTheLimit() {\n val result = systemUnderTest.isValueOverTheLimit(4)\n\n assertEquals(false, result)\n}\n\nThe first iteration of the tests can produce a False Negative (Test passes even though production code is broken). These types of tests give us a false comfort of security, because the regressions might not be caught by our automated test suite. It will be either need to be caught by manual testing, or worse it will land production and cause us some problems.\n\nFocusing on boundary values, helps us avoid this issue altogether, giving us more confidence when releasing and shortening the development loop. Because if some issue is caught by a Manual test, we will probably be focusing on something different at the moment, but we will need to go back to this issue, change context, fix it and hope it will pass.\n\n\n12. Using \"private\" functions in tests\n\nPrivate functions are private for a reason, they are implementation details of the system under test and should not be exposed to the outside just for testing.\n\nInstead of testing the private function, you should be testing the function which calls the private function, this is the expected public behavior of your SUT. Testing private functions, could lead to a situation where you have great test coverage, but production is still broken. The reason for this is that the private function is only called by tests and not by production code. This would not happen if tests were written for the public function.\n\nIf the private function has so many edge cases that it needs to be tested in isolation, then this means that it's a great candidate for a separate Unit. Extracting a separate class makes the behavior easier to understand, and allow for testing all the edge cases in isolation. Remember that you still need to make sure that the original SUT has a test which verifies that the extracted class is used, either through a mock, or just using the real implementation and checking just one condition branch / edge case.\n\n\n13. Too generic test case naming\n\nNaming is hard, and naming tests is no exception. A well written test case name, will convey what the expected behavior of our SUT is, and what is needed to achieve this desired outcome.\n\nWhen a test with a good name fails, you should be able to figure out what went wrong, even without delving into the code. This is also helpful for example on automatic CI runs, which run outside your IDE.\n\nWhen naming tests, most of the time you can avoid glue words like: \"A\", \"The\". As long as it is understandable, then the name is fine. Glue words usually don't add anything to the explanation, they just fit some grammar requirements, however there are cases when their use makes the name better.\n\nIn my opinion, the most important thing is to have the name focus on the SUT outcome / behavior and why it happened. This should be enough to understand what is going on.\n\nIf the name is really long or hard to understand, you can take it as a hint that maybe the SUT is doing too much and could be split into more units. This would make the tests more granular and easier to understand.\n\n\n14. Conditions in tests\n\nTests should be as straight forward as it is possible, given we have this set-up when doing this action, then we expect that this happens. Once we start introduction conditions, it becomes much harder to understand.\n\nGiven we have this set-up when doing this action then we expect that this happens but only if we set up some value to A, if we set it to B, then something different happens. Take for example this test which asserts something different based on a boolean value from a Fake:\n\n@Test\nfun messageIsSentOnSenderSuccess() {\n fakeSender.isSuccessful = true\n\n systemUnderTest.send(\"1234\")\n\n assertSentMessage()\n}\n\n@Test\nfun messageIsNotSentOnSenderFailure() {\n fakeSender.isSuccessful = false\n\n systemUnderTest.send(\"1234\")\n\n assertSentMessage()\n}\n\nprivate fun assertSentMessage() {\n if (fakeSender.isSuccessful) {\n assertEquals(fakeSender.messages, listOf(\"1234\"))\n } else {\n assertEquals(fakeSender.messages, emptyList())\n }\n}\n\nThe above test case names are descriptive, but without diving into the implementation of assertSentMessage, it's hard to understand why both the success and failure test case use the same assertion.\n\nA better approach would be to be more explicit with the assertion naming without using conditions:\n\n@Test\nfun messageIsSentOnSenderSuccess() {\n fakeSender.isSuccessful = true\n\n systemUnderTest.send(\"1234\")\n\n assertMessageWasSent(\"1234\")\n}\n\n@Test\nfun messageIsNotSentOnSenderFailure() {\n fakeSender.isSuccessful = false\n\n systemUnderTest.send(\"1234\")\n\n assertMessageWasNotSent()\n}\n\nprivate fun assertMessageWasSent(message: String) {\n assertEquals(fakeSender.messages, listOf(message))\n}\n\nprivate fun assertMessageWasNotSent() {\n assertEquals(fakeSender.messages, emptyList())\n}\n\nThis way, it's much easier to see the difference between the two test cases, because their assertions are different. Additionally, the message is passed in explicitly to the assertion helper function, making it easier to understand and is more extendable for future test cases.\n\nOne condition splits the logic into two branches and makes it harder to follow, because you have to keep in mind both paths. Tests should be plain simple and easy to follow reading from top to bottom, without going back and re-checking (e.g. other conditional branches).\n\n\n15. Regular loops instead of Parameterized / Table Tests\n\nSometimes we don't want to keep our test code DRY, so instead of repeating the same test with different values, we loop through it, but that is not the best idea. When such a test fails:\n\n 1. We don't know which index / iteration causes the failure unless we provide some extra logic / infrastructure to make it possible, which in turns complicates our test code more.\n 2. If we don't use soft assertions, a failure will prevent further iteration to run. So if it fails at the first index, the rest of them are not executed.\n 3. We lose the ability to descriptively name our test, because we will only have one test.\n\n@Test\nfun validVersionsReturnTrue() {\n val validVersions = listOf(\"1.0\", \"1.2\", \"1.3\", \"1.5\")\n\n validVersions.forEach { version ->\n assertEquals(systemUnderTest.isValid(version), true)\n }\n}\n\nIf some version is not valid, we get something like this: expected: but was:. We don't know which version failed, just that something did fail. To help with this issue, we could add some print statements, or change the assertion to include the version name, but there is a better way.\n\nMost testing frameworks provide an easy way to write multiple test cases for different input (Parameterized tests or Table tests):\n\n@ParameterizedTest\n@ValueSource(strings = [\"1.0\", \"1.2\", \"1.3\", \"1.5\"])\nfun validVersionsReturnTrue(version: String) {\n assertEquals(systemUnderTest.isValid(version), true)\n}\n\nThis approach is much more concise, and the best part is that it gives us a list of which test case passes / fails. No more confusion and debugging when trying to find out which input causes an error.\n\nIf your framework does not provide such tools, you can just create a \"testCase\" helper function:\n\n@Test\nfun version1_0isValid() = testCase(\"1.0\")\n\n@Test\nfun version1_2isValid() = testCase(\"1.2\")\n\n@Test\nfun version1_3isValid() = testCase(\"1.3\")\n\n@Test\nfun version1_5isValid() = testCase(\"1.5\")\n\nprivate fun testCase(version: String) {\n assertEquals(systemUnderTest.isValid(version), true)\n}\n\nIt is more verbose, but it is still better than a regular loop, because the test results are still easy to understand, so you know at first glance what failed.", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", "slug": "testing", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#871807", "url": "/tag/testing/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/testing-mistakes-part-3/", "excerpt": "The third part of my Testing Mistakes series"}, {"title": "5 Beginner Testing Mistakes I Noticed While Working with Less Experienced Developers", "custom_excerpt": "Having a good test suite helps with catching regressions / bugs a lot faster, and gives developers more confidence when merging / releasing their product. In this series, I'll share my recommendations for improving your tests.", "published_at": "2023-01-20T16:01:57.000+00:00", "plaintext": "I've been a big fan of testing ever since I was introduced to it, most of the code I write is written with Test Driven Development. Having a good test suite helps with catching regressions / bugs a lot faster, and gives developers more confidence when merging / releasing their product.\n\nThat's why I encourage / expect my teammates to write tests for the important parts of the code (In our case, it's the shared KMM codebase for Android and iOS). When reviewing PRs, I sometimes see testing approaches which could be better, in this series, I'll share my recommendations for improving your tests.\n\nA lot of these tips are related with each other, and sometimes they complement each other. However, I'll try to keep every point straight forward and make it easy to understand without dragging additional information into them.\n\n💡The first part focuses on1. Arrange Act Assert2. Not creating helper functions3. Unnecessary set up / Arrangement4. Testing multiple things instead of focusing on one5. Not using explicit set-up values in tests, but depending on default or global values\n\nThis series was continued in three more parts, here's the full list of topics which I wrote about:\n\n💡6. Overusing mock frameworks7. Testing implementation details instead of the outcome8. Comparing whole complex data structures instead of only the tested fields9. Not using Soft assertions10. Repeating the SUT Algorithm in assertions instead of an explicit value11. Not focusing on boundary values12. Using \"private\" functions in tests13. Too generic test case naming14. Conditions in tests15. Regular loops instead of Parameterized / Table Tests16. Ignoring mutation testing and not verifying if the tests are covering all cases17. Creating objects in every test instead of having object mothers18. Not using explicit data structures in tests, but referencing previously created ones19. Not caring about test code quality20. Always adding tests into only one file21. Ignoring test automation in the CI process\n\nThese parts are available here:\n\n🔗Second partThird partFourth part\n\nIf you're interested and like have access to them subscribe to my newsletter or sign up right here on my website (No passwords required).\n\nNote: All the examples here, are written with Kotlin, however, I believe that the tips presented here, could be applied in most modern programming languages.\n\n\n1. Not using the Arrange Act Assert (Triple A) Convention\n\nOften times I see developers group the test code like they would on production code, i.e. based on what logic is related. In tests, however, there exists a common convention / pattern which splits the code into three distinct groups separated by a new line:\n\n@Test\nfun something() {\n givenApiReturnsSuccess() // arrange / set up\n \n val result = systemUnderTest.fetchDetail(\"1\") // act / action\n \n assertEquals(Detail(\"1\"), result) // assert / verify\n}\n\nThese groups don't have to be one-liners, but it would be best to keep the Act group as small as possible (preferably one line). Keeping the AAA convention in all the tests helps with understanding:\n\n * What set-up is needed\n * What action is taken to achieve the outcome\n * What is verified by the test.\n\n\n2. Not creating helper functions\n\nTests almost always include some boilerplate, be it set up code, verification and sometimes even the action. Helper functions help address this issue\n\n * Help with test readability by hiding irrelevant implementation details. With a readable test, it is much easier to understand what the system under test does\n * Protects from code changes, e.g: instead of changing a parameter in every test case, it only needs to be changed in the helper function\n\nTake a look at the following test:\n\n@Test\nfun storageItemsAreReturnedWithTimestamp() {\n val item1 = StorageItem(id = 1, name = \"\")\n val item2 = StorageItem(id = 2, name = \"\")\n fakeStorage.items = listOf(item1, item2)\n val timestamp = 123\n fakeTimestampProvider.value = timestamp\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.fetchFromStorage()\n\n val result1 = result.first { item -> item.id == 1 }\n assertEquals(timestamp, result1.timestamp)\n val result2 = result.first { item -> item.id == 2 }\n assertEquals(timestamp, result2.timestamp)\n}\n\nIn this example, we have a systemUnderTest (SUT) which retrieves items from a storage and adds the current timestamp to them. However, the code includes some boilerplate that will most likely be present in other test cases, an improved version could look like this:\n\n@Test\nfun storageItemsAreReturnedWithTimestamp() {\n fakeStorage.items = listOf(createStorageItem(1), createStorageItem(2))\n val timestamp = 123\n fakeTimestampProvider.value = timestamp\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.fetchFromStorage()\n\n verifyItemHasCorrectTimestamp(result, id = 1, expectedTimestamp = timestamp)\n verifyItemHasCorrectTimestamp(result, id = 2, expectedTimestamp = timestamp)\n}\n\nprivate fun createStorageItem(id: String): StorageItem {\n return StorageItem(id = 1, name = \"\")\n}\n\nprivate fun verifyItemHasCorrectTimestamp(\n items: List,\n id: Int,\n expectedTimestamp: Int\n) {\n val item = result.first { item -> item.id == id }\n assertEquals(expectedTimestamp, item.timestamp)\n}\n\nThe above code is more verbose judging just by the number of lines, however the more test cases we have, the more we benefit from such helper functions.\n\nOther examples:\n\nfun givenApiReturnsSuccess(id: String, result: Detail) {\n mockk { fetchDetail(id) } returns result\n}\n\nfun givenDatabaseContainsItemWithId(id: String) {\n val item = ComplexItem(id = id, name = \"\", timestamp = 0L)\n fakeStorage.items = listOf(item)\n}\n\nfun executeAndAdvanceTime() {\n systemUnderTest.execute()\n advanceTimeBy(milliseconds = 2000)\n}\n\n\nOne additional example I like to use is a \"testCase\" helper function. From time to time you will have a behavior which requires a lot of tests, with slightly different set-up / assertions:\n\n@Test\nfun `Timestamp of 0 becomes \"00 00 000\"`() {\n formatTestCase(0, \"00:00:000\")\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Timestamp of 500 becomes \"00 00 500\"`() {\n formatTestCase(500, \"00:00:500\")\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Timestamp of 59999 becomes \"00 59 999\"`() {\n formatTestCase(59999, \"00:59:999\")\n}\n\nprivate fun formatTestCase(\n timestamp: Long,\n expectedString: String\n) {\n val result = systemUnderTest.format(timestamp)\n\n result shouldBe expectedString\n}\n\nThese types of helper functions can be even more complex, as long as they help with the readability and maintainability of the test.\n\nHowever, there is one important point I'd like to address before going to the next point. Don't force out these helper functions, as the name suggests, they should help you and not make your life harder.\n\nThere are cases where it's better to leave the code inline without any helper functions. Like with everything in programming, this knowledge comes with experience, as long as the helper functions make the tests easier to understand and maintain, go for it.\n\n\n3. Unnecessary set up / Arrangement\n\nSometimes when creating a new test case, we copy an existing one, and change it to fit a new test case. When doing, so we sometimes copy more than we need, tests should only contain code which is relevant to that particular test case. More code in the tests, means it will be harder to understand, and sometimes even confuse the reader because some parts are irrelevant.\n\nImagine, we have a function which always retrieves something from a database cache (the function never calls the API):\n\n@Test\nfun databaseIsUsedToRetrieveCachedValue() {\n val item = Item(...)\n givenDatabaseContainsItem(item)\n givenApiReturnsSuccess(item)\n \n val result = systemUnderTest.fetchFromStorage()\n \n assertEquals(item, result)\n}\n\nThe above test is confusing for two reasons: looking at the set-up we can take a guess that the action uses both the database and the API, additional confusion is added, because we're not sure if the item comes from the database or from the API. Only inspecting / debugging the production code would give us the answer.\n\nKeeping our test code focused on only the relevant things will make our test cases easier to understand. In this case, removing the givenApiReturnsSuccess would reduce the confusion by a lot:\n\n@Test\nfun databaseIsUsedToRetrieveCachedValue() {\n val item = Item(...)\n givenDatabaseContainsItem(item)\n \n val result = systemUnderTest.fetchFromStorage()\n \n assertEquals(item, result)\n}\n\n\n4. Testing multiple things instead of focusing on one\n\nMost tests should focus on one particular behavior of the system under test. However, sometimes tests are created in ways that verify multiple things. At first sight it might seem beneficial, because less code should make it easier to understand, right?\n\n@Test\nfun stateAfterInitializingIsCorrect() {\n givenApiReturns(listOf(1, 2, 3))\n\n systemUnderTest.initialize()\n \n assertEquals(false, systemUnderTest.isLoading)\n assertEquals(listOf(), systemUnderTest.selectedItems)\n assertEquals(listOf(1, 2, 3), systemUnderTest.items)\n}\n\nWell not exactly, the problems arise, when the tests stop passing. Then you have to first look at the test code, and if that doesn't suffice you'll have to debug it, which is not ideal... That's why it's better to break it up into more test cases:\n\n@Test\nfun isLoadingIsFalseAfterInitializing() {\n systemUnderTest.initialize()\n \n assertEquals(false, systemUnderTest.isLoading)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun selectedItemsAreEmptyAfterInitializing() {\n systemUnderTest.initialize()\n \n assertEquals(listOf(), systemUnderTest.selectedItems)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun itemsAreRetrievedFromTheApiOnInitialization() {\n givenApiReturns(listOf(1, 2, 3))\n \n systemUnderTest.initialize()\n \n assertEquals(listOf(1, 2, 3), systemUnderTest.items)\n}\n\nHaving separate test cases for the verification involves more code, but the tests should be easier to understand, because they focus only on one aspect of the SUT. The benefit of that is, that if the test fails, the name should be more than enough to understand what's wrong with the SUT (As long as the test name is descriptive...)\n\nIt's important to understand and separate things that are different in each test case.\n\n\n5. Not using explicit set-up values in tests, but depending on default or global values\n\nTests should be as explicit as possible (while not hindering its readability).\n\nSometimes when we define a test double, we might be tempted to put default values in order to reduce the amount of code we need to write in our tests. In my opinion this is not the best approach, test doubles should not have hidden values which make the tests pass, takes this for example:\n\ninterface Config {\n val numberOfRepetitions: Int\n}\n\nclass FakeConfig : Config {\n override var numberOfRepetitions: Int = 3\n}\n\nclass SomeTest {\n // ...\n\n @Test\n fun apiCallOnFailShouldBeRepeatedTheCorrectAmountOfTimes() {\n mockApi.shouldReturnError()\n\n systemUnderTest.fetch()\n\n assertEquals(3, mockApi.fetchCallCount)\n }\n}\n\nIn the example above, we have a hidden default value in inside FakeConfig. This value is not set explicitly in the tests, so without delving into the implementation details, we won't know where the magic number 3 comes from. Of course, we could rename the test to be even more wordy, but it's better to let the test code speak for itself:\n\nclass FakeConfig : Config {\n override var numberOfRepetitions: Int = 0\n}\n\nclass SomeTest {\n ...\n\n @Test\n fun apiCallOnFailShouldBeRepeatedTheCorrectAmountOfTimes() {\n mockApi.shouldReturnError()\n val repetitionAmount = 3\n fakeConfig.numberOfRepetitions = repetitionAmount\n\n systemUnderTest.fetch()\n\n assertEquals(repetitionAmount, mockApi.fetchCallCount)\n }\n}\n\nIn my opinion, the above test is much easier to understand and the systemUnderTest behavior is much more explicit, even if it takes up more lines.\n\n\nIf you'd like to see more:\n\n🔗Second partThird partFourth part", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", "slug": "testing", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#871807", "url": "/tag/testing/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/5-beginner-testing-mistakes-i-noticed-while-working-with-less-experienced-developers/", "excerpt": "Having a good test suite helps with catching regressions / bugs a lot faster, and gives developers more confidence when merging / releasing their product. In this series, I'll share my recommendations for improving your tests."}, {"title": "21 Testing Mistakes - Part 2", "custom_excerpt": "The second part of my Testing Mistakes series", "published_at": "2023-04-07T06:00:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "💡This part focuses on6. Overusing mock frameworks7. Testing implementation details instead of the outcome8. Comparing whole complex data structures instead of only the tested fields9. Not using Soft assertions10. Repeating the SUT Algorithm in assertions instead of an explicit value🔗First partSecond partThird partFourth part\n\n\n6. Overusing mock frameworks\n\nLet me start off by saying, I'm not a big fan of mock frameworks because, they increase the test suite run-time and usually decrease re-usability by repeating the same mock set-up instead of creating one re-usable test double.\n\nThis repeated mock framework boilerplate, sooner or later, will break in a lot of places when something changes (e.g. its contract). This is the exact opposite of what we do in production code (keeping things DRY). However, having a re-usable test double follows that good practices, because we don't repeat ourselves.\n\nThese handwritten test doubles are written by us, and can be extended to suit our needs. For example, we could have additional functions which we can use for verification avoiding some boilerplate and protect ourselves from additional changes:\n\nclass FakeNavigator : Navigator {\n\n var commands: List = emptyList()\n \n override fun navigate(command: NavigationCommand) {\n commands.add(command)\n }\n\n fun assertLatestCommandEquals(item: Item) {\n assertEquals(item, commands.last())\n }\n}\n\nAnother thing with mocking frameworks is that they are also easy to misuse by testing implementation details of the SUT instead of its behavior / outcome. This in turn leads to brittle test suites that break frequently when changing some implementation details even though the behavior stays the same. I will elaborate on this more in the next point.\n\nI do see the benefit of mocking frameworks and use them sometimes, however in my opinions they have more downsides than upsides. If you want to keep using mocking frameworks, go ahead. My only suggestion would be that if you have classes / interfaces which are mocked frequently, create a Mock Wrapper or a Test Double by hand, it will keep your test code DRY and encourage more re-usability.\n\nMocking is not practical — Use fakesThis article talks about the benefits fakes provide over mocks in testing software. Fakes lead to better API and readable/robust tests.MediumPravin SonawaneReplacing Mocks :: Ryan HarterAfter reading a recent post by Sam Edwards detailing how he wraps mock objects for tests, I had a good discussion with him and others about the alternative: simply making Fake implementations.Replacing MocksComment from discussion m0zzie's comment from discussion \"Do you use the Impl suffix if you only have one concrete class on your interface?\".\n\n\n7. Testing implementation details instead of the outcome\n\nBefore delving into the details, I want to point out that the popular \"mock\" represents all form of test doubles: Fakes, Stubs, Spies etc. Because of this, it's hard to differentiate between different types of test doubles. In most cases, a Stub or a Fake should cover most of the needs, however with mock frameworks, usually everything becomes a mock.\n\nMock verification can lead to brittle tests when it is not used correctly. Let me show you this through a SUT which retrieves items from a database:\n\n@Test\nfun databaseIsCalledWhenRetrievingItems() {\n sut.getItems()\n\n verify {\n mockDatabase.getAll()\n }\n}\n\nThe test passes, so this means that everything works, right? Well, not exactly, we just verify the implementation details that the database was called, not that its return value is used. The following implementation passes, but it doesn't exactly behave how we expect:\n\nclass SystemUnderTest(private val database: Database) {\n \n fun getItems(): List {\n database.getAll()\n return emptyList()\n }\n}\n\nA better way to test this would be to use the \"Stubbing\" functionality that most mock frameworks provide:\n\n@Test\nfun databaseItemsAreRetrieved() {\n val items = listOf(Item(\"1\"), Item(\"2\"))\n every { mockDatabase.getAll() } returns items\n \n val result = sut.getItems()\n\n assertEquals(items, result)\n}\n\nThe test above doesn't verify that the database was called directly, it simply checks if the SUT returned value is the same as what we set up in the mock. This way we actually verify the behavior of the SUT which is \"returning items from a database\" not just the implementation details. I know that the above example is really trivial, but I just wanted to get the point across, and believe me, I sometimes see the test suites where most of the focus is on the implementation details, instead of the behavior of the SUT.\n\nIn most cases, it's better to set up return values and verify that the SUT uses them correctly, instead of just checking if a function was called. However, there are cases where we want to verify that some function was called a certain amount of times, for example when checking if a function correctly returns early in some conditions. In these situations verifying the number of calls is fine, because it is part of the SUT behavior, e.g. \"It shouldn't call the API again if the previous request was a success\".\n\nIn the above database example, instead of using a mocking framework to set up the return value, I would simply create a FakeDatababase, which will probably be an in-memory database that behaves like the real implementation:\n\ninterface Database {\n fun getAll(): List\n \n fun addItem(item: Item)\n}\n\ninterface FakeDatabase : Database {\n var items: List = emptyList()\n\n override fun getAll(): List = items\n\n override fun addItem(item: Item) {\n items.add(item)\n }\n}\n\n@Test\nfun databaseItemsAreRetrieved() {\n val items = listOf(Item(\"1\"), Item(\"2\"))\n fakeDatabase.items = items\n\n val result = sut.getItems()\n\n assertEquals(items, result)\n}\n\nThis fake implementation can be easily re-used across multiple tests, and makes the test code work closer to how the production code does it. That is because we're still using a database, but it is just an in-memory one.\n\nIf you're interested about different types of Test Doubles and their purpose, checkout this write-up:\n\nbliki: TestDoubleTest Double is generic term for fakes, mocks, stubs, dummies and spies.martinfowler.comMartin Fowler\n\n\n8. Comparing whole complex data structures instead of only the tested fields\n\nA lot of tests verify correctness by comparing Data Structures, usually as a result of some conversion from another type (e.g. from API or Database). However, sometimes the data structure has fields that have some complex logic behind them.\n\nIdeally, such complex logic should be tested in isolation. Otherwise, changes in unrelated parts could cause the complex logic tests to fail, even though they should pass.\n\nFor arguments’ sake, take a look at this example, which uses some hidden logic for determining a Person's Title\n\nenum Title { Mr, Mrs, Ms, Miss }\n\ndata class Person(\n val name: String,\n val age: Int,\n val title: Title,\n)\n\nfun createPerson(name: String, age: String): Person {\n // name and age is just assigned as is\n // title is calculated based on age and name\n}\n\n@Test\nfun bobAge5IsCorrectlyCreated() {\n val result = createPerson(\"Bob\", 5)\n\n val expectedResult = Person(\"Bob\", 5, Title.Mr)\n assertEquals(expectedResult, result)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun bethanyAge30IsCorrectlyCreated() {\n val result = createPerson(\"Bethany\", 30)\n\n val expectedResult = Person(\"Bethany\", 30, Title.Mrs)\n assertEquals(expectedResult, result)\n}\n\n...\n\n\n\nAs you can see, every test repeats the assertions for the name and age property. If we were to add a new property the Person structure, all tests would need to be updated to include that property, and if the new field is complex then every test would need to take care of using the correct value.\n\nA better way to test this logic would be to have one test that verifies the simple fields, and the rest would focus on the complex title logic\n\n@Test\nfun simplePropertiesAreCorrectlyCreated() {\n val result = createPerson(\"Bob\", 5)\n\n assertEquals(\"Bob\", result.name)\n assertEquals(5, result.age)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun bobAge5HasCorrectTitle() {\n val result = createPerson(\"Bob\", 5)\n\n assertEquals(Title.Mr, result.title)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun bethanyAge30HasCorrectTitle() {\n val result = createPerson(\"Bob\", 5)\n\n assertEquals(Title.Mrs, result.title)\n}\n\n...\n\nThis way, the complex logic has granular tests which should be less brittle and not tied up with other irrelevant logic. However, this is a double-edged sword, because while the tests are more resilient, they won't detect if a new field is added. This is up to the developer to remember about it, and add tests for it, either by adding another assertion to the simple Properties test, or by adding a couple tests is the field is more complex.\n\n\n9. Not using Soft assertions\n\nSometimes in tests we want to make assertions on a couple of fields, either because they are part of the same behavior, or they are so simple, that we don't want to bother with adding separate tests for all of them. Or sometimes the field is more complex, and we want to verify its type, nullability etc.\n\nUsually assertions throw an error, meaning that if we have multiple assertions, when something fails the rest of the assertions won't execute:\n\ndata class Pet(\n val name: String,\n val age: Int, \n)\n\n@Test\nfun multipleFieldsAreCorrect() {\n ...\n assertEquals(\"Kitty\", result.name)\n assertEquals(3, result.age)\n}\n\nIn the above example, if the name is incorrect, the age won't even be verified, so we're not sure if the age was correct or not. To help with this issue, we can use soft assertions:\n\n@Test\nfun multipleFieldsAreCorrect() {\n ...\n assertSoftly {\n result.name shouldBe \"Kitty\"\n result.age shouldBe 3\n }\n}\n\nWith soft assertions, everything will be checked first, and then the test will fail. In the above example, if the name is invalid, the age is still verified, and we get a full report of what's incorrect:\n\nio.kotest.assertions.MultiAssertionError: \nThe following 2 assertions failed:\n1) expected:<\"Kitty\"> but was:<\"Cat\">\n\tat com.akjaw.example.Test.multipleFieldsAreCorrect(Test.kt:125)\n2) expected:<3> but was:<4>\n\tat com.akjaw.example.Test.multipleFieldsAreCorrect(Test.kt:126)\n\n\nMost assertions frameworks have soft assertions which you can use in your tests, the above example uses Kotest assertions.\n\nAnother example where I like to use soft assertions is for the type of the class (a Kotlin sealed class in this example)\n\nsealed class Pet {\n data class Cat(val name: String, val age: Int) : Pet()\n data class Dog(val name: String) : Pet()\n}\n\n@Test\nfun catIsCorrect() {\n ...\n assertSoftly {\n result.shouldBeInstanceOf()\n result.name shouldBe \"Kitty\"\n result.age shouldBe 3\n }\n}\n\nshouldBeInstanceOf asserts the type along with smart casting it, meaning that the result variable will behave like it was the Pet.Cat type allowing access to all the Pet.Cat specific fields.\n\n\n10. Repeating the SUT Algorithm in assertions instead of an explicit value\n\nSometimes when writing tests, we want to go the easy way, and make them as simple to write as possible. This laziness can lead to tests which have assertions that mirror the implementation details / algorithm of the class we want to test.\n\nclass Stringifier {\n fun execute(list: List): String =\n list.joinToString(\"|\")\n}\n\nclass StringifierTest {\n\n @Test\n fun stringifyReturnsTheValuesSeparatedByPipe() {\n val input = listOf(1, 2, 3, 4, 5)\n val systemUnderTest = Stringifier()\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.execute(input)\n\n result shouldBe input.joinToString(\"|\")\n }\n}\n\nIn the above example, the assertions are the same as the SUT algorithm, and it makes the test pass. However, for more complex cases these assertions are hard to understand and sometimes require a debugging session to just understand what is the expected outcome.\n\nThis confusion makes the tests harder to understand, and if we were to refactor the algorithm to use a different function. The result would be a hard-to-understand test which doesn't even match what the system under test is doing. A better way of doing this would be:\n\n@Test\nfun stringifyReturnsTheValuesSeparatedByPipe() {\n val input = listOf(1, 2, 3, 4, 5)\n val systemUnderTest = Stringifier()\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.execute(input)\n\n result shouldBe \"1|2|3|4|5\"\n}\n\n\nThis explicit assertion value makes it easy to understand what the SUT does. Additionally, we don't have to wonder about some aspects of how the SUT works, e.g: will there be pipes at the beginning or end?", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", "slug": "testing", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#871807", "url": "/tag/testing/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/testing-mistakes-part-2/", "excerpt": "The second part of my Testing Mistakes series"}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform GitHub Actions CI Verification using Labels", "custom_excerpt": "Having automatic verifications is an important part of Software Development, as the project and team grows it becomes crucial that no regressions are introduced into the \"main\" branch by mistake.", "published_at": "2023-09-02T11:24:59.000+00:00", "plaintext": "In this article, I'll show how to set up a Continuous Integration for a Kotlin Multiplatform repository. This CI will verify that the code changes don't break the main branch. The tool used for this is GitHub Actions which I use for every personal project because it is integrated with GitHub, and it is free for open source projects.\n\nThe CI verification consists of building the project and running tests both on PRs and on the main branch. However, depending on what label is selected for the PR only selected targets will be run, which saves time and costs (for paid plans).\n\nThe repository (which includes an Android, Desktop and iOS target) used in this article is available here:\n\nGitHub - AKJAW/kotlin-multiplatform-github-actionsContribute to AKJAW/kotlin-multiplatform-github-actions development by creating an account on GitHub.GitHubAKJAW\n\nKeep in mind that this article shows a version of the repository which contains boilerplate. How to remove this boilerplate is the topic of my other article.\n\n\nWhy is such verification needed?\n\nSuch a Continuous Integration will give us confidence, that changes made in the project don't break anything. It offloads the verification to an automated process, which doesn't forget and runs with little manual input.\n\nIf you'd like to fully automate this verification, checkout out my follow-up article on setting up automated labels.\n\nHaving automatic verifications is an important part of Software Development, as the project and team grows it becomes crucial that no regressions are introduced into the \"main\" branch by mistake.\n\n\nSteps\n\nBefore delving into the details, I just want to show the steps which will be used in the workflow. Every job contains the same set-up steps, so they will be omitted for brevity in the examples, the full code is available in the repository.\n\nsteps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n\n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n\nBuild\n\nThe Android and Desktop jobs are pretty much the same, with only the module being different. Both can be run on Ubuntu machines (which are the cheapest):\n\nAndroid:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false) }}\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n # ...\n - run: ./gradlew :androidApp:assemble\n\n\nDesktop:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false) }}\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n # ...\n - run: ./gradlew :desktopApp:assemble\n\niOS requires a macOS machine and is more involved as besides Gradle it also needs to set up CocoaPods and pods installation before running the build:\n\niOS:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false) }}\n runs-on: macos-12\n\n steps:\n # ...\n - run: ./gradlew :shared:generateDummyFramework\n\n - name: Set up cocoapods\n uses: maxim-lobanov/setup-cocoapods@v1\n with:\n version: latest\n\n - name: Install Dependencies\n run: |\n cd iosApp\n pod install --verbose\n\n - run: xcodebuild build -workspace iosApp/iosApp.xcworkspace -configuration Debug -scheme iosApp -sdk iphoneos -destination name='iPhone 14' -verbose\n\n\n\nTest\n\nThese jobs are pretty much the same, the only difference being the last Gradle / Xcode command\n\n./gradlew clean testDebugUnitTest -p shared/\n\n./gradlew clean desktopTest -p shared\n\nBecause now Macs with M1/M2 have a different architecture than Intel, they require a different command. The if statement launches the correct command depending on the system architecture (As of now every GitHub runner has Intel, but this future proofs the CI and allows for self-hosted runners using M1).\n\nif [[ $(uname -m) == 'arm64' ]]; then ./gradlew clean iosSimulatorArm64Test -p shared/; else ./gradlew clean iosX64Test -p shared/; fi\n\n./gradlew clean testDebug -p androidApp/\n\n./gradlew clean jvmTest -p desktopApp/\n\n💡The Gradle -p parameter is needed if the project is modularized. It basically runs the command for all sub-modules in the given module/directory.\n\nxcodebuild build test -workspace iosApp/iosApp.xcworkspace -configuration Debug -scheme iosApp -sdk iphoneos -destination name='iPhone 14' -verbose\n\nWith native iOS tests I've run into a problem caused by Compose Multiplatform which I was not able to solve, so for now they are just skipped.\n\nUncaught Kotlin exception: kotlin.IllegalStateException: Metal is not supported on this system\n\nMaybe someone will be able to show me a different way of running tests which works on GHA 😄 (Comment below if you have any ideas).\n\nIf you'd like to learn more about Kotlin Multiplatform Testing, you can check out my other articles which focus on this topic.\n\n\nCode Review Workflow\n\nBoth the Build and UnitTests workflows are called from the main Code Review workflow:\n\nname: Code review\n\non:\n workflow_dispatch:\n push:\n branches:\n - main\n pull_request:\n types: [opened, ready_for_review, synchronize]\n branches:\n - main\n\njobs:\n Build:\n uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml\n\n UnitTests:\n uses: ./.github/workflows/test.yml\n\nThis is the workflow which will be run on opened PRs and on the main branch after merging.\n\n\nRunning the verification only when it makes sense\n\nYou may have noticed, that all the build jobs share the same if statement (along with UnitTests):\n\nif: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false) }}\n\nThis is because the jobs should only run on certain conditions\n\n * When a PR is opened\n * When a commit is pushed (but only on the main branch or on an opened PR)\n * When stared manually on GitHub\n\nThe jobs shouldn't run when the PR is a draft, because these PRs are still a Work In Progress, so it makes no sense to waste resources on verifying them.\n\n\nRunning only the required jobs\n\nThe current solution runs every target / job regardless of what was changed. This can be improved by introducing labels to the project\n\nThen these labels can be used on GHA to decide if something should be run or not:\n\nAndroid:\n if: ${{ contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android')}}\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n # ...\n - run: ./gradlew :androidApp:assemble \n\nThe Android build job will be only run when the PR contains either a KMP or an Android label.\n\nRunning only specified jobs speeds up the CI and also helps with reducing the costs of GitHub Actions on paid plans / private repositories. With Kotlin Multiplatform, the bulk of the costs comes from iOS, as the macOS machines cost 10 times more than Ubuntu!\n\nPlease note that the above if statement for labels needs to be combined with the if statement from the previous section, which results in this pretty thing:\n\n${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false && (contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android'))) }}\n\nThis can be improved, by creating a set-up job and moving the labels to variables, however this will be explained more in-depth in my next article as stated in the beginning.\n\n\nBlocking merging when the CI fails\n\nUnfortunately, GitHub treats skipped jobs as failed, meaning that with status checks, only the KMP label would work correctly, all other labels would result in a blocked merge option, because some jobs were skipped.\n\nThis can be fixed by adding another job which runs after all other jobs complete:\n\njobs:\n SetUp: # ...\n Build: # ...\n UnitTests: # ...\n AllowMerge:\n if: always()\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n needs: [ Build, UnitTests ]\n steps:\n - run: |\n if [ ${{ github.event_name }} == pull_request ] && [ ${{ join(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name) == '' }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n if [ ${{ (contains(needs.Build.result, 'failure')) }} == true ] || [ ${{ (contains(needs.UnitTests.result, 'failure')) }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n else\n exit 0\n fi\n\nAfter this change, the GitHub repository can block merging when the AllowMerge doesn't pass.\n\nThe first if statement ensures that any PR has at least one label, when it's missing a label, the run will fail. Thanks to this, the problem of human forgetfulness is out of the CI equation. When someone forgets to add the label, the PR won't be allowed to merge.\n\nThe second if statement verifies that there were no failures on any of the previous jobs. In case there was at least one failure, the PR will be blocked from merging. This should guarantee that no regressions can be introduced into the main branch (unless someone puts a wrong label on the PR).\n\nBlocking merging when the CI fails is a good way to decrease the number of issues on the main branch. It gives us confidence, that at any point in time the main branch is working and can be more or less released.\n\n\nStatic analysis tools\n\nUsing these types of tools as part of the Code Review workflow will keep the codebase more predictable and healthy. If you're interested in this topic, checkout my article on it:\n\nAdding Konsist and Ktlint to a GitHub Actions Continuous IntegrationAutomating code and architecture checks in the Continuous Integration pipeline can significantly reduce Code Review time and prevent human forgetfulness.AKJAWAleksander Jaworski\n\n\nExtras\n\n\nAutomated Labels\n\nDepending on human memory isn't always the best way of doing things. Adding labels can be automated using the Labeler actions, to learn more click here:\n\nKotlin Multiplatform GitHub Actions Automated Pull Request LabelsHaving automated labels makes the Code Review process much safer by preventing merges for broken Pull Requests, ensuring that the main branch is stableAKJAWAleksander Jaworski\n\n\nGradle Caching\n\nCaching can be achieved using the official gradle-build-action, which has a built-in caching system. The default behavior only saves the cache on the main branch, and other branches can only read from the cache.\n\nThis behavior can be customized, so the cache can be saved on multiple branches as specified in the documentation.\n\n\nConcurrency\n\nA lot of time was spent optimizing the CI, to not run when not needed, e.g. when only changing Android, the iOS jobs shouldn't run. However, there is one more optimization which cancels any unneeded jobs.\n\nBy unneeded I mean a Code Review run which is not valid anymore, for example when a new commit was pushed on an opened PR. In that case, the currently running CI on the old commit should be stopped.\n\nFortunately, this functionality is supported in GitHub Actions:\n\nname: Code review\n\nconcurrency:\n group: ${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}\n cancel-in-progress: ${{ github.ref != 'refs/heads/main' }}\n\nThese 3 lines, will automatically cancel any jobs which will be replaced by new ones, potentially saving a lot of unnecessary \"billing minutes\".\n\n\nCourse\n\nI'm a co-author of an Android course which goes more in-depth into CI/CD for Android like, the above Code Review, Manual builds for testers, Release builds, Nightly verification and more. Currently, the course is only available in Polish, but there is a waitlist for those interested in an English version of it, so feel free to check it out if you're interested!\n\nAndroid Next LevelA
