changes for this article are in this Pull Request.\n\n\n\nReminder\n\n\nThe Code Review workflow runs the jobs based on the Pull Request labels, so the Android label only runs Android related Gradle tasks and the KMP label runs everything.\n\n\njobs:\n SetUp:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n steps:\n - id: setVariables\n name: Set variables\n run: ...\n outputs:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n shouldRunIos: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunIos }}\n shouldRunDesktop: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunDesktop }}\n\n Build:\n needs: SetUp\n uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml\n with:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n shouldRunIos: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunIos }}\n shouldRunDesktop: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunDesktop }}\n\n UnitTests:\n needs: SetUp\n uses: ./.github/workflows/test.yml\n with:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n shouldRunIos: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunIos }}\n shouldRunDesktop: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunDesktop }}\n\n AllowMerge:\n ...\n\n\nThanks to this, the CI only builds things which are needed and allows for skipping unrelated jobs. There's no point in running the iOS build if there are only Android changes.\n\n\nAllowMerge, will pass if the dependent jobs passed or were skipped, in case of failure it will fail, thus blocking merging. Additionally, when the PR is opened without any label, then it will also fail.\n\n\nAllowMerge:\n if: always()\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n needs: [ Build, UnitTests ]\n steps:\n - run: |\n if [ ${{ github.event_name }} == pull_request ] && [ ${{ join(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name) == '' }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n elif [ ${{ (contains(needs.Build.result, 'failure')) }} == true ] || [ ${{ (contains(needs.UnitTests.result, 'failure')) }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n\n\nThe above logic will prevent a broken main branch, however, the Pull Request author needs to be diligent and apply the correct labels, which might be problematic if the changes start including other platforms and the author forgets to update them.\n\n\nThe workflow above only executes builds and tests based on what labels were applied. Thanks to this, the CI uses less resources when checking Pull Requests.\n\n\n\nAutomatic Labeling\n\n\nThe action for labeling is Labeler v5, which is configured as follows:\n\n\nKMP:\n- changed-files:\n - any-glob-to-any-file:\n - shared/**\n - konsist/**\n\nAndroid:\n- changed-files:\n - any-glob-to-any-file:\n - androidApp/**\n\niOS:\n- changed-files:\n - any-glob-to-any-file:\n - iosApp/**\n\nDesktop:\n- changed-files:\n - any-glob-to-any-file:\n - desktopApp/**\n\n\nThe repository for this project is a fork, and because of this Labeler requires additional permissions, to work correctly:\n\n\njobs:\n SetUp:\n permissions:\n contents: read\n pull-requests: write\n ...\n\n\n\nThe set-up outputs, which control which platform is run, are now based on the labeler output:\n\n\nSetUp:\n ...\n steps:\n - id: labeler\n if: ${{ !contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Ignore') }}\n uses: actions/labeler@v5\n - id: setVariables\n name: Set variables\n run: |\n ...\n hasKmpLabel=${{ contains(steps.labeler.outputs.all-labels, 'KMP') }}\n ...\n outputs:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n shouldRunIos: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunIos }}\n shouldRunDesktop: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunDesktop }}\n all-labels: ${{ steps.labeler.outputs.all-labels }}\n\n\nAdditionally, if the PR already has a label called 'Ignore' then the labeler does not apply any labels, allowing the PR to be merged without any checks which might be useful for Tooling, Documentation, Codeowner etc. changes. (More on this in the next section)\n\n\nThe AllowMerge job has one additional dependency to SetUp to access the labeler output:\n\n\nAllowMerge:\n if: always()\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n needs: [ SetUp, Build, UnitTests ]\n steps:\n - run: |\n if [ ${{ github.event_name }} == pull_request ] && [ ${{ join(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name) == '' }} == true ] && [ ${{ join(needs.SetUp.outputs.all-labels) == '' }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n elif [ ${{ (contains(needs.Build.result, 'failure')) }} == true ] || [ ${{ (contains(needs.UnitTests.result, 'failure')) }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n else\n exit 0\n fi\n\n\n\nThis output is used in the empty label condition to ensure that labels which were added automatically are also checked, preventing false positives.\n\n\nThanks to this automation, the Pull Request author doesn't need to worry about applying the correct labels. All that needs to be done is to create the Pull Request either a draft or open.\n\n\n\nHow it works\n\n\nWhen the PR is created, then the labeler adds the corresponding labels, which then dictate what jobs are run:\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\nIgnoring the labeling\n\n\nThere are some cases where there are no code changes, like tooling or documentation updates. In such cases, opening the PR with no matching directory changes will fail, because there are no labels.\n\n\nHowever, the workflow logic has a special case for the Ignore label, which prevents labeler from being run (and accidentally adding labels when it shouldn't):\n\n\nSetUp:\n ...\n steps:\n - id: labeler\n if: ${{ !contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Ignore') }}\n uses: actions/labeler@v5\n ...\n\n\nAllowMerge will pass because there is the Ignore label, allowing the PR to merge.\n\n\n\n\n\nSummary\n\n\nHaving automated labels makes the Code Review process much safer by preventing merges for broken Pull Requests, ensuring that the main branch is stable (The only exception being adding the Ignore label by accident).\n\n\nAdditionally, the PR authors don't need to remember which labels to add or even add them at all, labeler takes care of this when creating a Pull Request.\n\n\n\n", "tags": [], "url": "/kotlin-multiplatform-gha-automated-labels/", "excerpt": "Having automated labels makes the Code Review process much safer by preventing merges for broken Pull Requests, ensuring that the main branch is stable"}, {"title": "How I use Mutation Testing to Drive Good Test Case Coverage", "custom_excerpt": "Automated testing is important for efficient software development, catching bugs within minutes and saving valuable developer time. Mutation Testing validates test suites by altering production code logic. When tests pass despite code mutations, it signals missing test cases. ", "published_at": "2023-12-05T15:05:18.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Testing is an important part of software development, a good automated test suite can catch bugs in a matter of minutes. Automated tests are usually run as part of a Continuous Integration, which runs on a separate machine without much involvement of the developer.\n\n\nComparing that with manual testing, which usually: builds the whole app, requires clicking through it, sometimes even changing network responses to test edge cases (e.g. errors, missing data). All those manual steps performed through the day add up pretty quickly (not to mention the whole lifetime of the project) costing developers a lot of time which could be spent elsewhere.\n\n\nHowever, to have a good automated test suite, it needs to cover all edge cases for the important parts of the code. Important code could be just complex logic which everyone is scared to touch in fear of breaking it. It might also be code which is often changed, and with a little bit of oversight might introduce regressions (i.e. bugs).\n\n\nUnfortunately, having a good test suite isn't easy, every developer must be diligent and add tests for every logic condition. When the Author forgets to do so, then a Code Reviewer should catch this and raise the missing test cases.\n\n\nThe good news is, that Mutation Testing is an approach which allows for quickly catching missing test cases.\n\n\nAutomated testing is important for efficient software development, catching bugs within minutes and saving valuable developer time. To ensure a robust test suite, diligence is required from every developer.\n\n\n\nWhat's mutation testing\n\n\nIn simple terms, Mutation Testing involves changing the production code and then running the tests. If there's a failure after this change, then usually this condition is covered by tests*. However, if all tests pass, then most likely there is a missing test case for this condition.\n\n\n* Sometimes even if some test fails after the \"mutation\" it's good to double-check if the test fails for the correct reasons. For example, the test focuses on some other part of the production code, but fails as a side effect of this mutation.\n\n\n\nBoundary values example\n\n\nTake a look at this example production code:\n\n\nenum class Priority {\n LOW,\n HIGH,\n}\n\nfun convertToPriority(value: Int): Priority? {\n if (value !in 0..100) return null\n\n return if (value <= 100 && value > 50) {\n Priority.HIGH\n } else {\n Priority.LOW\n }\n}\n\n\nThe logic could be simplified, but I wanted to keep it this way to simulate logic with a lot of conditions which might be tricky to test. There are some bugs hidden in the above code, which the current test suite does not detect:\n\n\n@Test\nfun `When value is -1 then null is returned`() {\n convertToPriority(-1) shouldBe null\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `When value is 101 then null is returned`() {\n convertToPriority(101) shouldBe null\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `When value is 51 then High priority is returned`() {\n convertToPriority(51) shouldBe Priority.HIGH\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `When value is 50 then Low priority is returned`() {\n convertToPriority(50) shouldBe Priority.LOW\n}\n\n\nLooking at the test cases above, it might seem that everything is covered, however when it comes to boundary values it's really important to test all of them. In the function above, the boundary values are 100, 50 and 0.\n\n\nTo catch the missing test cases, it's as easy as changing the upper boundary value to something smaller, like 99:\n\n\n\nThe production code treats 100 as a High priority, so that's probably the requirement. However, after this change, all tests still pass, meaning that there's a missing test case for checking it:\n\n\n@Test\nfun `When value is 100 then High priority is returned`() {\n convertToPriority(100) shouldBe Priority.HIGH\n}\n\n\nAnother missing test case is for the lower boundary in the first condition:\n\n\n\nAll tests still pass, so the following test needs to be added:\n\n\n@Test\nfun `When value is 0 then Low priority is returned`() {\n convertToPriority(0) shouldBe Priority.LOW\n}\n\n\n\nMutation testing Cheat Sheet\n\n\nEvery condition like ifs, filters or anything which uses a boolean can be easily mutated by:\n\n\n * Commenting it out altogether (removing a condition branch)\n * Changing boundary values in comparisons (like in the example above)\n * Negating the condition (e.g. using !, .not(), or switching from any to all)\n\n\nOther code mutations could be returning a hard-coded value intentionally (e.g. an empty list, error or null)\n\n\nMutation Testing validates test suites by altering production code logic. When tests pass despite code mutations, it signals missing test cases. Special attention should be paid to boundary values and conditions.\n\n\n\nWhy Code Coverage doesn't tell the whole truth\n\n\nCode coverage is a misleading metric, look at this example:\n\n\n\nLooking at the above result it seems that everything is covered, however as previously pointed out, the above code has issues with the boundary values. So even though every line is covered, the test suite is not complete and is missing some cases.\n\n\nCoverage could be used for just checking untested lines, however, for complex conditions it can be misleading and give false confidence that every case is covered.\n\n\nCode Coverage cannot be used as a metric for how good a test suite actually is. It gives misleading confidence, which makes it easier to introduce bugs into productions.\n\n\n\nWriting Code\n\n\nWhen I'm writing code, most of the time I'm using Test Driven Development, which in practice should mean that there is no production code that doesn't have a corresponding test.\n\n\nThere are cases where I don't use TDD, or that the logic is so complex that I wasn't able to figure out all test cases for it. When that happens, Mutation Testing comes in handy, because it quickly allows me to find missing cases.\n\n\n\nReviewing Code\n\n\nWhen reviewing code which has tests, I usually open both the production code and the tests side by side in a split view. I first read through the test cases and try to understand the requirements of the class.\n\n\nAfter I'm done checking the existing code, I try to find any missing cases through mutation testing. Thanks to this, I don't need to spend a lot of time scanning through all test cases and checking the corresponding production code.\n\n\nBesides finding missing test cases, mutation testing can also find tests which are passing for the wrong reason. For example, the production code uses condition A and B. The test verifies that when condition A is met, it passes. However, through mutation testing, condition A is removed but the test still passes. This means that the test passes for the wrong reason and needs to be changed.\n\n\n\nSummary\n\n\nTo ensure that something is properly tested you first have to fully understand the code, mutation testing is kind of shortcut for this. The rule of thumb is that, if the changed code doesn't cause the test to fail, then some test case is missing.\n\n\nThis technique is helpful for driving good test coverage, it doesn't need to be used every time. There are less important classes which don't need to have perfect test coverage.\n\n\nThe main focus for using mutation testing should be on code which:\n\n\n * Is changed a lot (because changes can introduce regressions, and automated tests help with catching them)\n * Is critical for the functionality of the software (having tests for stuff which is only used for development might be overkill)\n * Has really complex logic, making it hard to change without braking anything. In such situations, tests can act like a guiding lighthouse, if everything is green, then proceed.\n\n\nIf you're interested in learning more about testing in-general, checkout my article series on this topic:\n\n\n5 Beginner Testing Mistakes I Noticed While Working with Less Experienced DevelopersHaving a good test suite helps with catching regressions / bugs a lot faster, and gives developers more confidence when merging / releasing their product. In this series, I’ll share my recommendations for improving your tests.Aleksander JaworskiAKJAW\n21 Testing Mistakes - Part 2The second part of my Testing Mistakes seriesAleksander JaworskiAKJAW\n21 Testing Mistakes - Part 3The third part of my Testing Mistakes seriesAleksander JaworskiAKJAW\n21 Testing Mistakes - Part 4The fourth part of my Testing Mistakes seriesAleksander JaworskiAKJAW\n\n\n", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", "slug": "testing", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#871807", "url": "/tag/testing/"}], "url": "/using-mutation-testing-for-good-test-coverage/", "excerpt": "Automated testing is important for efficient software development, catching bugs within minutes and saving valuable developer time. Mutation Testing validates test suites by altering production code logic. When tests pass despite code mutations, it signals missing test cases. "}, {"title": "Adding Konsist and Ktlint to a GitHub Actions Continuous Integration", "custom_excerpt": "Automating code and architecture checks in the Continuous Integration pipeline can significantly reduce Code Review time and prevent human forgetfulness.", "published_at": "2023-11-01T07:29:45.000+00:00", "plaintext": "This article will focus on adding linter and code checks to a GitHub Actions Code Review workflow. The examples here are based on my Kotlin Multiplatform GitHub Actions repository, but all workflows and jobs are applicable to any Kotlin / Android project.\n\n\nGitHub - AKJAW/kotlin-multiplatform-github-actions: Repository showcasing GitHub Actions for Kotlin MultiplatformRepository showcasing GitHub Actions for Kotlin Multiplatform - GitHub - AKJAW/kotlin-multiplatform-github-actions: Repository showcasing GitHub Actions for Kotlin MultiplatformAKJAWGitHub\n\n\nHowever, before diving into the implementation, I'd like to provide some arguments why it's a good idea to do this.\n\n\n\nWhy are such automations needed?\n\n\nEnforcing formatting and guidelines usually happens at the Code Review stage. You open a Pull Request, and a teammate informs you that a something is formatted incorrectly, or that the codebase uses a different convention for naming or packaging.\n\n\nVerifying this manually takes more time, as the person doing the Code Review needs to pay extra attention to these things. While additionally trying to understand the changes in the PR. This leads to increased Code Review time and then additional time for fixing all the issues pointed out.\n\n\nBesides the increased feedback loop, there is still a reliance on the human memory, the Reviewer might just forget about the conventions or be in a hurry, thus allowing the introduction of these \"inconsistencies\" into the codebase.\n\n\nAutomating such steps in the Continuous Integration pipeline should result in a healthier codebase, where every Pull Request follows the project guidelines. This eliminates the human forgetfulness aspect and also cuts down the feedback loop, because as soon as the PR is opened the verification is run and the author knows that some things need to be fixed.\n\n\nAutomating code and architecture checks in the Continuous Integration pipeline can significantly reduce Code Review time and prevent human forgetfulness, ensuring a healthier codebase that follows the project guidelines.\n\n\n\nKonsist\n\n\nI've recently started playing around Konsist, and I was blown away by its ease of use and power. You basically write tests which asserts something on your Kotlin files / classes / properties. The added benefit is that the tests are debuggable, meaning that it's easy to create the test inside the debugger evaluation window.\n\n\nI won't go into the details of how to set up Konsist, because the official documentation is a much better resource for that. I can just say that I put Konsist into a separate module to decrease the build / configuration time.\n\n\nAs for calling Konsist inside GitHub Actions, it's as simple as running all tests inside the Konsist module:\n\n\nname: Konsist\n\non:\n workflow_call:\n\njobs:\n Konsist:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n \n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n - run: ./gradlew :konsist:testDebugUnitTest\n\n\n\nKtlint\n\n\nWhile Konsist focuses on more Architectural / Structural guidelines, Ktlint focuses on code style conventions / standards. Both of them can work together to create a more predictable and healthy codebase.\n\n\nIn the example repository, Ktlint is added through a Gradle plugin, meaning that it is installed alongside the project and can be run through a Gradle Task:\n\n\nname: Ktlint\n\non:\n workflow_call:\n\njobs:\n Ktlint:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n \n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n - run: ./gradlew ktlintCheck\n\n\nIf your project doesn't use a Gradle plugin, but the Ktlint CLI, then it can be used on the GitHub machine like this:\n\n\n...\njobs:\n Ktlint:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n ...\n\n - name: Ktint set up\n run: curl -sSLO https://github.com/pinterest/ktlint/releases/download/0.50.0/ktlint && chmod a+x ktlint && sudo mv ktlint /usr/local/bin/\n\n - run: ktlint '!**/build/**'\n\n\n\n\n\n💡\n\nTo save time on linting errors, a pre-commit hook could be installed which auto formats the code before committing.\n\n\n\nRunning Konsist and Ktlint before other verifications\n\n\nAs you might've noticed, both the above workflows are declared as workflow_call, allowing them to be called from other workflows. If the other verifications like UnitTests or Builds ale also workflow_calls, then they can be set up in the following way:\n\n\nname: Code review\n\nconcurrency:\n group: ${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}\n cancel-in-progress: ${{ github.ref != 'refs/heads/main' }}\n\non:\n workflow_dispatch:\n push:\n branches:\n - main\n pull_request:\n types: [opened, ready_for_review, synchronize]\n branches:\n - main\n\njobs:\n Konsist:\n uses: ./.github/workflows/konsist.yml\n\n Ktlint:\n uses: ./.github/workflows/ktlint.yml\n\n Build:\n needs: [ Konsist, Ktlint ]\n uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml\n\n UnitTests:\n needs: [ Konsist, Ktlint ]\n uses: ./.github/workflows/test.yml\n\n\n\nUsing needs inside the Build and UnitTests jobs forces both of them to wait for Konsist and Ktlint to complete successfully before starting. So for example if Konsist passes, but Ktlint fails then Build and UnitTests are not started, thus saving precious billing minutes and gives faster feedback to the PR author.\n\n\nAs pointed out by Stylianos Gakis on Twitter, when billing minutes are not a problem it might be better to always run all verifications, even when Ktlint or Konsist fails. This gives the fastest feedback loop without having to re-run jobs.\n\n\n\n\n\nSummary\n\n\nHaving a good automation for these types of checks:\n\n\n * Enforces good practices across the whole codebase by blocking incorrect Pull Requests\n * Makes reviewing code easier and faster, because less attention needs to be given to the naming / packaging / styling etc\n * Speeds up Pull Request merges, because the CI gives fast feedback about what should be fixed\n * They are run automatically, so there's no need to manually check them before opening a PR\n\n\nIf you'd like to learn more about good GitHub Actions practices for actions and workflows, feel free to check out my previous article:\n\n\nGitHub Actions Reducing Duplication / BoilerplateYAML files, which are not that easy to maintain, changes are usually copied and pasted across multiple files. This article shows that there’s a better way.Aleksander JaworskiAKJAW\n\n\n\nKotlin Multiplatform Bonus\n\n\nBecause Kotlin Multiplatform PRs can be iOS only, it makes no sense to run Ktlint or Konsist on them. So if there are no Kotlin changes, then both of the jobs should just be skipped. However, GitHub Actions treats skipped jobs as failed thus blocks merging, this means that the approach needs to be different.\n\n\nThe changes which add the Kotlin Multiplatform Label logic (From my previous article) can be found inside this Pull Request. The basic idea is that the labels are passed in from the Code Review workflow (I'll omit Ktlint because it's the same).\n\n\n...\n\njobs:\n ...\n\n Konsist:\n needs: SetUp\n uses: ./.github/workflows/konsist.yml\n with:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n shouldRunDesktop: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunDesktop }}\n\n ...\n\n\nThen used like this\n\n\nname: Konsist\n\non:\n workflow_call:\n inputs:\n shouldRunKmp:\n required: true\n type: string\n shouldRunAndroid:\n required: true\n type: string\n shouldRunDesktop:\n required: true\n type: string\n\njobs:\n Konsist:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n ...\n\n - run: |\n if ${{ inputs.shouldRunKmp == 'true' || inputs.shouldRunAndroid == 'true' || inputs.shouldRunDesktop == 'true' }}; then\n ./gradlew :konsist:testDebugUnitTest\n else \n exit 0\n fi\n\n\n\nAnother approach could be using an if for the run step:\n\n\n steps:\n ...\n\n - if: ${{ inputs.shouldRunKmp == 'true' || inputs.shouldRunAndroid == 'true' || inputs.shouldRunDesktop == 'true' }}\n run: ./gradlew :konsist:testDebugUnitTest\n\n\nSo in cases when it doesn't make sense, Konsist and Ktlint are not executed:\n\n\n\nHowever, for iOS there is SwiftLint which could be used when the iOS label is applied to the PR, thus ensuring iOS style guidelines. Adding a SwiftLint job to the above workflow would be pretty much the same as for the Konsist job, just the command, parameters and condition would be different.\n\n\nIf you want to learn more about using GitHub Actions for a Kotlin Multiplatform repository, you can read my other article on this topic:\n\n\nKotlin Multiplatform GitHub Actions CI Verification using LabelsHaving automatic verifications is an important part of Software Development, as the project and team grows it becomes crucial that no regressions are introduced into the “main” branch by mistake.Aleksander code and architecture checks in the Continuous Integration pipeline can significantly reduce Code Review time and prevent human forgetfulness."}, {"title": "GitHub Actions Reducing Duplication / Boilerplate", "custom_excerpt": "YAML files, which are not that easy to maintain, changes are usually copied and pasted across multiple files. This article shows that there's a better way.", "published_at": "2023-09-09T07:34:34.000+00:00", "plaintext": "In my previous article, I've shown how to set up a CI for a Kotlin Multiplatform repository. In this post, I'll focus on how to remove GitHub Actions boilerplate.\n\n\n💡\n\nIf you don't use Kotlin Multiplatform, or even Kotlin, don't worry. The main focus of this article is platform-agnostic (YAML and bash). It touches on topics like extracting logic, composite actions, adding job variables.\n\n\nThe CI verification consists of building the project and running tests on: the main branch and PRs. However, for PRs depending on what label the PR has only selected targets will be run, which saves time and costs (for paid plans).\n\n\nThe repository (which is a Kotlin Multiplatform project for Android, Desktop and iOS) used in this article is available in this repository:\n\n\nGitHub - AKJAW/kotlin-multiplatform-github-actions: Repository showcasing GitHub Actions for Kotlin MultiplatformRepository showcasing GitHub Actions for Kotlin Multiplatform - GitHub - AKJAW/kotlin-multiplatform-github-actions: Repository showcasing GitHub Actions for Kotlin MultiplatformAKJAWGitHub\n\n\n\nThe Workflows\n\n\nWithout going into the implementation details of what is called where, here's a broad overview of the workflow structure:\n\n\nname: Code review\n\non:\n workflow_dispatch:\n push:\n branches:\n - main\n pull_request:\n types: [opened, ready_for_review, synchronize]\n branches:\n - main\n\njobs:\n Build:\n uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml\n\n UnitTests:\n uses: ./.github/workflows/test.yml\n\n AllowMerge:\n if: always()\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n needs: [ Build, UnitTests ]\n steps:\n - run: |\n if [ ${{ github.event_name }} == pull_request ] && [ ${{ join(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name) == '' }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n elif [ ${{ (contains(needs.Build.result, 'failure')) }} == true ] || [ ${{ (contains(needs.UnitTests.result, 'failure')) }} == true ]; then\n exit 1\n else\n exit 0\n fi\n\n\nThis is the main workflow which will be called by GitHub directly. It calls two other workflows that run the builds and tests. The last job is used for the GitHub PR status check, which fails if there is no PR label or when any of the Build / Tests jobs fail (which is needed because GitHub treats skipped jobs as failed).\n\n\nThis workflow doesn't really contain any repetition / boilerplate which can be improved. The problems lie in the two \"sub-workflows\" (Don't worry, you don't need to read the whole things):\n\n\nname: Build\n\non:\n workflow_call:\n\njobs:\n Android:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false && (contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android'))) }}\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n\n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n - run: ./gradlew :androidApp:assemble\n\n Desktop:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false && (contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Desktop'))) }}\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n\n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n - run: ./gradlew :desktopApp:assemble\n\n iOS:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false && (contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'iOS'))) }}\n runs-on: macos-12\n\n steps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n\n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n - run: ./gradlew :shared:generateDummyFramework\n\n - name: Set up cocoapods\n uses: maxim-lobanov/setup-cocoapods@v1\n with:\n version: latest\n\n - name: Install Dependencies\n run: |\n cd iosApp\n pod install --verbose\n\n - run: xcodebuild build -workspace iosApp/iosApp.xcworkspace -configuration Debug -scheme iosApp -sdk iphoneos -destination name='iPhone 14' -verbose\n\n\nThe basic idea is as follows for both the Builds and UnitTests workflows. The jobs should only run when\n\n\n * Started manually through workflow_dispatch\n * From a push on the main branch\n * When a PR is opened or pushed to (It's important that the PR is not a draft as to not waste billing minutes)\n\n\nAdditionally, PR labels control for which platforms the jobs will run:\n\n\n\nFor example, if the PR changes are only for the Android codebase, then the PR should have the Android label. The KMP label should be used when the shared codebase changes and because it is used by all platforms, all jobs should be run.\n\n\n\nBoilerplate\n\n\nIn this article I'll only focus on the Build workflow, however the same steps should be applied to the UnitTests workflow, as both of them have the following boilerplate:\n\n\n * All of the above jobs have the same two set-up steps\n * Java\n * Gradle\n * Repeated if statements\n * For the GitHub event (workflow_dispatch, push, pull_request)\n * For the PR label (github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name)\n\n\n\nExtracting steps to a separate file\n\n\nSteps can be moved to a composite action, which can be then re-used multiple times across all jobs.\n\n\nThe Java and Gradle composite action could look like this:\n\n\nname: Job set up\ndescription: Sets up Java and Gradle\nruns:\n using: \"composite\"\n steps:\n - uses: actions/setup-java@v3\n with:\n distribution: \"adopt\"\n java-version: \"11\"\n\n - name: Setup Gradle\n uses: gradle/gradle-build-action@v2\n\n\nThe name of the composite action becomes the name of the parent folder, which in this case is job-set-up.\n\n\nAnd then it can be re-used in all the jobs like this:\n\n\nAndroid:\n if: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' || github.event_name == 'push' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && github.event.pull_request.draft == false && (contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android'))) }}\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n\n steps:\n - uses: actions/checkout@v3\n\n - name: Job set up\n uses: ./.github/actions/job-set-up\n\n - run: ./gradlew :androidApp:assemble\n\n\nThanks to this extraction, whenever the set-up changes, only one file will need to be changed instead of going through all the jobs in all the workflows.\n\n\n\nExtracting the GitHub event condition\n\n\nAs shown previously, the if statement is pretty complex, which leads to it being hard to understand. To make the matter worse, it is scattered through all the jobs, making it hard to change.\n\n\nAs we all know, developers are lazy creatures, instead of changing all of them separately, they'll for sure copy and paste the if statement, which will sooner or later lead to bugs and typos.\n\n\nUnfortunately, if statements for jobs cannot be extracted to composite actions, so a different approach is needed.\n\n\nWhat can be done is to move the if statement to an additional job that runs before Build and UnitTests workflows:\n\n\n# ...\n\njobs:\n SetUp:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n steps:\n - id: setVariables\n name: Set variables\n run: |\n if [ ${{ github.event_name }} == workflow_dispatch ] || [ ${{ github.event_name }} == push ] || ([ ${{ github.event_name }} == pull_request ] && [ ${{ github.event.pull_request.draft }} == false ]); then\n exit 0\n else\n exit 1\n fi\n\n Build:\n needs: SetUp\n uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml\n\n UnitTests:\n needs: SetUp\n uses: ./.github/workflows/test.yml\n\n\n\nThe SetUp job checks if the workflow was started in the right conditions, if yes then it completes successfully, allowing the Build and UnitTests workflows to start. Otherwise, SetUp fails, causing Build and UnitTests to also fail, thus reducing the duplication inside the workflows while behaving in the same way.\n\n\nHere's what the Android build job looks after this change:\n\n\nAndroid:\n if: ${{ github.event_name != 'pull_request' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && (contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android'))) }}\n # ...\n\n\nThe if statement is better but still complex, the first condition (!= 'pull_request') is required because without it, manual or main branch runs would always result in skipped jobs, but for PRs, the labels are checked just like before.\n\n\n💡\n\nRemember to always double-check that condition changes didn't break other types of workflow runs.\n\n\n\nReducing Label Boilerplate\n\n\nThe below example only shows the Android build job, but please note that these changes affect all the jobs, but were just omitted for brevity.\n\n\nThe previous if statement was the same for all jobs, however label logic differs between jobs. This means that the if statement cannot be completely removed, however it can be improved using some bash logic and by passing variables between jobs:\n\n\n# ...\n\njobs:\n SetUp:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n steps:\n - id: setVariables\n name: Set variables\n run: |\n echo \"shouldRunKmp=${{ contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') }}\" >> \"$GITHUB_OUTPUT\"\n echo \"shouldRunAndroid=${{ contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android') }}\" >> \"$GITHUB_OUTPUT\"\n # ...\n outputs:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n \n Build:\n needs: SetUp\n uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml\n with:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ needs.SetUp.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n\n\nThe above set-up logic reads the PR labels and passes them to other jobs, which can be used like this:\n\n\nname: Build\n\non:\n workflow_call:\n inputs:\n shouldRunKmp:\n required: true\n type: string\n shouldRunAndroid:\n required: true\n type: string\n # ...\n\njobs:\n Android:\n if: ${{ github.event_name != 'pull_request' || (github.event_name == 'pull_request' && (inputs.shouldRunKmp == 'true' || inputs.shouldRunAndroid == 'true')) }}\n # ...\n\n\nThis improves the if statement, but it can still be improved further. The SetUp job already has an early return based on the event_name, but here it is duplicated. Instead of duplicating it, the logic can be included as part of the output variables logic:\n\n\nSetUp:\n runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n steps:\n - id: setVariables\n name: Set variables\n run: |\n isFromMain=${{ github.ref == 'refs/heads/main' }}\n isManual=${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' }}\n hasKmpLabel=${{ contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'KMP') }}\n shouldRunKmp=false\n if $isFromMain || $isManual || $hasKmpLabel ; then\n shouldRunKmp=true\n fi\n echo \"shouldRunKmp=$shouldRunKmp\" >> \"$GITHUB_OUTPUT\"\n echo \"shouldRunAndroid=${{ contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'Android') }}\" >> \"$GITHUB_OUTPUT\"\n # ...\n outputs:\n shouldRunKmp: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunKmp }}\n shouldRunAndroid: ${{ steps.setVariables.outputs.shouldRunAndroid }}\n\n\nIn case the PR run on the main branch or started manually, the shouldRunKmp is set to true, meaning that all jobs will be started, and for PRs, the labels decide what is run. This change, reduces the job if statements to just using the input variables:\n\n\njobs:\n Android:\n if: ${{ inputs.shouldRunKmp == 'true' || inputs.shouldRunAndroid == 'true' }}\n # ...\n\n\nWith this change, most of the logic resides in the SetUp job, which makes it easier to understand and also eliminates copy and paste errors. In the future, if the conditions change, probably only the SetUp job will need to change and all other jobs will remain unchanged.\n\n\n\nWorkflow Reusability\n\n\nAs you may have noticed, the Build and UnitTests workflows were specified as separate workflows using workflow_call. Thanks to this, they can be used in other workflows.\n\n\nFor example, if the project had a nightly verification, both of these workflows could be re-used to make sure that nothing broke the main branch. Please note, that with the above logic, the nightly would need to pass in the correct variables for the jobs to run (Or just the KMP one).\n\n\n\nSummary\n\n\n * Composite actions - help with repeated steps, like common set-up logic\n * Set-up jobs - can contain early returns for workflow starts in the wrong conditions\n * Output variables - can be used to reduce the number of conditions in jobs\n * Reusable workflows - can be re-used across multiple workflows, eliminating copying and pasting logic throughout different files\n\n\nAll the changes discussed in this article can be seen in this PR.\n\n\n\nCourse\n\n\nIf you found this helpful, you might be interested in the Android Next Level course I'm a co-author of. It goes more in-depth into CI/CD for Android, e.g. Code Review, Manual builds for testers, Release builds, Nightly verification and more. This article shows that there's a better way."}, {"title": "Building a Kotlin Multiplatform Mobile Proof of Concept Conclusions", "custom_excerpt": "This article contains information about our experiences with Kotlin Multiplatform, which hopefully might be useful for others.", "published_at": "2021-07-30T16:02:21.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Currently, we're planning a rebuild of our apps using the Kotlin Multiplatform, Jetpack Compose and Swift UI for Android and iOS respectively. This article contains information about our experiences with Kotlin Multiplatform, which hopefully might be useful for others.\n\nThe Touchlab KaMPKit project was used as the starter for the Proof of Concept.\n\n\nThe Proof Of Concept Architecture:\n\nThe platforms communicate with the KMP module using use cases. However, we are thinking that maybe exposing an interface that groups related features might be beneficial. The implementation would still call the use cases underneath, however such an interface would help with creating Kotlin / Native wrappers (explained later).\n\nThis means that the Presentation layer is also implemented on both platforms. We feel that this gives us more control of how we integrate the platforms with Kotlin Multiplatform.\n\n\nKotlin Multiplatform:\n\nKotlin / Native different threading model:\n\nThis is probably the biggest hurdle we've faced when working on the POC. Kotlin / Native is very restrictive when it comes to sharing classes between threads, whereas Kotlin / JVM is not.\n\nThe oversimplified version is that when referencing a class from a different thread the whole class becomes frozen along with its dependencies, meaning that they cannot be mutated / changed anymore.\n\nAt first, it seemed counterproductive and a burden, but after reading some resources around the topic, we've found that this restriction makes the code more predictable when it comes to multi-threading. Big shout out to Kevin Galligan and Touchlab for creating so many resources around the topic.\n\nThis new threading model forced us to separate the long-lasting logic into self-contained classes which switch the threads and the return a result without freezing any unwanted classes.\n\nPreventing accidental freezing of classes\n\nClasses which are mutable or classes that have mutable dependencies cannot be frozen. If this happens, the moment that a mutation will take place an InvalidMutabilityException will be thrown.\n\nTo prevent this, Kotlin / Native exposes a function called ensureNeverFrozen(). This function throws a FreezingException when the class gets frozen, with a verbose stack trace that helps debug where the freeze started.\n\nThis function and other related to freezing are only available on Kotlin / Native which makes  them unavailable in the common code. The easiest way to get around this is using the Stately-common library.\n\nWriting integration tests in which only the boundary dependencies are replaced (Database, API) helped catch FreezingException in a much faster feedback loop than running the iOS app. Here are some resources related to the threading model and freezing that helped us tremendously.\n\nConcurrency overview | Kotlin Multiplatform Mobile DocsKotlin Multiplatform Mobile Docs HelpConcurrency in Kotlin/Native | KotlinKotlin HelpKotlin Playground: Edit, Run, Share Kotlin Code OnlineKotlin Playground: Edit, Run, Share Kotlin Code OnlineKotlin/Native - Isolated StateYou can have concurrent mutable state with reasonable performance by keeping it isolated to a single thread.DEV CommunityKevin Galligan\n\nExposing coroutines and Flows to Kotlin / Native\n\nCoroutines can be called from Swift given a completionHandler closure is provided with two parameters, one for the success data and one for an error.  The problem is that the second parameter for the error only \"catches\" cancellation exceptions, all other errors thrown from the coroutine will need to be caught. Catching Kotlin errors from Swift is awkward and might get out of hand quickly, a better solution is to create a wrapper:\n\nWorking with Kotlin Coroutines and RxSwiftA recent client engagement involved interop between Kotlin coroutines in shared code, and RxSwift on...DEV CommunityRussell Wolf\n\nLike the articles state, the wrappers require additional layer/layers, but it makes using Kotlin from Swift much simpler and easier. As for coroutines the wrapper makes error handling more explicit by providing two closures / lambdas (for success or failure) additionally it also makes thread switching easier.\n\nExposing Flows is more complicated because it is a continuous stream of data unlike coroutines, Swift cannot use flows without a wrapper. The simplest option is the so called \"CommonFlow\" which allows Swift code to pass a closure which is called every time the flow emits an item.\n\nListen to Kotlin coroutine flow from iOSI have setup a Kotlin Multiplatform project and attached a SQLDelight database to it. Its all setup and running correctly as i have tested it on the android side using the following:commonMain: ...Stack OverflowWazza\n\nThis solution however omits error handling, which is not ideal for production use, but it is nice for getting things started quickly.\n\nMore robust solutions can be found here:\n\nWrapping Kotlin Flow with Swift Combine Publisher in a Kotlin Multiplatform project · John O’ReillyKotlin Coroutines and Swift, revisitedLast year I wrote about a pattern for interop between Kotlin coroutines and RxSwift. I appreciate the...DEV CommunityRussell Wolf\n\nWe've opted to use the flow wrappers that Russell Wolf suggested in his article.\n\nKotlin / Native CoroutinesInternalError\n\nDuring development, we came across a weird error:\n\nUncaught Kotlin exception: kotlinx.coroutines.CoroutinesInternalError: Fatal exception in coroutines machinery for DispatchedContinuation[MainDispatcher, Continuation @ $start$lambda-1COROUTINE$19]. Please read KDoc to 'handleFatalException' method and report this incident to maintainers\n\nHowever, down the stack there is a more helpful message:\n\nCaused by: kotlin.native.concurrent.FreezingException: freezing of Continuation @ $start$lambda-1$lambda-0COROUTINE$18 has failed\n\nA CoroutineExceptionHandler can also be used to give a more clear error message:\n\nprivate val exceptionHandler = CoroutineExceptionHandler { coroutineContext, throwable ->\n\tprintln(throwable.stackTraceToString())\n}\n\nThe CoroutinesInternalError might be caused by the CFRunLoopRun (at least in tests).\n\nError Handling\n\nFor ease of use, it is better to handle as much error handling as it is possible in Kotlin. The previously mentioned coroutine and flow wrappers make it easier to integrate error handling in Swift.\n\nTesting\n\nTesting is a crucial part of the Kotlin Multiplatform module, especially when it comes to the Kotlin / Native threading. The strategy we used for writing tests is using real dependencies where we only replace boundary dependencies (Database, API, Socket etc.) with test doubles.\n\nThanks to this, any concurrency / freezing / mutability exceptions will be caught by the tests before running the iOS app. This speeds up the feedback loop significantly, as running the app takes much longer.\n\nIn order to run suspending functions inside the test, the starter BaseTest class was used (expect android ios) and flows were tested using Turbine.\n\nExpect Actual - Using standard Objective-C frameworks in Kotlin / Native\n\nWe needed to create a class which uses some cryptographic algorithms, KMP doesn't have that great support for it yet, so we opted for implementing them separately in Kotlin / JVM and Kotlin / Native.\n\nWe wrote some tests in commonTest which used a different implementation depending on which platform is running the tests. The JVM implementation was completed without issues, however the Native one was not completed.  \n\nKotlin / Native has access to the CoreCrypto framework / library, but it makes heavy use of custom types which made it difficult to use in Kotlin, which is why we abandoned the idea.\n\nBesides CoreCrypto there are a lot more standard frameworks and maybe for simpler problems they can be utilized to create working implementations in Kotlin / Native.\n\nThe cryptographic algorithms were implemented in Swift and injected to the KMP module (explained later).\n\n\nAndroid\n\nWe didn't find any issues when using Kotlin Multiplatform, so this section is just a placeholder.\n\n\niOS\n\nConsuming coroutines and flows\n\nWhen using the wrappers described in the previous section, consuming coroutines and flows from Swift is not so bad. The biggest issues are generics which need to be casted (e.g. NSArray to [String]).\n\nWe also experimented with creating an additional wrapper for coroutines in Swift. The wrapper uses the Result Enum which makes the Swift integration even easier.\n\nReading, Writing and Debugging Kotlin Code\n\nReading and debugging Kotlin from Xcode is possible thanks to the Kotlin XCode plugin from Touchlab:\n\nGitHub - touchlab/xcode-kotlin: Kotlin Native Xcode PluginKotlin Native Xcode Plugin. Contribute to touchlab/xcode-kotlin development by creating an account on GitHub.GitHubtouchlab\n\nWriting Kotlin code still requires either IntelliJ or Android Studio.\n\nKotlin has a similar syntax to Swift, which doesn't make the language switch so abrupt, but there are some small differences which take some time to get used to. We found that the best way for iOS developers to get more familiar with Kotlin is to write Kotlin code under the supervision of an Android developer (pair/mob programming).\n\nError reporting\n\nFor the POC we used Firebase Crashlytics with the addition of CrashKiOS to make the Kotlin errors show up correctly in the iOS Firebase console.\n\nEven though we completed every step in the CrashKiOS documentation, the Kotlin errors were still unreadable / missing. The solution for us was to modify the Upload dSYMs to Crashlytics step with the following solution.\n\nThe resulting \"upload dSYMs\" script was as follows:\n\n\"KaMPKitiOS/Scripts/upload-symbols\" -val -gsp \"KaMPKitiOS/GoogleService-Info.plist\" -p ios -- \"${DWARF_DSYM_FOLDER_PATH}\"\n\"KaMPKitiOS/Scripts/upload-symbols\" -gsp \"KaMPKitiOS/GoogleService-Info.plist\" -p ios -- \"${DWARF_DSYM_FOLDER_PATH}\" > /dev/null 2>&1 &\n\"KaMPKitiOS/Scripts/upload-symbols\" -gsp \"KaMPKitiOS/GoogleService-Info.plist\" -p ios \"../build/bin/ios/releaseFramework/shared.framework.dSYM/\"\n\"KaMPKitiOS/Scripts/upload-symbols\" -gsp \"KaMPKitiOS/GoogleService-Info.plist\" -p ios \"../build/bin/ios/debugFramework/shared.framework.dSYM/\"\n\nThanks to this the dSYMs of the framework are also uploaded which makes the errors in the console readable.\n\nImplementing Kotlin interfaces / Using Swift implementations in Kotlin Multiplatform\n\nKotlin interfaces are compiled to Objective-C protocols, which makes it possible to implement them in Swift. Special attention is needed when it comes to the types used in the protocol (the easiest way to check the types is to look at the compiled Kotlin code).\n\nThe solution for the cryptographic algorithms was to implement the Kotlin interface in Swift and leverage the native ecosystem for cryptographic frameworks. The biggest issue with this solution is that it makes it impossible to test using Kotlin (at least as far as we know). This resulted in a \"duplicated\" tests for Kotlin / JVM and Swift\n\nIntroducing this Swift implementation into the Koin dependency graph is done the same way as in the starter. An initKoin method is called with the \"to be injected\" classes passed in as parameters.\n\nKotlin Sealed classes need to be casted\n\nKotlin Sealed classes are compiled into Objective-C protocols, which makes smart-casting with the switch statement impossible. Because of this, sealed class sub-types need to be downcasted.\n\n\nConclusion:\n\nKotlin Multiplatform is still a little bit sharp around the edges, but JetBrains and the Kotlin community is working hard on making it better and more stable. We didn't encounter any major blockers stopping us from continuing the POC, making it a valid candidate for production use.\n\nI think the biggest benefit of working on Kotlin Multiplatform was that it forced more interactions between the platforms (Android and iOS) which prompted more knowledge sharing between team members. We found that pair programming sessions with and Android and iOS developers are beneficial because they speed up the knowledge sharing for Kotlin Multiplatform. Overall, the cooperation between both platforms increased significantly since we started working on a Kotlin Multiplatform codebase.\n\nBig thanks to Mateusz Wagner for helping me with the iOS side of the proof of concept.\n\nIf you'd like to dive deeper into the topic check out these articles\n\nRefactoring an Android App to Kotlin MultiplatformRecently I reactivated my old project Timi[https://github.com/AKJAW/Timi-Multiplatform] which I used to learn Compose.This time my focus is on learning the iOS side of Kotlin Multiplatform, I’mhoping that this experience will help me better understand my colleagues on theother platform. Multiplatform, which hopefully might be useful for others."}, {"title": "Writing UI Tests for Pager Layouts in Jetpack Compose", "custom_excerpt": "Writing UI tests for pager layouts can be problematic in Compose. In this post, I'll share my experience and solutions when dealing with such screens.", "published_at": "2022-02-19T15:18:58.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Recently, I was tasked with creating a Pager screen using Compose. Because the screen had a lot of edge cases in it, I also wanted to write UI tests alongside the screen to ensure all features are covered. In this post, I'll share my experience on testing such screens.\n\nAll the working code samples can be found here on the GitHub repository testing-compose-pager.\n\nHere's a brief overview on what will be touched in this article:\n\n * The semantic node tree for pager layouts\n * Swiping between screens\n * Selecting nodes only from a specific pager layout page\n * Selecting off-screen tabs\n\n\nThe app under test\n\nThe main screen just allows navigating to the two pager screens (It will not be tested)\n\nThe static pager screen has 3 predefined pages, the summary page has two buttons that allow changing the selected page. The other two pages just contain a simple text.\n\nThe dynamic pager screen allows for increasing and decreasing the pager layout page count. Every page has more or less the same content.\n\nThe Pager library used for this article comes from the Accompanist Utils library for Jetpack Compose.\n\n\nThe Static Pager Screen\n\nHere's a simplified structure of the static pager screen composable, the full source code is available on the GitHub repository.\n\nenum class StaticPagerScreenPage {\n Summary,\n Info,\n Details,\n}\n\n@OptIn(ExperimentalPagerApi::class)\n@Composable\nfun StaticPagerScreen() {\n val pages = remember {\n listOf(\n StaticPagerScreenPage.Summary,\n StaticPagerScreenPage.Info,\n StaticPagerScreenPage.Details,\n )\n }\n Column {\n TabRow() {\n ...\n }\n HorizontalPager() { index ->\n val page = pages[index]\n Box() {\n ....\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\n\nThe Static Pager Tests\n\nThe static pager screen is easier to test because every page has unique content. Meaning that the testing framework won't be confused when selecting nodes (The next and previous pages don't interfere).\n\n\nSet up\n\n@Before\nfun setUp() {\n composeTestRule.setContent {\n TestingComposePagerTheme {\n StaticPagerScreen()\n }\n }\n}\n\n\nPager semantic node tree\n\nBefore showing the tests, I want to explain how compose interprets the pager content in its semantic node tree.\n\nBy default, the pager works by \"placing\" the current, previous and next page in the tree. This means that at the same time, three pages can exist in the semantic node tree. Here's a simplified tree when opening the static pager screen:\n\n|-Node #5 at (l=0.0, t=303.0, r=1080.0, b=2148.0)px\n CollectionInfo = 'androidx.compose.ui.semantics.CollectionInfo@bf668a0'\n Actions = [IndexForKey, ScrollBy, ScrollToIndex]\n |-Node #18 at (l=0.0, t=303.0, r=1080.0, b=2148.0)px\n | |-Node #19 at (l=241.0, t=675.0, r=840.0, b=768.0)px\n | | Text = '[The Summary page]'\n | | Actions = [GetTextLayoutResult]\n |-Node #26 at (l=0.0, t=303.0, r=1080.0, b=2148.0)px\n |-Node #27 at (l=0.0, t=303.0, r=662.0, b=396.0)px\n Text = '[The Information page]'\n Actions = [GetTextLayoutResult]\n\nThe Node #18 and #26 represent the content of the first and second page. In tests, both of these pages are accessible, but only the first one is displayed:\n\n@Test\nfun theSecondPageIsInTheNodeTree() {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"The Summary page\").assertIsDisplayed()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"The Information page\").assertIsNotDisplayed()\n}\n\n\n\nThe third page however does not exist in the node tree:\n\n@Test\nfun theThirdPageDoesNotExistInTheNodeTree() {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"The Details page\").assertDoesNotExist()\n}\n\n\n\nThis behavior of rendering sibling pages can be problematic when dealing with pages that have content which is not unique between pages. The dynamic pager screen section goes into details on how to differentiate between these types of pages in tests.\n\n\nSwiping between pages\n\nSwiping horizontally between content is one of the most recognizable features of pagers. Fortunately, with compose, swiping gestures are really easy to perform:\n\n@Test\nfun swipingLeftOnRootTwoTimesOpensTheDetailsPage() {\n composeTestRule.onRoot().performTouchInput {\n swipeLeft()\n swipeLeft()\n }\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Details\").assertIsSelected()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"The Details page\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\nThe test verification ensures that the Details tab is selected, and that the details page content is displayed after swiping two times to the left.\n\nPerforming swiping onRoot works because swipes are performed in the middle of the node (which, in this case, the whole screen):\n\nval height: Int get() = visibleSize.height\n\nval centerY: Float get() = height / 2f\n\nfun TouchInjectionScope.swipeLeft(\n startX: Float = right,\n endX: Float = left,\n durationMillis: Long = 200\n) {\n ...\n val start = Offset(startX, centerY)\n val end = Offset(endX, centerY)\n swipe(start, end, durationMillis)\n}\n\n\nChanging pages by tapping the tab\n\nAnother popular way of changing pages is by tapping their corresponding tab.\n\n@Test\nfun clickingInfoTabOpensTheInfoPage() {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Info\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Info\").assertIsSelected()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"The Information page\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\n\nChanging pages by tapping the summary page button\n\nThere might be also additional ways of changing pages.\n\n@Test\nfun clickingGoToInfoOpensTheInfoPage() {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Go to info\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Info\").assertIsSelected()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"The Information page\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\n\nStatic pager summary\n\nBecause all static pages have unique content, all nodes be accessed like in normal composable screens i.e. using onNodeWithText, onNodeWithTag, onNodeWithContentDescription.\n\nThe full static test source code is available on GitHub and here are the tests in action:\n\n\nThe Dynamic Pager Screen\n\nThis screen is more involved because the number of pages can change, and their content is not unique (The increase and decrease buttons).\n\nHere's a simplified structure of the dynamic pager screen composable, the full source code is available on the GitHub repository.\n\n@OptIn(ExperimentalPagerApi::class)\n@Composable\nfun DynamicPagerScreen() {\n var pageCount by remember { mutableStateOf(1) }\n val pages = remember(pageCount) { List(pageCount) { \"Page $it\" } }\n Column {\n ScrollableTabRow() {\n ...\n }\n HorizontalPager(\n count = pages.size,\n state = pagerState,\n ) { index ->\n val page = pages[index]\n Box() {\n ...\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\n\n\nIn order to make the tests easier to write, some test tags can be added (a content description can also be used instead):\n\nColumn {\n ScrollableTabRow(modifier = Modifier.testTag(\"dynamic-pager-tab-row\")) { ... }\n HorizontalPager() { index ->\n val page = pages[index]\n Box(modifier = Modifier.testTag(\"dynamic-pager-$index\")) { ... }\n }\n}\n\n\n\nThe tags can also be extracted to a companion object, to make maintenance easier:\n\nobject TestTagsDynamicPagerScreen {\n\n private const val pager = \"dynamic-pager\"\n const val tabRow = \"dynamic-pager-tab-row\"\n\n fun getPageTag(index: Int) = \"$pager-$index\"\n}\n\n...\n\nColumn {\n ScrollableTabRow(modifier = Modifier.testTag(TestTagsDynamicPagerScreen.tabRow)) { ... }\n HorizontalPager() { index ->\n val page = pages[index]\n Box(modifier = Modifier.testTag(TestTagsDynamicPagerScreen.getPageTag(index))) { ... }\n }\n}\n\n\n\nThe Dynamic Pager Tests\n\n\nSet up\n\n@Before\nfun setUp() {\n composeTestRule.setContent {\n TestingComposePagerTheme {\n DynamicPagerScreen()\n }\n }\n}\n\n\nDealing with repeating page content\n\nBecause every page has the same two buttons, the tests will need to specify which page needs to be used.\n\nFor example, on entering, the dynamic pager screen only has one page available. This is the reason is why the following snipped of code works:\n\n@Test\nfun worksIfOnlyOnePageExists() {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Increase\").performClick()\n}\n\n\n\nBut when there are two pages (both have the Increase button) the testing framework gets confused:\n\n@Test\nfun crashesOnSecondClick() {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Increase\").performClick()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Increase\").performClick()\n}\n\nReason: Expected exactly '1' node but found '2' nodes that satisfy: (Text + EditableText contains 'Increase' (ignoreCase: false))\n\n\n\nTo get around this, the selector needs to know on which screen should the button be clicked:\n\n@Test\nfun doesNotCrash() {\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n}\n\nprivate fun ComposeContentTestRule.onPagerButton(\n pageIndex: Int,\n text: String\n): SemanticsNodeInteraction {\n val isOnTheCorrectPage =\n hasAnyAncestor(hasTestTag(TestTagsDynamicPagerScreen.getPageTag(pageIndex)))\n return onAllNodesWithText(text)\n .filterToOne(isOnTheCorrectPage)\n}\n\nThe basic idea is, that all Increase buttons are selected (using onAllNodesWithText) and then they are filtered using a matcher. The matcher works as follows: \"give me a node that has an ancestor that has a test tag with the requested page index.\"\n\nIn the example above, the Increase button is clicked only on the first page (index == 0).\n\n\nChecking if tabs are created\n\nThe main feature of the dynamic screen is that the number of tabs can be changed on demand.\n\n@Test\nfun increaseClickedOnceTimesThenTabCountIncreases() {\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 0\").assertIsDisplayed()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 1\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\n\nChecking off-screen tabs\n\nWhen there are too many tabs, they will be added off-screen. In order to access them, the tab row will need to be scrolled horizontally.\n\n@Test\nfun increaseClickedMultipleTimesThenTabCountIncreases() {\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithTag(TestTagsDynamicPagerScreen.tabRow)\n .performTouchInput {\n swipeLeft()\n }\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 1\").assertIsDisplayed()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 2\").assertIsDisplayed()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 3\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\n\nClicking increase on a different page\n\nThe increase and decrease buttons are available on every page, and they should behave in the same way regardless of the page.\n\n@Test\nfun increaseOnSecondTabClickedThenTabCountIncreases() {\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 1\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 1, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 0\").assertIsDisplayed()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 1\").assertIsDisplayed()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 2\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\n\nSwiping on page content\n\nInstead of performing the swiping onRoot it can also be performed on one particular page.\n\n@Test\nfun swipingLeftOnPageOpensTheCorrectPage() {\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n composeTestRule.onPagerButton(pageIndex = 0, text = \"Increase\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.swipeOnPagerScreen(pageIndex = 0)\n composeTestRule.swipeOnPagerScreen(pageIndex = 1)\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Page 2\").assertIsSelected()\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"On page: 2\").assertIsDisplayed()\n}\n\nprivate fun ComposeContentTestRule.swipeOnPagerScreen(\n pageIndex: Int\n) {\n onNodeWithTag(TestTagsDynamicPagerScreen.getPageTag(pageIndex))\n .performTouchInput {\n swipeLeft()\n }\n}\n\n\nDynamic pager summary\n\nRepeating content on pages can be problematic, but compose offers a some-what elegant way of selecting content only on one particular page.\n\nThe full dynamic test source code is available on GitHub and below you can see the tests in action:\n\n\nTesting screens with network or database data\n\nThis article dived deep into the Compose framework using simple examples, but what about real use cases where we need to fetch something from the API, or when we need some database rows?\n\nThese exact topics are covered in some of my other articles:\n\nUsing Ktor Client MockEngine for Integration and UI TestsMockEngine replaces real network calls with mocked ones that use pre-defined data and status codes. The engine can be shared between Integration and UI tests.AKJAWAleksander JaworskiKotlin Multiplatform In-Memory SQLDelight Database for Integration and UI Testing on iOS and AndroidDatabases are an integral part of mobile application development, so it’s important that these features are properly tested.AKJAWAleksander JaworskiUsing Apollo Kotlin Data Builders for TestingData Builders provide an easy way for creating GraphQL response classes. In this post I’ll show how they can be used to improve your tests.AKJAWAleksander Jaworski\n\nSide-note: Normally when writing UI Tests it's good to use some form of pattern, like the Robot pattern:\n\nTesting Robots – Jake WhartonLibraries like Espresso allow UI tests to have stable interactions with your app, but without discipline these tests can become hard to manage and require frequent updating. This talk will cover how the so-called robot pattern allows you to create stable, readable, and maintainable tests with the ai…Jake Wharton\n\nIf you have a different way of testing pager screens in compose, feel free to share them below in the comment section.", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", "slug": "testing", "description": null, "feature_image": null,
