"url": "/keeping-gradle-dependencies-up-to-date-with-github-actions-and-refreshversions/", "excerpt": "How to use GitHub Actions to semi-automate dependency updates using RefreshVersions"}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform For Android and the Web - Part 2 The shared module", "custom_excerpt": "This part explains how Kotlin Multiplatform can share code between platforms. Additionally the architecture of the client platform (Web app and Android) is also shown.", "published_at": "2021-08-23T06:18:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "This article series contains parts of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. The thesis is based on my Fuller Stack project which consists of an Android App, a Kotlin React.JS app and a Ktor server. The full thesis pdf can be found here.\n\nPart 1 Introduction and the Server\nPart 2 The shared module (This part)\nPart 3 The Web App\nPart 4 The Android App\nPart 5 The Summary\n\nAs stated in the first part, the shared module contains shared logic and data structures for the client platforms (Android and React). The platform modules depend on the shared module the most.\n\n\nStructure\n\nThe shared module consists of other sub-modules:\n\nEach sub-module is grouped by the platform:\n\n * Common contains code that is written in common Kotlin ensuring that it can be used by all other sub-modules;\n * React contains code for the Kotlin/JS environment;\n * Android contains corde for the Kotlin/JVM environment.\n\nThe platform groups are also split into two:\n\n * Main suffix - contains code that other modules use;\n * Test suffix - contains code for testing the corresponding \"Main\" sub-module.\n\nThe client modules for the apps (Android, React) have only a dependency on the shared module. The sub-modules are irrelevant because depending on the platform, only the corresponding will be included.\n\n\nHow the code is shared\n\nThe following sections will describe two ways which can be used to shared code: The \"Expect and Actual declaration\" and \"Dependency inversion\".\n\n\nThe Expect and Actual declaration\n\nThe declaration is a Kotlin Multiplatform specific feature. It works for most of Kotlin declarations, such as:\n\n * Functions;\n * Classes;\n * Interfaces;\n * Properties.\n\nThis mechanism works as follows: The Common sub-module \"expects\" that the declaration exists, and the platform sub-modules provide the \"actual\" declaration. One can imagine a situation where the shared common sub-module needs a function to log a message, but the platforms have different ways of doing it. Using the expect and actual declaration, the implementation could look like this:\n\n//CommonMain\nexpect fun logMessage(message: String)\n\n//ReactMain\nactual fun logMessage(message: String) {\n console.log(message)\n}\n\n//AndroidMain\nactual fun logMessage(message: String) {\n println(message)\n}\n\nThanks to this declaration the use of the logMessage function is the same for every module but their internal implementation is different depending on the platform.\n\nThe expect, and actual declaration can also be used for data structures. Imagine a situation where the common code uses a database, but the platform specific database implementation requires a different convention for their entities:\n\n//CommonMain\nexpect class ItemEntity {\n val id: Int\n val name: String\n}\n\n//ReactMain\nactual data class ItemEntity(\n actual val id: Int,\n actual val name: String\n)\n\n//AndroidMain\nimport androidx.room.Entity\nimport androidx.room.PrimaryKey\n\n@Entity(tableName = \"items\")\nactual data class ItemEntity(\n @PrimaryKey(autoGenerate = true) actual val id: Int,\n actual val name: String\n)\n\nThe Android module uses the Room persistence library which requires additional annotations such as the database table name and the primary key, on the other hand the React does not need them. Thanks to the expect and actual declarations, this data structure can be used as one and the same, independently of platform specific implementations. This exact situation happened when working on this MSc thesis.\n\n\nDependency inversion\n\nDependency inversion is an object-oriented design which allows for better decoupling between modules. It also promotes better design by forcing classes to depend on abstraction instead of implementation. With dependency inversion, the shared module is able to use outside dependencies which are hidden behind abstraction, while the expect and actual declaration does not (without using dependency inversion).\n\nThe Client class uses the logMessage function directly, but internally it maps to the platform specific implementation.\n\nWhen a module needs something from another module, it depends on that module. In the previous Figure the React and Android modules depend on the Shared module in order to use the logMessage function. If the Shared module needs something from the React or Android module, it cannot simply depend on them because that would lead to circular dependency. As the red crosses suggest, this does not work and the code will not compile.\n\nTo allow the shared module to use something from an external module, dependency inversion shown can be applied.\n\nThe platform specific implementation does not reside in the Shared module anymore, which allows the ConsoleLogger and PrintLogger classes to use other classes from their respective modules without creating a circular dependency. All clients use the Logger interface but behind their abstraction there is a platform specific implementation.\n\n//CommonMain\ninterface Logger {\n\n fun logMessage(message: String)\n}\n\n//React\ninterface ConsoleLogger : Logger {\n\n override fun logMessage(message: String) {\n console.log(message)\n }\n}\n\n//Android\ninterface PrintLogger : Logger {\n\n override fun logMessage(message: String) {\n println(message)\n }\n}\n\nThis technique is possible thanks to dependency injection. All clients have a dependency on the Logger interface without knowing about its internal implementation, which is injected into the clients constructor but are hidden behind an abstraction.\n\n//CommonMain\nclass SharedClient(private val logger: Logger) {\n ...\n}\n\n//React\nclass ReactClient(private val logger: Logger) {\n ...\n}\n\n//Android\nclass AndroidClient(private val logger: Logger) {\n ...\n}\n\n\nModule contents\n\n\nFeatures\n\nMost of the apps features reside in the shared module:\n\n * Creating notes;\n * Retrieving notes;\n * Updating notes;\n * Deleting notes;\n * Synchronizing between the local and API notes;\n * Real time note updates from the web socket;\n * Sorting;\n * Searching.\n\nMost of these operations make use of the local database and the API, which differ between platforms. The React and Android modules provide those implementations.\n\nSorting and searching is implemented using only the Common Kotlin module which comes from the programming language itself.\n\n\nData structures\n\nThe most important data structures are the ones used for representing the notes.\n\n * Note - representation of the note in the UI on both client platforms;\n * NoteEntity - database representation of the Note;\n * NoteSchema - network representation of the Note.\n\nThe database data structure uses the expect and actual declaration. This means that their implementation details are different between the client platforms, but they behave in the same way. The reason for this is that the Android database data structures require additional information for the database to work correctly.\n\nOther less important data structures include:\n\n * Payloads - encapsulate data that is sent to the API;\n * Structures with one value - Helper structures that make the code more decriptive. For example, instead of using a number type for creation and last modification timestamp, they are represented by two data structures CreationTimestamp and LastModificationTimestamp.\n\ndata class LastModificationTimestamp(val unix: Long)\ndata class CreationTimestamp(val unix: Long)\n\n\nArchitecture\n\nFlow of control means that the view layer calls the presentation layer, which then calls the domain layer and so on. The platform specific view and presentation layer will be briefly described here in an abstract manner and more in depth later in their respective chapters.\n\n\nView layer\n\nThe view layer is responsible for showing the UI to the user and reacting to user input e.g. clicking. Usually, most of the classes in this layer are coupled to a framework, for example: Android or React.JS. The logic in this layer should only be related to showing the UI. Adding other logic to this layer makes it almost impossible to reuse in other layers. Additionally, this layer should only have a direct contact with the presentation layer, meaning that the presentation layer orchestrates the UI related data flow.\n\n\nPresentation layer\n\nThe presentation layer orchestrates the data flow between the view layer and the domain layer. The view layer informs of a user (or a system) interaction, which the presentation layer interprets and calls the domain layer. Most of the time the domain layer returns data which is then prepared before being shown in the view.\n\n\nDomain layer\n\nThe domain layer contains the  \"business logic\" of the app (system). This logic defines how the app operates and what it can do. Most of the time, the features of an app are the business logic. In the case of this app, most of the business logic is contained within use cases (sometimes called interactors).\n\nUse cases usually represent a single user (client) interaction with the system, for example: the logic for updating a note is encapsulated inside a use case class called \"UpdateNote\". All of the app features, mentioned in the previous section, are represented by use cases.\n\nFrom an architecture standpoint, use cases help the codebase be more focused and follow the single responsibility principle. Thanks to them the project structure is more clear, and just basing on use cases it is possible to predict what a system does. For example, given a set of use cases called: \"GetNotes\", \"AddNote\" one can guess that a part of the system is responsible for managing notes.\n\nThe use cases used in this project are divided into two categories: \"Synchronous\" and \"Asynchronous\". The  asynchronous use cases contact outside systems like databases or APIs. In other words, the asynchronous use cases have side effects in the form of persisting data or retrieving them. The synchronous use cases are side effect free.\n\nEvery use case is related to the main functionality of the app which are \"Notes\". Additional use cases like \"SingUserIn\", \"SignUserOut\" could have been made if the authentication flow behaved in a similar way on both the client platforms. Unfortunately the authentication libraries used vary vastly between the web and Android.\n\nUse cases can also be composed of other use cases as is the case of the \"SynchronizeNotes\" use case, where it exploits other use cases underneath. This supports keeping the uses cases small or focused which, in turn, helps with the Single responsibility principle.\n\nOne of the most important things about the domain layer is that it should have no outside dependencies. The use of outside systems is possible thanks to the combination of dependency injection and dependency inversion.\n\nThe use cases in the above example use the database and the API but only through an interface which is defined in the domain layer.\n\n\nData layer\n\nThe most notable things the data layer contains in this project are the data sources for the app, which in this case are a database, an API and a web socket. The interfaces for the data sources are defined in the domain layer, but their respective implementation resides in the client platforms.\n\nIn this project besides the database and the API, there is an additional data persistence system which is used for settings. This persistence follows the same dependency rules as the previous data sources.\n\nThe details of the platform implementation will be covered  in the coming Android and React parts.\n\n\nTesting\n\nThe shared module contains the business logic for both of the apps (system) and because of this the shared module contains the most unit tests. The features of the system are represented by use cases which are the most important part of the system. Therefore the test mainly focuses on the use cases which all have their own unit test.\n\nMost of the features connect with the database and the API (both referred to as a storage later). Unit tests have to be fast and reliable which unfortunately is not possible with the real implementation. Therefore, they cannot be used in unit tests because they are neither fast nor reliable (e.g. bad internet connection). Instead of the real implementations a test double can be used, which can be achieved through a framework/library or written by hand e.g. fakes.\n\nFor simplicity sake when referring to mocking I mean using a framework/library for it. Mocking is a way of providing a test double and defining its behavior or state in the test. Every class has public properties and/or functions the clients can use. Both of these things make up the contract which describes how a class can be used. The most straightforward way of defining a contract is through an interface but classes without an interface also define a contract. Additionally, they allow for checking if a mocked property or a function were used. Unfortunately, because of the nature of mocking it is hard to implement a library such that it would work with Kotlin Multiplatform. At the time of writing the thesis there is no reliable way of creating mocks without implementing them by hand.\n\nAnother way of defining test doubles are fakes which usually work in a similar way as the real implementation which usually use an in-memory cache instead of a database or API. The downside is that fakes can only be implemented for interfaces. Because of the nature of Kotlin Multiplatform mocking is difficult so fakes were used instead.\n\nIn the Shared module test suite the most important fakes are the ones that represent the database and the API. They just hold the state in memory which makes them ideal for testing. Most of the tests just assert that the storage has the correct state after executing an action. An additional fake was created for providing a predefined timestamp in order to have consistency when operating with notes timestamps.\n\nThe framework which was used for writing the shared module tests is called Kotest. It provides multiple ways of writing tests depending on the preference, however most of the testing styles leverage Kotlins great support for functions. Additionally, Kotest provides an additional library for making test assertions which use Kotlins capabilities for writing DSLs.\n\n\nTechnologies used\n\nBecause a lot of the implementations are provided by the client platforms which are only orchestrated by the shared module, libraries used in the shared module are sparse:\n\n * Kotlin Coroutines - provides asynchronous programming features to the Kotlin languages. It is mainly used when communicating with outside systems like a database or the API;\n * Klock - library used for operating with notes timestamps and formatting them to dates;\n * Kotlin Serialization - provides JSON serialization for API requests and responses;\n * Kodein - allows multiplatform dependency injection;\n * Kotest - test framework with Kotlin Multiplatform support.\n\n\n\nIf you'd like to dive deeper into this topic check out my other articles\n\nModularizing a Kotlin Multiplatform Mobile ProjectThis article shows an example modularization strategy for Kotlin Multiplatform Mobile projects and touches on some issues related to modularization and the iOS platform.AKJAWAleksander JaworskiKotlin Multiplatform Parameterized Tests and Grouping Using The Standard Kotlin Testing FrameworkKeeping Kotlin Multiplatform tests clean while using the standard kotlin.test frameworkAKJAWAleksander JaworskiTesting on Kotlin Multiplatform Mobile and a Strategy to Speed Up Development TimeAutomated tests are an integral part of developing software, they help catch bugs before they reach the users and save developers time by cutting down manual testing.AKJAWAleksander JaworskiRefactoring an Android App to Kotlin MultiplatformRecently I reactivated my old project Timi[https://github.com/AKJAW/Timi-Multiplatform] which I used to learn Compose.This time my focus is on learning the iOS side of Kotlin Multiplatform, I’mhoping that this experience will help me better understand my colleagues on theother platform. Working…AKJAWAleksander Jaworski", "tags": [{"id": "610422a85e5e944f4dab9a01", "name": "Multiplatform", "slug": "multiplatform", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#7b4fff", "url": "/tag/multiplatform/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-2-the-shared-module/", "excerpt": "This part explains how Kotlin Multiplatform can share code between platforms. Additionally the architecture of the client platform (Web app and Android) is also shown."}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform For Android and the Web - Part 5 The Summary", "custom_excerpt": "The last part of the series contains code statistics, shows what problems I've encountered along the way and at the end I share my opinion on Kotlin Multiplatform", "published_at": "2021-09-13T16:23:10.000+00:00", "plaintext": "This article series contains parts of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. The thesis is based on my Fuller Stack project which consists of an Android App, a Kotlin React.JS app and a Ktor server. The full thesis pdf can be found here.\n\nPart 1 Introduction and the Server\nPart 2 The shared module\nPart 3 The Web App\nPart 4 The Android App\nPart 5 The Summary (This part)\n\n\nProject code distribution\n\nThe code distribution will be counted by Kotlin source code lines meaning that empty lines and comments are not counted. Code like generated libraries (Dukat) and tests were not taken into account.\n\nMost of the code resides in the client platforms, i.e. Android and React, respectively.\n\n\n\nThe platform modules contain a lot of code for the view and presentation layers. However, additionally these modules also contain a lot of implementations for the data layer. Some of the implementations could have been avoided by using Kotlin Multiplatform libraries which work on both platforms. Most notably in this project the networking, settings persistence and maybe the database could have been implemented fully in the shared module.\n\nFor projects with more business logic than the one for this MSc the shared module could potentially have more code compared to the client platforms. The Android and the React platforms are significantly different from each other which makes the code re-usage harder. If the project was comprised of two mobile platforms (Android and iOS) the code reuse would be higher because Kotlin Multiplatform for mobile community is much bigger. There exist more libraries for mobile and additionally the two platforms which are more similar to each other than Android and React.\n\nThe React module does not contain any tests so its lines of code did not change. However, the shared module almost doubles in lines of code because it contains the most tests. In my opinion tests should also be considered in the overall metric, because if the platforms did not share code, both platforms would probably contain production and tests code which would be approximately the same.\n\n\nHiccups / problems encountered\n\n\nKotlin/JS wrapper dependency\n\nSome Kotlin/JS libraries (Mostly related to React.JS) are dependent on the official Kotlin wrapper libraries. From my experience I can state that some versions of the wrappers are not compatible with each other. Having a project like the one below could potentially not work correctly.\n\nThe project uses the Kotlin wrapper library for React.JS with the 17.0.1 version. Additionally, the project uses an external library which internally uses the same Kotlin wrapper library but with a different version. This discrepancy in versions could lead to unexpected errors. The solution would be to find a version of the wrapper and the library that are compatible with each other (maybe by trail and error).\n\n\nKotlin 1.4 update\n\nKotlin 1.4 was a feature release which brought major changes in the language. This update changed some things with how Kotlin multiplatform is defined making the project not compile. However, the fix for this was not hard. The most problematic part of the update was Kotlin/JS and its wrappers / libraries. I was forced to hold of on updating to Kotlin 1.4 until the wrappers and libraries I used were also up to date with this version. Because of this I think that in a project which uses Kotlin/JS feature release updates should not be performed straightaway.\n\n\nPlatform dependent implementation problems\n\nThe main focus of Kotlin Multiplatform is the mobile platforms (Android and iOS). Because of this some of the popular multiplatform libraries do not support Kotlin/JS. This forces some implementations to be done on both platforms instead of the shared module. Because of this separation special care has to be taken when creating these implementations in order not to create a difference between the platforms.\n\nIn this project the local database was the biggest problem when keeping the platforms aligned. The JavaScript database library Dexie had problems. The auto incrementing of the identifier only worked if the note data structure had an undefined value (similar to null in JavaScript) as the Id. Introducing a possibility for an undefined value in the shared module made no sense because it is only a local problem for the Kotlin/JS platform. The solution was creating an intermediary data structure between the JavaScript database and the shared module.\n\nAdditionally, the Android Room database disallows coroutine suspending functions when retrieving values from the database. This made the Kotlin/JS database even harder to implement, I won't go into the details because it is not relevant for this thesis.\n\n\nTesting the shared module\n\nKotlin Multiplatform does not have mocking frameworks which means that all test doubles need to be created by the developer. This is what was done for the API and the database, however sharing these test doubles in tests from different modules is troublesome. For example, in order to use a test double for an Android module it needs to be defined in the main common module. Defining it only in a test module does not make it visible outside of the shared module. This results in some name space pollution because production code can access these classes even though they should just be used by tests.\n\nAnother problem stems from testing coroutines. There exists an additional library for testing coroutines. Unfortunately, it is only available for the JVM which makes it unavailable in the shared module package. This means that the shared module does not have officially supported utilities for testing coroutines.\n\n\nBroken navigation to declaration\n\nThe IDE I used for creating this MSc project is called IntelliJ IDEA. It has a functionality called \"Go to declaration\". What it does is basically to open the file where the function/class etc. was declared. This makes it easier to reason about what is the implementation or what properties/functions (later called member) a class contains. Unfortunately, throughout working on the project I had a bug with this functionality. For example, navigating from the Android module to a Shared module declaration did not work as expected. I wanted to navigate to the exact Kotlin file where the member was declared, however instead of going to the Kotlin file I went to the generated Java class.\n\nThis is an ongoing issue with Kotlin Multiplatform. The oldest issue ticket I was able to find is three years old KTIJ-11683. Searching through the web I was not able to find a working solution because there is little information about this issue. This makes me wonder if this is something that happens seldom and is caused by my project configuration. This is not a make or break problem, yet it is a significant inconvenience when crossing the Android and Shared module boundaries.\n\n\nKotlin/JS RAM usage\n\nEvery time I was working on the React side of this MSc project I was forced to restart my browser from time to time. The reason for this was that my machines memory was disappearing bit by bit every time I made a change in the React module. In web development a popular practice is Live reloading which boils down to refreshing the page whenever a change is made. This can also be achieved with Kolin/JS. However, every time a live reload happened on the project, the memory usage went up a little bit. I suspect that the memory issue is related to live reloading however I do not have 100% certainty. This is just my personal intuition after developing the React module.\n\n\nSummary\n\n\nKotlin/JVM\n\nThe JVM platform was the initial target for Kotlin making it the most refined part of the Kotlin programming language. In this thesis it was used for creating the Android app along with the Server. Because the JVM is already a widely established platform it benefits Kotlin because it can be used everywhere Java and other JVM languages can be used, for example desktop applications.\n\nKotlin/JVM is also the most popular way of using Kotlin. The language has mostly gained traction in the Android development circle, however it is expanding to the server side. Spring is a popular Java framework for creating robust enterprise applications and it also has an official support for Kotlin.\n\nAccording to AppBrain Kotlin is used in 75% of the top 500 USA apps. Keep in mind that the scale of the Kotlin use is unknown, i.e. it could be the whole application or one file.\n\nOn the server side Kotlin is used at such companies as ING, Amazon, Adobe.\n\n\nKotlin/JS\n\nAs shown in this thesis Kotlin can also be used for creating websites or single page applications. It has the ability of being transpiled into JavaScript. On top of this, Kotlin can also call JavaScript code and use JavaScript libraries. Leveraging the JavaScript ecosystem is an important part of the front-end development because there is no point in reinventing the wheel.\n\nAll of this comes with some issues like the one described in the previous section, however I still think Kotlin has a potential for building JavaScript applications. Kotlin/JS is not the main focus of Kotlin and because of this it has a lot more rough edges than for example Kotlin/JVM. As time goes by I do believe that it will be more refined.\n\nThe framework I used for creating the web application was called React.JS which is popular in the JavaScript world. In the near future there will be an official way of building UI for the web which is discussed in the Compose section.\n\nIn my opinion for small projects or projects using Kotlin Multiplatform building web applications using Kotlin/JS might be the right choice. However, for more complex applications I would personally go with the standard which is JavaScript or TypeScript. These communities are much larger than Kotlin/JS which means that there are less issues and they are easier to be resolved.\n\n\nKotlin/Native and more\n\nKotlin/Native was not discussed in this thesis, however it is the main driving force behind Kotlin Multiplatform. Kotlin/Native makes it possible to use Kotlin for writing applications for the following platforms (This is not a complete list):\n\n * macOs;\n * iOS;\n * Linux;\n * Windows.\n\nBesides all of the above platforms Kotlin can also be used as a scripting language. I have not had a chance to use it but I suspect that it could be helpful for small scripts which need to be run from time to time. The main issue would be that Kotlin is not an established language which means that the script is not as portable as for example bash.\n\n\nKotlin overview\n\nIt is a modern language which is not forced to provide backwards compatibility for choices made over 10 years ago (like Java). The Kotlin language has learned from the mistakes of other popular albeit old languages and connected their features into a cohesive package. Before releasing a stable version of Kotlin, Jetbrains was developing it for 6 years which allowed them to refine the language without the worry of backwards compatibility.\n\nAt its current state Kotlin can be used in a plethora of environments and the list just keeps growing. Kotlin Multiplatform offers one of a kind code sharing between platforms where the UI stays native. It might be a solution where an application needs to be released for multiple platforms.\n\n\nCompose\n\nCompose is a UI framework which works in a similar way that React.JS where the UI is driven by state. The Compose framework exposes a DSL for building the UI which looks in a similar way to how it was done in this project.\n\nAt first the framework was focused on the Android platform with the promise of replacing the cumbersome way UI is currently built on Android. In the same vein that Kotlin offers interoperability with Java, Compose also offers interoperability with the old Android UI system. Thanks to this Compose can be introduced into a project step by step, just like Kotlin in a Java codebase without a need of a ground-up rewrite.\n\nJetpack Compose is developed by Google with the focus being Android, however JetBrains has also developed different versions of Compose. The most mature is Compose for Desktop. Although still in alpha it proves that Compose can potentially be used for sharing the UI between platforms. Recently it has been announced that Compose will also be coming to the web with the help of Kotlin/JS. I think that the addition of Compose for the web will make Kotlin/JS more popular in the future.\n\nWhen I stared working on this MSc thesis project Kotlin Multiplatform was still experimental and Jetpack Compose in alpha. Additionally, Compose was not supported in my IDE yet this prompted me to not use it in the Android app. If I were to start working on this project in a year from now I would definitely have chosen Compose for the Android UI. Depending on the state of Compose for web, I would have used it for the web UI.\n\n\nKotlin Multiplatform Mobile\n\nIn my opinion this is bread and butter of Kotlin Multiplatform, the mobile platforms have always been split. Currently the are two main ones: Android and iOS. The mobile platforms usually require two separate development teams where each one is focused on their own platform. Both the teams work on similar things but the only difference is the targeting platform. To reduce the development overhead mobile cross-platform solutions were created like Xamarin, React Native or Flutter which have a varying level of success.\n\nAll of the solutions mentioned above have problems which are UI related. Both of the Mobile platforms differ vastly in the way they create the UI, solutions like Flutter have to come up with a way in which both the platforms UI behave and look in a similar way. In some situations these cross-platform apps might look out of place because they use custom UI elements instead of using what the native platform has to offer. Keep in mind that this is an oversimplification because I am only focusing on the UI without other native parts of the applications.\n\nKotlin Multiplatform works in a different way because instead of sharing everything between the platforms it only focuses on the logic which for sure has to be the same between both of them. Thanks to this the UI stays native along with other native parts which means that both platform teams can leverage everything they have learned about their corresponding platform. The one big thing that is added is the Shared module which from the Android platform perspective will be easier to integrate than on the iOS platform.\n\nIn my opinion one big advantage of Kotlin Multiplatform is that if the solution does not meet the requirements, the Android platform will still have more or less a working App without Kotlin Multiplaform because Android also uses Kotlin. The iOS platform will be in a worse place because there will be a need to recreate the Shared module in their programming language. However, everything native or related to the UI already exists and can be used freely. In the case a different cross-platform solution was used the whole app would have to be rewritten from scratch on both of the platforms.\n\nIf you'd like to dive deeper into this topic check out my other articles\n\nRefactoring an Android App to Kotlin MultiplatformRecently I reactivated my old project Timi[https://github.com/AKJAW/Timi-Multiplatform] which I used to learn Compose.This time my focus is on learning the iOS side of Kotlin Multiplatform, I’mhoping that this experience will help me better understand my colleagues on theother platform. But you want to keep you commits granular, more focused on a single thing. To help with this I use Changelists:\n\n * Default changelists (Changes) - The work that I want to commit now\n * next changelist - Stuff I want to commit at a later point, it is usually a TODO comment, but sometimes it's even initial code changes. However beware, that keeping refactor changes in the next changelist can break compilation, because of broken imports split into two changelists\n * remove changelist - Temporary changes which should not be commited. For example, some experiments with gradle, code, or some hacky workarounds to fix some temporary issues.\n\n\nShelf - Permamently saving something for later (Like stashing but in the IDE)\n\nWhen you're in the middle of working on your code, but have to quickly look something up on the main branch, or change to another, shelving the changes might be your best option. The shelf works like the built in git stash, but it is friendlier to work with, it's also simmilair to the next changelist, but more permament.\n\nKeep in mind that they are not saved in the repository and only in the .idea directory, so they can be lost.\n\n\nFile history\n\nSometines you need to see what changes happened to a file, this is where the Show History function can be used. It lists all of the commits which included changes for this file.\n\n\nHonorable mentions:\n\nResolve Git conflicts | IntelliJ IDEAIntelliJ IDEA HelpInvestigate changes in Git repository | IntelliJ IDEAIntelliJ IDEA Help\n\n\nGeneric tips\n\nIf you'd like to improve your overall IDE workflow, checkout this article\n\n17 IntelliJ IDEA/Android Studio shortcuts and features for a faster development cycleA list of IDE shortcuts and features that I use on an almost daily basisAKJAWAleksander Jaworski\n\n\nCommand line\n\n\nTL;DR\n\nMy build is broken and I don't know why, get me out of here I don't care about losing my progress:  git reset HEAD --hard\n\nI needed to quickly change branches, but I forgot what branch I was on earlier:  git switch -\n\nI want to make a complex commit but I only have the command line: git add -i\n\nMy last commit has some issues (code / commit message): git commit --amend\n\nI don't want to write so much: git config --global alias. \"\"\n\n\nUser friendly file staging\n\n\n\ngit add -i interactive mode which allows adding, removing, diffing, patching and more\n\nInteractive mode provides a much more user friendly staging management. If you don't have your IDE open and need to do a quick commit, add -i might be your best option. Besides adding there are a lot more useful features, feel free to explore them.\n\n\nUndoing current or previous work\n\n\n\ngit reset HEAD - reset staging \"I added some file to staging by accident, let me redo it\"\n\ngit reset HEAD --hard - reset all staged and unstaged files \"I want to throw away all my current changes and start from scratch\"\n\ngit reset HEAD~ - undo the last commit and reset staging but keep them them in the working directory \"I want to clean up something in the last commit / I committed to the wrong branch and want to keep this change for later\"\n\ngit reset HEAD~ --hard - remove the last commit \"The last commit makes no sense, let me throw it away\"\n\n* Beware that resetting something that was already pushed might create conflicts if someone else is working with that branch. If you are sure you're the only one, feel free to force push.\n\n** HEAD~ allows specifying a number, for example: git reset HEAD~3 --hard would remove the last 3 commits\n\nGit - Reset DemystifiedReset Demystified\n\n\nLooking at the commit history / Searching for something\n\n\n\ngit log -S 'code content' - search through commits based on code content\n\ngit log -p -S 'code content' - the same but also shows the commit diff\n\ngit log --grep='commit message content' - search through commits based on the commit message\n\ngit log --pretty=oneline - shows a shortened log\n\nRewriting history\n\n\n\ngit commit --amend - \"I want to clean up the last commit\"\n\ngit rebase -i HEAD~n - rebase the last n commits.\n\nInteractive rebase is a powerful tool for cleaning up the commit history. Here are my most used features:\n\n * r, reword - \"I want to clean up a commit message further into history\"\n * e, edit - \"I want to clean up some code further into history\"\n * s, squash - \"The fix took 5 commits, but it would be cleaner as one. let me squash them into one singular commit\"\n * d, drop - \"Some previous commit is irrelevant, and it is not referenced in newer commits, I can safely remove it\"\n\n* Beware that rebasing or amending changes the commit hashes, which can cause conflicts if the work was already pushed. If you are sure you're the only one working on that branch, feel free to force push.\n\nGit - Rewriting HistoryRewriting HistorySquash commits into one with GitA nice way to group some changes together, especially before sharing them with others.Internal PointersMonocasual LaboratoriesA Branch in Time (a story about revision histories)A talk about software maintainability and the power of our revision histories. I gave this talk at Brighton Ruby and RubyConf in 2018 as well as RubyConf AU in 2019.tekin.co.ukTekin SüleymanTelling stories through your commitsA short talk sharing the key practices to make your commit history usefully document your code\n\n\nCherry picking\n\nThere are situation where:\n\n * You or a colleague did a fix for an issue that's bothering you, but the fix is on a branch which can't be yet merged\n * There is some really nasty merge conflicts, god knows why, but the problematic commit is in a middle of the PR\n\nThis is where you can cherry-pick one or multiple commits onto a different branch. I use it rarely, but when I need it, it is really helpful.\n\nWhat Does Git Cherry Pick Do, And When Should You Use It?git cherry-pick is a simple but powerful tool that allows you to selectively transfer commits from one branch to another. You can use it when you don’t want to merge an entire branch into master, but would still like to include changes from a feature branch.How-To GeekAnthony HeddingsGit Cherry Pick | Atlassian Git TutorialLearn about git cherry-pick, including usage, benefits, and examples using the cherry pick command.AtlassianAtlassian\n\nBranch navigation\n\n\n\n * git checkout \n * git switch \n * git checkout -b - create branch and check out\n * git switch -c - create branch and check out\n * \"git switch -\" - checkout as previous branch\n\nSwitch is basically a newer version of checkout, but with a less action-packed and confusing functionality.\n\nGit 2.23 Adds Switch and Restore CommandsGit 2.23 introduces two new commands meant to replace two common uses of git checkout: git switch to switch to a new branch after creating it if necessary, and git restore to restore changes from a given commit.InfoQSergio De Simone\n\n\nAliases\n\n\n\ngit config --get-regexp alias - listing existing aliases\n\ngit config --global alias. \"\" - setting an alias\n\nMy aliases:\n\nalias.ss status -s\nalias.ai add -i\nalias.logone log --pretty=oneline\nalias.s- switch -\nalias.sc switch -c\n\n\nGit hooks\n\nGit hooks are a powerful tool for automating some part of your workflow, in my case it usually is:\n\n * Formatting code using a linter before committing (pre-commit hook)\n * Ensuring that the commit message is formatted according to Conventional Commits guidelines (commit-msg hook)\n\n\nMy workflow\n\nIs a mix of both IDE and the command line.\n\nIDE:\n\n * Managing changed files\n * Diffing / Viewing changes\n * Committing\n * Resolving conflicts\n\nCommand line:\n\n * Pushing and Force pushing\n * Pulling and Fetching\n * Cleaning up commit history\n * Changing and deleting branches\n * Searching through commits\n * Resetting the workspace or the last commit\n * Cherry picking\n\n\nGit book\n\nMost of my knowledge about how git works comes from the free Pro Git book. The engine can be shared between Integration and UI tests.", "published_at": "2021-12-10T19:09:18.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Recently I started doing more integrations tests which try to use as much real implementation as is possible for a given feature. Also, I stared using Ktor Client for network requests more, and that's when I discovered the MockEngine. The engine replaces real network calls with pre-defined data and status codes.\n\nIn this article, I'll try to show that adding it to a codebase is pretty straight forward. The project used in this article is a sample Android app which illustrates how to use MockEngine for Integration and UI tests\n\n\nThe application\n\nBefore we can start testing the application, we need to know what our requirements are. The app allows the user to search through GitHub repositories sorted by their star count. The application UI contains:\n\n * A text input for the repository name\n * A button which fetches the search results\n * The result, which can be either an error message or a list of repositories\n\n\nMocking the responses\n\n\nUnderstanding the API we're mocking\n\nNow that we know what our functionality is, we need to know what data the GitHub Search API returns, so we can prepare our mocks.\n\nBefore mocking the responses, we could write an exploratory test where we call the real API and just ensure that Ktor responds with the correct response.\n\nclass GithubSearchApi {\n\n private val httpClient = HttpClient {\n install(JsonFeature) {\n serializer = KotlinxSerializer(\n Json {\n prettyPrint = true\n isLenient = true\n ignoreUnknownKeys = true\n }\n )\n }\n }\n\n suspend fun fetchRepositories(): List {\n val wrapper: ResponseWrapperSchema = httpClient\n .get(\"https://api.github.com/search/repositories?q=tetris&sort=stars&order=desc\")\n return wrapper.items\n }\n}\n\nclass GitHubApiExploratoryTest : KoinTest {\n \n @Test\n fun `Correctly fetches \"tetris\" repositories`() = runBlocking {\n val results = GithubSearchApi().fetchRepositories()\n\n results.first().name shouldBe \"react-tetris\"\n }\n}\n\nOnce this test passes, we know that our API assumptions and modeling is correct, then we can start mocking this response.\n\n\nPreparing data for the mock response\n\nThis step just involves copying the actual response to a JSON file or a simple Kotlin string, which we then return in our mocked response\n\nconst val FOR_TETRIS = \"\"\"\n{\n \"total_count\": 46200,\n \"incomplete_results\": false,\n \"items\": [\n {\n \"id\": 76954504,\n \"node_id\": \"MDEwOlJlcG9zaXRvcnk3Njk1NDUwNA==\",\n \"name\": \"react-tetris\",\n \"full_name\": \"chvin/react-tetris\",\n \"private\": false,\n \"owner\": {\n \"login\": \"chvin\",\n \"id\": 5383506,\n \"node_id\": \"MDQ6VXNlcjUzODM1MDY=\",\n \"avatar_url\": \"https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/5383506?v=4\",\n \"gravatar_id\": \"\",\n \"url\": \"https://api.github.com/users/chvin\",\n \"html_url\": \"https://github.com/chvin\",\n// ...\n\nIn the sample project, I also created mocked responses for invalid user input and for \"tet\" search input.\n\n\nCreating a simple MockEngine to pass the exploratory test\n\nThe MockEngine \"constructor\" takes in a lambda which takes in a request (HttpRequestData). Inside the lambda, we can respond to that request.\n\nclass GitHubApiMock {\n\n val engine = MockEngine { request ->\n respond(\n content = MockedApiResponse.FOR_TETRIS,\n status = HttpStatusCode.OK,\n headers = headersOf(HttpHeaders.ContentType, \"application/json\")\n )\n }\n}\n\n\n\nTo use the mocked engine, we need to pass it to the Ktor HttpClient.\n\nclass GithubSearchApi {\n\n val mockEngine = GitHubApiMock().engine\n\n private val httpClient = HttpClient(\n engine = mockEngine\n ) {\n install(JsonFeature) {\n serializer = KotlinxSerializer(\n Json {\n prettyPrint = true\n isLenient = true\n ignoreUnknownKeys = true\n }\n )\n }\n }\n\n suspend fun fetchRepositories(): List {\n val wrapper: ResponseWrapperSchema = httpClient\n .get(\"https://api.github.com/search/repositories?q=tetris&sort=stars&order=desc\")\n return wrapper.items\n }\n}\n\n\n\nThe test should remain the same because we want to verify that it behaves the same way as the real GitHub API.\n\nclass GitHubApiExploratoryTest : KoinTest {\n \n @Test\n fun `Correctly fetches \"tetris\" repositories`() = runBlocking {\n val results = GithubSearchApi().fetchRepositories()\n\n results.first().name shouldBe \"react-tetris\"\n }\n}\n\n\nCustomizing our MockEngine\n\nHaving an API that returns only one response is kind of pointless, however, the MockEngine allows us to extend its functionality based on the request that the user sent.\n\nclass GithubSearchApi {\n\n\t// ...\n\n suspend fun fetchRepositories(\n \tsearchKeyword: String\n ): List? {\n return try {\n val wrapper: ResponseWrapperSchema = httpClient\n .get(\"https://api.github.com/search/repositories?q=$searchKeyword&sort=stars&order=desc\")\n wrapper.items\n } catch (e: ResponseException) {\n null\n }\n }\n}\n\nWe allow the user to pass in a search keyword, which we will handle in the MockEngine.\n\nclass GitHubApiMock {\n\n companion object {\n const val TETRIS_KEYWORD = \"tetris\"\n const val TET_KEYWORD = \"tet\"\n }\n\n val engine = MockEngine { request ->\n handleSearchRequest(request)\n ?: errorResponse()\n }\n\n private fun MockRequestHandleScope.handleSearchRequest(\n \trequest: HttpRequestData\n ): HttpResponseData? {\n \t// 1\n if (request.url.encodedPath.contains(\"search/repositories\").not()) {\n return null\n }\n\n\t\t// 2\n val searchKeyword = request.url.parameters[\"q\"] ?: \"\"\n val responseContent = when (searchKeyword.lowercase()) {\n \"tetris\" -> MockedApiResponse.FOR_TETRIS\n \"tet\" -> MockedApiResponse.FOR_TET\n else -> MockedApiResponse.FOR_INVALID\n }\n\n return respond(\n content = responseContent,\n status = HttpStatusCode.OK,\n headers = headersOf(HttpHeaders.ContentType, \"application/json\")\n )\n }\n\n private fun MockRequestHandleScope.errorResponse(): HttpResponseData {\n return respond(\n content = \"\",\n status = HttpStatusCode.BadRequest,\n headers = headersOf(HttpHeaders.ContentType, \"application/json\")\n )\n }\n}\n\n 1. If the request is for a different endpoint than the repository search, then we respond with an error.\n 2. Based on the user search keyword, we return a different response\n\nSometimes in tests we also want to simulate API errors, so we can handle edge cases gracefully.\n\nclass GitHubApiMock {\n\n private var isSuccess: Boolean? = null\n get() = field ?: throw IllegalStateException(\"Mock has not beet initialized\")\n\n fun givenSuccess() {\n isSuccess = true\n }\n\n fun givenFailure() {\n isSuccess = false\n }\n\n val engine = MockEngine { request ->\n handleSearchRequest(request)\n ?: errorResponse()\n }\n\n private fun MockRequestHandleScope.handleSearchRequest(\n \trequest: HttpRequestData\n ): HttpResponseData? {\n // ...\n\n val statusCode = if (isSuccess == true) {\n HttpStatusCode.OK\n } else {\n HttpStatusCode.InternalServerError\n }\n\n return respond(\n content = responseContent,\n status = statusCode,\n headers = headersOf(HttpHeaders.ContentType, \"application/json\")\n )\n }\n\n // ...\n}\n\nThe givenSuccess and givenFailures are called in the tests in order to set up the correct API response.\n\nThe finished GitHubApiMock class used in the sample project is in the GitHub Repository. The mock is placed alongside production code to keep the example simple, in a real project it could be extracted to a test-utils module for example.\n\nTo make the MockEngine more extendable, instead of creating a method for handling each endpoint request, a class with a similar method could be injected through the constructor.\n\nFor even more extendibility, the class could be hidden behind an interface and be injected as a list of those interfaces.\n\ninterface MockHandler {\n\n fun handleRequest(\n scope: MockRequestHandleScope,\n request: HttpRequestData\n ): HttpResponseData?\n}\n\nclass GitHubApiMock(handlers: List) {\n\n\t// ...\n\n val engine = MockEngine { request ->\n handlers.forEach { handler ->\n val response = handler.handleRequest(this, request)\n if (response != null) {\n return@MockEngine response\n }\n }\n return@MockEngine errorResponse()\n }\n \n // ...\n}\n\n\nThis way the MockEngine doesn't know about the handlers implementation details, and the tests could only inject the handlers which they need.\n\n\nWriting the Integration tests\n\nIn the sample project, the integration tests entry point is the domain class for the search feature. The domain class has the following contract:\n\nclass SearchManager {\n\n suspend fun perform(keyword: String): SearchResult\n}\n\n\n\nFocusing on the Successful and Failure responses\n\nAt first, we don't care about the requirements other than getting the repository search result or an error.\n\n@Test\nfun `On API success returns the correct first result for keyword 'tetris'`() = runTest {\n gitHubApiMock.givenSuccess()\n\n val result = searchManager.perform(GitHubApiMock.TETRIS_KEYWORD)\n \n assertSoftly {\n result.shouldBeInstanceOf()\n result.repositories.first() shouldBe RepositoryInfo(\n id = 76954504,\n name = \"react-tetris\",\n ownerName = \"chvin\"\n )\n }\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `On API failure returns an error`() = runTest {\n gitHubApiMock.givenFailure()\n\n val result = searchManager.perform(GitHubApiMock.TETRIS_KEYWORD)\n\n result shouldBe SearchResult.ApiError\n}\n\n\nValidating user input\n\nTaking into account our business requirement that the search should only happen if the user input length is at least 3 characters long.\n\nFirst, we can write an acceptance test for it:\n\n@Test\nfun `Gives correct result if keyword is invalid`() = runTest {\n val result = searchManager.perform(\"te\")\n\n result shouldBe SearchResult.InvalidKeyword\n}\n\nNow we can implement the validation (of course with a corresponding unit test).\n\nclass SearchKeywordValidator {\n\n fun validate(input: String): Boolean = input.trim().count() >= 3\n}\n\n\nEnsuring that the API was not called\n\nThe MockEngine also allows accessing the request and response history. If we wanted to ensure that the API is not called on an invalid input, we could check the request history on the MockEngine.\n\n@Test\nfun `The API is not called if keyword is invalid`() = runTest {\n val result = searchManager.perform(\"te\")\n\n result shouldBe SearchResult.InvalidKeyword\n\n gitHubApiMock.engine.requestHistory.shouldBeEmpty()\n}\n\n\nHow to set up and maintain Integration tests with the help of dependency injection libraries / frameworks\n\nWriting Integration can be cumbersome if there are a lot of classes, to help this there is a concept called Object Mothers. However, I think that this could be simplified by using a DI library / framework.\n\nBesides simplifying the Integration test set-up, a similar strategy for dependency injection could be probably used when writing UI tests.\n\nThe framework I used in the example is Koin which is really easy to use in testing, however I'm sure this is also possible using other solutions like Dagger Hilt. For more details about my Koin set up, please check out the sample project on GitHub.\n\n\nUsing the MockEngine in UI tests\n\nAn additional benefit of using the Ktor MockEngine is that it can be also used in the Android app. The end user might not appreciate the small amount of results, however the pre-defined responses are a perfect fit for automated UI tests. The tests won't use an internet connection, which should reduce their flakiness, also network edge cases can also be easily mocked up.\n\nI won't go into details of how the tests look like because they all depend on what testing framework is used. The most important part is that the dependency injection graph needs to be structured in such a way, that the UI tests are able to inject the MockEngine and replace the real HttpClientEngine. This step should also be a lot easier if the Integration tests also make use of such dependency graph.\n\nThe UI tests for the sample app look like this:\n\nIf you're interested in their implementation, the source code is in the repository.\n\n\nWhat are the benefits of using Ktor MockEngine\n\nFor me, the biggest benefit is that the full network layer can be tested because we can make the mock behave just like the real API. We don't need to create additional abstractions in order to create test doubles for the network layer.\n\nThose test doubles that simulate the network layer also require special maintenance attention. Changes in the production network layer also need to be applied in the test double.\n\nThe MockEngine also nicely ties into the Classic / Chicago Test Driven Development, where the tests try to use as many production dependencies as possible.\n\nBesides mocking Ktor I've also covered In-Memory SQLDelight testing or Apollo GraphQL fake networking which might interest you. Additionally, I have an article series about what testing mistakes you should avoid if you want to improve your test suite.\n\n\nA Kotlin Multiplatform side-note\n\nI've used the MockEngine with success on a Kotlin Multiplatform Mobile project. However, I haven't got the chance to use it in the iOS UI tests. I don't see why it shouldn't work, but you never know (I'll write an update here when I'll have a chance to try it).\n\nAs far as Kotlin Multiplatform development goes, the MockEngine can really speed up the development cycle because the tests provide fast feedback about the implementation (even on Kotlin / Native).\n\nCurrently, as of writing this article, one of the biggest \"issues\" with Kotlin Multiplatform is the threading model which does not allow mutations on a background thread.\n\nNetwork requests are one of the places that we extensively use the background thread. If we wanted to implement some cache for the response data, we would almost definitely need to mutate something.\n\nWriting Integration tests, where real dependencies are used, can save a lot of debugging time on the iOS side caused by wrong mutations on the background thread. The MockEngine is a great fit for this, because the tests still use the real Ktor HttpClientEngine, but just with predefined responses.", "tags": [{"id": "61b3a61afba33c6d2cd0ec7e", "name": "Testing", The engine can be shared between Integration and UI tests."}, {"title": "Using Apollo Kotlin Data Builders for Testing", "custom_excerpt": "Data Builders provide an easy way for creating GraphQL response classes. In this post I'll show how they can be used to improve your tests.", "published_at": "2022-11-09T17:46:20.000+00:00", "plaintext": "All the code described in the article is available here\n\nGitHub - AKJAW/ApolloDataBuildersContribute to AKJAW/ApolloDataBuilders development by creating an account on GitHub.GitHubAKJAW\n\n\nAPI\n\nThe GraphQL API used for this article is called Countries and returns simple information about continents, countries and languages.\n\nGitHub - trevorblades/countries: 🌎 Public GraphQL API for information about countries🌎 Public GraphQL API for information about countries - GitHub - trevorblades/countries: 🌎 Public GraphQL API for information about countriesGitHubtrevorblades\n\nSandbox: https://studio.apollographql.com/public/countries/explorer\n\nThe Query we will be testing:\n\nquery Country($code: ID!) {\n country(code: $code) {\n name\n languages {\n ...LanguageFragment\n }\n }\n}\n\nfragment LanguageFragment on Language {\n name\n}\n\n\n\nResponse for CountryQuery(\"GB\"):\n\n{\n \"data\": {\n \"country\": {\n \"name\": \"United Kingdom\",\n \"languages\": [\n {\n \"name\": \"English\"\n }\n ]\n }\n }\n}\n\n\nApollo\n\nBased on the query above, Apollo generates something along these lines:\n\npublic data class CountryQuery(\n public val code: String,\n) : Query {\n\n public data class Data(\n public val country: Country?,\n ) : Query.Data\n\n public data class Country(\n public val name: String,\n public val languages: List,\n )\n\n public data class Language(\n public val __typename: String,\n public val languageFragment: LanguageFragment,\n )\n}\n\npublic data class LanguageFragment(\n public val name: String?,\n) : Fragment.Data\n\nThe Country data class can be instantiated manually\n\nCountryQuery.Country(\n name = \"United Kingdom\",\n languages = listOf(\n CountryQuery.Language(\n __typename = \"Language\",\n languageFragment = LanguageFragment(\"English\")\n )\n )\n)\n\nHowever, this involves a lot of boilerplate because there are no default values. Additionally, whenever the query changes the compilation will break (all constructor calls need to be updated)\n\nTo mitigate this, Apollo provides Data builders, which provides a nice DSL for the class creation.\n\n\nData Builders\n\nThe main benefit of builders is that they provide default values for any known types (Unknown custom scalars will be explained later) and they don't care about fragments. Meaning that they don't break so easily when the query is updated.\n\n\nUnit tests\n\nUsually the Query data will be converted to a domain data structure, in this case it could be:\n\ndata class Country(\n val name: String,\n val language: List,\n)\n\ndata class Language(\n val name: String\n)\n\n\n\n\nGiven we have a correctly implemented converter, we could have a test that verifies that the country name is correctly converted:\n\n@Test\nfun `The country name is correctly converted`() {\n val schema = CountryQuery.Data {\n country = buildCountry {\n name = \"Poland\"\n }\n }.country!!\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(schema)\n\n result.name shouldBe \"Poland\"\n}\n\n\nThe data builder creates the whole Query \"Data\" that's why we need to access the nullable .country!! at the end.\n\nTo avoid repeating the same boilerplate every time, a helper function can be extracted:\n\n@Test\nfun `The country name is correctly converted`() {\n val schema = createCountry {\n name = \"Poland\"\n }\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.convert(schema)\n\n result.name shouldBe \"Poland\"\n}\n\nprivate fun createCountry(\n block: CountryBuilder.() -> Unit\n): CountryQuery.Country =\n CountryQuery.Data {\n country = buildCountry {\n block()\n }\n }.country!!\n\n\n\nThanks to this, the boilerplate is mitigated while still allowing the full usage like:\n\ncreateCountry {\n name = \"Poland\"\n languages = listOf(\n buildLanguage {\n name = \"Polish\"\n },\n )\n}\n\n\nThe full converter test is available in the repository.\n\n\nCustom Scalars\n\nWhen the data builder encounters a field which was not set inside the DSL, it will use a Resolver to put a default value there. For numbers, it starts at 0 and increments every time it is used, for strings the field name is used.\n\nHowever, sometimes the GraphQL schema contains a type which Apollo cannot resolve\n\n\n\"\"\"\nA custom scalar for presentation purposes\n\"\"\"\nscalar CustomScalar\n\nquery CountryWithCustomScalar {\n country(code: \"GB\") {\n name\n customScalar\n }\n}\n\n\n\nBecause Apollo does not know what to do with this type, an error will be thrown if it is not set in the DSL\n\njava.lang.IllegalStateException: Don't know how to instantiate leaf CustomScalar\n\n\n\nTo help with this, we can define our own resolver\n\nclass MyFakeResolver : FakeResolver {\n\n private val delegate = DefaultFakeResolver(__Schema.all)\n\n override fun resolveLeaf(context: FakeResolverContext): Any {\n return if (context.mergedField.type.leafType().name == \"CustomScalar\") {\n return \"\"\n } else {\n delegate.resolveLeaf(context)\n }\n }\n\n override fun resolveListSize(context: FakeResolverContext): Int {\n return delegate.resolveListSize(context)\n }\n\n override fun resolveMaybeNull(context: FakeResolverContext): Boolean {\n return delegate.resolveMaybeNull(context)\n }\n\n override fun resolveTypename(context: FakeResolverContext): String {\n return delegate.resolveTypename(context)\n }\n}\n\n\nThis resolver uses an empty string for every CustomScalar field and delegates to the default behavior in every other case.\n\nVerification test:\n\n@Test\nfun `Does not throw exception with resolver`() {\n shouldNotThrowAny {\n CountryWithCustomScalarQuery.Data(MyFakeResolver()) {\n country = buildCountry {\n name = \"Custom\"\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `Throws an exception without resolver`() {\n shouldThrow {\n CountryWithCustomScalarQuery.Data {\n country = buildCountry {\n name = \"Custom\"\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\n\n\nThe Resolver and verification test can be found in the repository.\n\n\nIntegration / UI tests\n\nIn cases where we want to test our logic along with the network layer, for example through Mock HttpInterceptor or MockServerHandler. The data builder can be used to provide the JSON string.\n\nobject Responses {\n\n// {\"data\":{\"country\":{\"name\":\"United Kingdom\",\"languages\":[{\"__typename\":\"Language\",\"name\":\"English\"}]}}}\n val SUCCESS =\n CountryQuery.Data {\n country = buildCountry {\n name = \"United Kingdom\"\n languages = listOf(\n buildLanguage {\n name = \"English\"\n }\n )\n }\n }.toDataJson()\n\n// {\"data\":{\"country\":null}}\n val SUCCESS_NULL_SCHEMA = CountryQuery.Data {\n country = null\n }.toDataJson()\n\n private fun Operation.Data.toDataJson() =\n \"\"\"{\"data\":${this.toJsonString()}}\"\"\"\n\n private fun CountryQuery.Data.toDataJsonKmm() =\n \"\"\"{\"data\":${CountryQuery_ResponseAdapter.Data.obj().toJsonString(this)}}\"\"\"\n}\n\n\nThe main benefit of using the builder here is that the JSON will always be up-to-date with the latest Query changes and is more resilient to parsing issues.\n\nSidenote: the JVM Apollo implementation provides a generic Operation.Data.toJsonString() function however on KMM, every query requires its own function (like in the snippet above).\n\nThe full integration test code is available here.\n\nBesides Apollo I've also covered Mocking Ktor network requests and In-Memory SQLDelight testing which might interest you. Additionally, I have an article series about what testing mistakes you should avoid if you want to improve your test suite.\n\n\nGraphQL testing course\n\nRecently I had the pleasure of recording a couple of GraphQL testing course videos which will be a part of the KotlinTesting Unit testing course. Unfortunately, the course is currently only available in Polish, but we're planning to expand it to a more world-wide audience. If you're interested in the English version or have any questions, please let me know.\n\nIn the course, I discuss things like:\n\n * How Apollo can be used for building apps using a GraphQL API\n * Pros and Cons of different data creation Manual instantiation vs Builders\n * Sanity tests for quick prototyping and learning how an API works\n * Verifying the system correctness through integration tests which include the network layer\n * Different ways of creating Integration tests in Apollo\n\nAll the course videos are recorded using a Test Driven Development approach which some might find interesting, along the way I also share some information about my approach to testing.\n\nMore information about the course can be found here:\n\nWebinar | Praktycznie o testach na "excerpt": "Data Builders provide an easy way for creating GraphQL response classes. In this post I'll show how they can be used to improve your tests."}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform In-Memory SQLDelight Database for Integration and UI Testing on iOS and Android", "custom_excerpt": "Databases are an integral part of mobile application development, so it's important that these features are properly tested. ", "published_at": "2022-06-11T22:33:09.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Databases are an integral part of almost every mobile application, in this post I'll cover how to use SQLDelight to provide an in-memory database for test environments.\n\n\nWhy use in-memory databases?\n\nTests should be predictable, and the execution order of the tests should change the test results. If we were to use a real database for tests, then this database would need to be cleared before (or after) every test case execution.\n\nEven if a real database is cleared between tests, it is not guaranteed.  Sometimes the test can crash, or  be aborted, this can result in the database being persisted between test runs, which might change the outcome of the next test.\n\nIn-memory databases have the advantage of being discarded after every test case, so the next test execution should have no way of re-using an old in-memory database instance.\n\n\nApp features\n\nThe application shown in this article is available on GitHub. It has three features which we will be testing:\n\n * Adding new items\n * Updating existing items\n * Showing the list of items\n\nThe functionality is pretty basic, but it is enough to show how you can verify correct app behavior using an in-memory database.\n\n\nThe App Architecture\n\nI tried keeping the code as simple as possible because it is not a main focus of the article. Basically, there are 3 UseCases corresponding to the app features:\n\n * GetTimestampItems\n * AddTimestampItem\n * UpdateTimestampItem\n\nThe basic idea is that the Add and Update UseCases insert / update items in the database with the current timestamp:\n\ninternal class AddTimestampItem(\n private val tableQueries: TableQueries,\n private val timestampProvider: TimestampProvider,\n) {\n\n suspend fun execute() = withContext(Dispatchers.Default) {\n tableQueries.insertItem(timestampProvider.getTimestampMilliseconds())\n }\n}\n\nGetting the items boils down to retrieving them from the database and converting them to a more UI friendly format.\n\ninternal class GetTimestampItems(\n private val tableQueries: TableQueries,\n) {\n\n fun execute(): Flow> =\n tableQueries.selectAll()\n .asFlow()\n .mapToList()\n .map { items -> ... }\n\t\n ...\n}\n\n\n\nThe two important things in this article is TableQueries and TimestampProvider. The TableQueries is an object that is generated by SQLDelight, in tests we will replace the production database with an in-memory one. TimestampProvider returns the current system timestamp, but in tests we will replace it with a test doubles that allows controlling the returned value (relying on real system time makes the test unpredictable).\n\n\nDatabase Schema\n\nCREATE TABLE TimestampItemEntity (\n id INTEGER NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,\n time INTEGER NOT NULL,\n version INTEGER AS Int NOT NULL\n);\n\nselectAll:\nSELECT * FROM TimestampItemEntity;\n\ninsertItem:\nINSERT OR IGNORE INTO TimestampItemEntity(time, version)\nVALUES (?, 1);\n\nupdateTimestamp:\nUPDATE TimestampItemEntity SET time = ?, version = version + 1 WHERE id = ?;\n\n\nEvery item has:\n\n * An auto-generated ID\n * The timestamp which will be shown in the UI\n * A version which is increased when the item is updated\n\n\nIntegration tests\n\nBefore diving into the tests, I just want to point out that all tests use Koin for replacing the production dependencies and retrieving classes used in the test.\n\nIn this article I won't get into the details of using Koin in tests, if  you want to learn more about this topic I invite you to my other article focused around this topic. All the test code is also available in the GitHub Repository if you want to just look at the source code.\n\n\nTesting added items\n\nclass AddTimestampItemTest : KoinComponent {\n\n private val tableQueries: TableQueries by inject()\n private val mockTimestampProvider: MockTimestampProvider by inject()\n private val systemUnderTest: AddTimestampItem by inject()\n\n @BeforeTest\n fun setUp() {\n startTestKoin()\n }\n\n @AfterTest\n fun tearDown() {\n stopTestKoin()\n }\n\n @Test\n fun `Added item uses the current timestamp as the name`() = runTest {\n mockTimestampProvider.setNextTimestamp(123)\n\n systemUnderTest.execute()\n\n val result = tableQueries.selectAll().executeAsList().first()\n result.time shouldBe 123\n }\n\n @Test\n fun `Added item has a version equal to 1`() = runTest {\n systemUnderTest.execute()\n\n val result = tableQueries.selectAll().executeAsList().first()\n result.version shouldBe 1\n }\n}\n\n\nThe test cases set a predefined value for the TimestampProvider, then invoke the UseCase and verifies that the database is in the correct state.\n\n\nTimestampProvider test double\n\ninterface TimestampProvider {\n\n fun getTimestampMilliseconds(): Long\n}\n\nclass MockTimestampProvider : TimestampProvider {\n private var timestampValues: MutableList = mutableListOf()\n private var lastValue: Long = 0\n\n fun setNextTimestamp(value: Long) {\n timestampValues.add(value)\n }\n\n override fun getTimestampMilliseconds(): Long {\n lastValue = timestampValues.firstOrNull() ?: lastValue\n timestampValues.removeFirstOrNull()\n return lastValue\n }\n}\n\nMockTimestampProvider allows setting predefined values that are returned when the  getTimestampMilliseconds() is called. The reason for using a \"Queue\" for the values will be explained later, but it has to do iOS UI testing.\n\n\nSetting up the in-memory database driver\n\nThe in-memory driver creation is done by an expect-actual function:\n\n// commonMain\nexpect fun createInMemorySqlDriver(): SqlDriver\n\n\n\nThe Android in-memory function is pretty simple because it just uses the JVM JDBC driver:\n\n// androidMain\nactual fun createInMemorySqlDriver(): SqlDriver {\n val driver: SqlDriver = JdbcSqliteDriver(JdbcSqliteDriver.IN_MEMORY)\n AppDatabase.Schema.create(driver)\n return driver\n}\n\n\n\n\nFor iOS, it gets more complicated:\n\n// iosMain\nprivate var index = 0\n\nactual fun createInMemorySqlDriver(): SqlDriver {\n index++\n val schema = AppDatabase.Schema\n return NativeSqliteDriver(\n DatabaseConfiguration(\n name = \"test-$index.db\",\n version = schema.version,\n create = { connection ->\n wrapConnection(connection) { schema.create(it) }\n },\n upgrade = { connection, oldVersion, newVersion ->\n wrapConnection(connection) { \n schema.migrate(it, oldVersion, newVersion) \n }\n },\n inMemory = true\n )\n )\n}\n\nThe base function was created by Touchlab in their KaMPKit starter. I just added the index variable, so the database name is different on each invocation. Without this, the same database is reused in each test case, making the tests unpredictable (running order changes the test outcome).\n\nThe createInMemorySqlDriver() function is then used by Koin to introduce the correct database driver to the dependency graph:\n\nval testModule = module {\n single { MockTimestampProvider() }\n single { get() }\n single { createInMemorySqlDriver() }\n}\n\n\n\n\nTesting items retrieval\n\nclass GetTimestampItemsTest : KoinComponent {\n\n private val tableQueries: TableQueries by inject()\n private val systemUnderTest: GetTimestampItems by inject()\n\n @BeforeTest\n fun setUp() {\n startTestKoin()\n }\n\n @AfterTest\n fun tearDown() {\n stopTestKoin()\n }\n\n @Test\n fun `The date is correctly mapped`() = runTest {\n tableQueries.insertItem(1652980264000)\n tableQueries.insertItem(1652980224000)\n\n val result = systemUnderTest.execute().first()\n\n assertSoftly {\n result shouldHaveSize 2\n result[0].name shouldBe \"2022-05-19T17:11:04\"\n result[1].name shouldBe \"2022-05-19T17:10:24\"\n }\n }\n}\n\nIn the set-up phase, two items are inserted to the database, then the UseCase retrieves the items and the test case verifies that the timestamp is correctly formatted.\n\n\nAndroid UI Tests\n\n\nSetting up the in-memory database driver\n\nThe significant difference with the in-memory database driver for Android UI tests is that the Android driver has to be used:\n\nAndroidSqliteDriver(\n schema = AppDatabase.Schema,\n context = get(),\n name = null,\n)\n\n\nThe Android OS does not support the JVM database driver, so the normal AndroidSqliteDriver is required, however passing null as the name of the database, makes the database an in-memory one:\n\n/**\n* @param name Name of the database file, or null for an in-memory database.\n* @return This\n*/\n@NonNull\npublic Builder name(@Nullable String name) {\n mName = name;\n return this;\n}\n\n\n\n\nThe test\n\nThe UI test cases focus on adding a new item and updating an existing one. As I stated previously, Koin usage for testing is covered in my other article.\n\nclass MatchScreenTest : KoinComponent {\n\n @get:Rule\n val composeTestRule = createAndroidComposeRule()\n val mockTimestampProvider: MockTimestampProvider by inject()\n\n @Before\n fun setUp() {\n ...\n }\n\n @Test\n fun addingAnItemPopulatesTheListWithCurrentTimestamp() {\n mockTimestampProvider.setNextTimestamp(1653823433000)\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Add\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.waitUntilCatching(1000) {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"2022-05-29T11:23:53\").assertIsDisplayed()\n }\n }\n\n @Test\n fun refreshingAnItemUpdatesTheNameWithCurrentTimestamp() {\n mockTimestampProvider.setNextTimestamp(0)\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"Add\").performClick()\n mockTimestampProvider.setNextTimestamp(1653823433000)\n\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithContentDescription(\"Refresh\").performClick()\n\n composeTestRule.waitUntilCatching(1000) {\n composeTestRule.onNodeWithText(\"2022-05-29T11:23:53\").assertIsDisplayed()\n }\n }\n\n private fun ComposeTestRule.waitUntilCatching(\n timeoutMillis: Long, assertion: () -> Unit\n ) = ...\n}\n\nThe test cases set a pre-defined value for the TimestampProvider just as the integration test did, then it executes an action and verifies if the correct UI text is displayed.\n\n\niOS UI Tests\n\n\nSetting up the in-memory database driver\n\nThe same createInMemorySqlDriver() can be used just like in the integration tests.\n\n\nSetting up dependency injection\n\nUsing Koin in iOS UI testing is not as straight forward as for Android, so I'll quickly run through the steps.\n\nfun setUpKoinForTesting() {\n val modules = listOf(\n *sharedModules,\n module {\n single { createInMemorySqlDriver() }\n single { MockTimestampProvider() }\n single { get() }\n }\n )\n unloadKoinModules(modules)\n loadKoinModules(modules)\n}\n\nThe setUpKoinForTesting() function needs to be called between every iOS test case to \"reload\" the database.\n\nobject TestDoubleRetriever : KoinComponent {\n\n val mockTimestampProvider: MockTimestampProvider\n get() = get()\n}\n\nTestDoubleRetriever is just a helper object to make test double retrieval easier on iOS.\n\n\nThe test\n\nThe main difference between Android and iOS UI testing is that on iOS, we are not allowed to touch production code inside the test. So, calling setUpKoinForTesting() in the setUp function will not work.\n\nThe iOS testing framework allows sending so-called launch arguments:\n\napp.launchArguments = [\"enable-testing\", \"-mock-time\", \"0,1653823433000\"]\napp.launch()\n\nThese values can be used in the production code to set up the test doubles to the correct state:\n\n#if DEBUG\nif CommandLine.arguments.contains(\"enable-testing\") {\n SetUpKoinForTestingKt.setUpKoinForTesting()\n UserDefaults.standard.string(forKey: \"mock-time\")?\n .split(separator: \",\")\n .map { timeString in\n Int64(timeString)\n }\n .compactMap { $0 }\n .forEach { time in\n TestDoubleRetriever.shared.mockTimestampProvider\n .setNextTimestamp(value: time)\n }\n}\n#endif\n\n\n\nThe test cases are pretty much the same as for the Android app, just with a different test double set up:\n\nclass KaMPKitiOSUITests: XCTestCase {\n let app = XCUIApplication()\n\n func testAddingAnItemPopulatesTheListWithCurrentTimestamp() {\n app.launchArguments = [\"enable-testing\", \"-mock-time\", \"1653823433000\"]\n app.launch()\n \n app.staticTexts[\"Add\"].tap()\n\n let itemExists = app.staticTexts[\"2022-05-29T11:23:53\"].exists\n XCTAssert(itemExists)\n }\n \n func testRefreshingAnItemUpdatesTheNameWithCurrentTimestamp() {\n app.launchArguments = [\"enable-testing\", \"-mock-time\", \"0,1653823433000\"]\n app.launch()\n app.staticTexts[\"Add\"].tap()\n \n app.buttons[\"Refresh\"].tap()\n\n let itemExists = app.staticTexts[\"2022-05-29T11:23:53\"].exists\n XCTAssert(itemExists)\n }\n}\n\nAs you can see, the TimestampProvider values have to be defined before the app even launches. This limitation enforces that the Kotlin test doubles have to provide a way of defining multiple return values beforehand, and that is the reason for the \"Queue\" in the MockTimestampProvider.\n\n\nSummary\n\nI hope that this simple example shows how SQLDelight in-memory drivers can be helpful when testing the application.\n\nBesides SQLDelight I've also covered Mocking Ktor network requests and Apollo GraphQL fake networking which might interest you.
