"}, {"title": "Implementing a Stopwatch with Kotlin Coroutines and Flow Part 2 - Multiple Stopwatches with Unit and Compose UI Tests", "custom_excerpt": "The first part focused on getting the stopwatch working, this one will focus on changing the implementation to support multiple stopwatches.", "published_at": "2021-06-16T06:14:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "The first part focused on getting the stopwatch working, this one will focus on changing the implementation to support multiple stopwatches.\n\n\nPart 1 Reminder\n\nHere's how the orchestrator for handling one stopwatch looked like:\n\ninternal class StopwatchListOrchestrator(\n private val stopwatchStateHolder: StopwatchStateHolder,\n private val scope: CoroutineScope,\n) {\n\n private var job: Job? = null\n private val mutableTicker = MutableStateFlow(\"\")\n val ticker: StateFlow = mutableTicker\n\n fun start() {\n if (job == null) startJob()\n stopwatchStateHolder.start()\n }\n\n private fun startJob() {\n scope.launch {\n while (isActive) {\n mutableTicker.value = stopwatchStateHolder.getStringTimeRepresentation()\n delay(20)\n }\n }\n }\n\n fun pause() {\n stopwatchStateHolder.pause()\n stopJob()\n }\n\n fun stop() {\n stopwatchStateHolder.stop()\n stopJob()\n clearValue()\n }\n\n private fun stopJob() {\n scope.coroutineContext.cancelChildren()\n job = null\n }\n\n private fun clearValue() {\n mutableTicker.value = \"\"\n }\n}\n\n\nThe StopwatchStateHolder represents one stopwatch instance, it has the methods for changing the stopwatch state and for getting its string representation. The implementation of it will be omitted as it remains the same as in the first part of this article series.\n\n\nA little background\n\nThe app I'm building (Timi Compose) revolves around so-called tasks, the idea is that the user will use the stopwatch to track the time spent on doing a task. In this article, the tasks will only be used as an identifier without going into details: one task can only have one stopwatch, and a stopwatch cannot exist without a task.\n\n\nWhat has to change\n\nCurrently, the public API of the orchestrator only assumes one stopwatch:\n\nclass StopwatchListOrchestrator(...) {\n\n val ticker: StateFlow\n \n fun start()\n\n fun pause()\n\n fun stop()\n}\n\nEvery stopwatch action will require a task in order to differentiate between multiple stopwatches:\n\nfun start(task: Task)\n\nfun pause(task: Task)\n\nfun stop(task: Task)\n\nThe ticker StateFlow which is collected in the UI only emits a string, in order to correctly bind tasks to their stopwatches it needs to emit a data structure which allows for that. For this I've used a Map which is then iterated on in the UI to produce a list of stopwatches:\n\nval ticker: StateFlow>\n\nThe last thing that needs to change is the fact that the Orchestrator only uses one StopwatchStateHolder for all stopwatch actions. Currently, a single Holder is injected through the constructor, but this only allows for a single stopwatch to exist.\n\nTo allow multiple stopwatches, the Holder needs to be created when a stopwatch is first started. However, instead of creating the Holder inside the Orchestrator it's better to move this responsibility to a separate class which will be a factory for the Holders:\n\ninternal class StopwatchStateHolderFactory(\n private val stopwatchStateCalculator: StopwatchStateCalculator,\n private val elapsedTimeCalculator: ElapsedTimeCalculator,\n private val timestampMillisecondsFormatter: TimestampMillisecondsFormatter,\n) {\n\n fun create(): StopwatchStateHolder {\n return StopwatchStateHolder(\n stopwatchStateCalculator = stopwatchStateCalculator,\n elapsedTimeCalculator = elapsedTimeCalculator,\n timestampMillisecondsFormatter = timestampMillisecondsFormatter\n )\n }\n}\n\n\nApplying the changes\n\n\nThe constructor\n\ninternal class StopwatchListOrchestrator(\n private val stopwatchStateHolderFactory: StopwatchStateHolderFactory,\n private val scope: CoroutineScope,\n)\n\n\nStateFlow ticker\n\nprivate var job: Job? = null\nprivate var stopwatchStateHolders = ConcurrentHashMap()\nprivate val mutableTicker = MutableStateFlow>(mapOf())\nval ticker: StateFlow> = mutableTicker\n\nThe reasoning behind the ticker type was explained earlier however one additional property was introduced to keep track of the existing stopwatches: stopwatchStateHolders.\n\nA ConcurrentHashMap was used to safely modify the Map without causing a ConcurrentModificationException. This exception could happen because one thread is reading the value (updating the ticker) and a different thread modifies it (changing the stopwatch state).\n\n\nStopwatch start\n\nfun start(task: Task) {\n if (job == null) startJob()\n val stopwatchForTask = stopwatchStateHolders.getOrPut(task) {\n stopwatchStateHolderFactory.create()\n }\n stopwatchForTask.start()\n}\n\nprivate fun startJob() {\n job = scope.launch {\n while (isActive) {\n val newValues = stopwatchStateHolders\n .toSortedMap(compareBy { task -> task.id })\n .map { (task, stateHolder) ->\n task to stateHolder.getStringTimeRepresentation()\n }\n .toMap()\n mutableTicker.value = newValues\n delay(20)\n }\n }\n}\n\nThe job is only null when there are no existing stopwatches.\n\nThe stopwatchStateHolders is used to update the ticker and map the tasks to their stopwatch time.\n\nThe stopwatch is created with the getOrPut extension function, which calls the passed in lambda in order to create the element (only if it doesn't exist).\n\nI've done performance tests to the ticker update loop by providing 10000 stopwatches, and it seemed to work fairly well. The mapping lasted well over 20 milliseconds, but to the end user there was nothing out of the ordinary. Looking at this from the perspective of real world usage, I don't believe there will be a lot of situations when over 5 stopwatches are running at the same time.\n\n\nStopwatch pause\n\nfun pause(task: Task) {\n val stopwatchForTask = stopwatchStateHolders[task] ?: return\n stopwatchForTask.pause()\n if (areAllStopwatchesPaused()) stopJob()\n}\n\nprivate fun areAllStopwatchesPaused(): Boolean =\n stopwatchStateHolders.values.all { stateHolder ->\n stateHolder.currentState is StopwatchState.Paused\n }\n\nThe pausing is self-explanatory however when all existing stopwatches are paused then the ticker update job is stopped. The reasoning for this is that when no stopwatches are running, the ticker value won't change either way.\n\n\nStopwatch stop\n\nfun stop(task: Task) {\n stopwatchStateHolders.remove(task)\n if (stopwatchStateHolders.isEmpty()) {\n stopJob()\n resetTicker()\n }\n}\n\nIn case no stopwatches exist, the ticker update job is also stopped with the addition of resetting the ticker value.\n\n\nHelpers\n\nprivate fun stopJob() {\n scope.coroutineContext.cancelChildren()\n job = null\n}\n\nprivate fun resetTicker() {\n mutableTicker.value = mapOf()\n}\n\nThe difference between canceling the coroutine scope and its children was already outlined in the first part, but I'll try to briefly explain it. Cancelling the scope prevents any new stopwatches from starting in the future. Cancelling only the children does not block new stopwatches from starting.\n\n\nThe full Orchestrator implementation\n\ninternal class StopwatchListOrchestrator(\n private val stopwatchStateHolderFactory: StopwatchStateHolderFactory,\n private val scope: CoroutineScope,\n) {\n private var job: Job? = null\n private var stopwatchStateHolders = ConcurrentHashMap()\n private val mutableTicker = MutableStateFlow>(mapOf())\n val ticker: StateFlow> = mutableTicker\n\n fun start(task: Task) {\n if (job == null) startJob()\n val stopwatchForTask = stopwatchStateHolders.getOrPut(task) {\n stopwatchStateHolderFactory.create()\n }\n stopwatchForTask.start()\n }\n\n private fun startJob() {\n job = scope.launch {\n while (isActive) {\n val newValues = stopwatchStateHolders\n .toSortedMap(compareBy { task -> task.id })\n .map { (task, stateHolder) ->\n task to stateHolder.getStringTimeRepresentation()\n }\n .toMap()\n mutableTicker.value = newValues\n delay(20)\n }\n }\n }\n\n fun pause(task: Task) {\n val stopwatchForTask = stopwatchStateHolders[task] ?: return\n stopwatchForTask.pause()\n if (areAllStopwatchesPaused()) stopJob()\n }\n\n private fun areAllStopwatchesPaused(): Boolean =\n stopwatchStateHolders.values.all { stateHolder ->\n stateHolder.currentState is StopwatchState.Paused\n }\n\n fun stop(task: Task) {\n stopwatchStateHolders.remove(task)\n if (stopwatchStateHolders.isEmpty()) {\n stopJob()\n resetTicker()\n }\n }\n\n private fun stopJob() {\n scope.coroutineContext.cancelChildren()\n job = null\n }\n\n private fun resetTicker() {\n mutableTicker.value = mapOf()\n }\n}\n\n\n\nUnit tests\n\nI won't show all the tests because it would create too much noise, if you're interested checkout the GitHub repository. It contains tests for the classes from the first part and of course this new orchestrator.\n\nThe test framework I'm using is JUnit5, strikt for assertions and mockk.\n\n\nTest expectThat(result[TASK1]).isEqualTo(\"2\")\n expectThat(result[TASK2]).isEqualTo(\"2\")\n}\n\n\nStopwatch pause\n\n@Test\nfun `When a stopwatch is paused then its value should not change`() = runBlockingTest {\n givenStateHolderReturnsTime(\"0\", \"1\", \"2\", \"3\", \"4\", \"5\")\n systemUnderTest.start(task = TASK1)\n coroutineDispatcher.advanceTimeBy(50)\n systemUnderTest.pause(task = coroutineScope.coroutineContext.job\n expectThat(job.children.count()).isEqualTo(count)\n}\n\n@Test\nfun `When the first stopwatch is started then scope should become active`() {\n become inactive`() {\n every { stopwatchStateHolder.currentState }\n .returns(StopwatchState.Paused(0.toTimestampMilliseconds()))\n systemUnderTest.start(task = TASK1)\n systemUnderTest.start(task = systemUnderTest.pause(task = TASK1)\n\n systemUnderTest.start(task = TASK1)\n\n expectThat(coroutineScope).hasChildrenCount(1)\n}\n\n\nUI tests\n\nDisclaimer: I'm not that experienced with UI testing, The implementation of the test infrastructure is hidden because it's not that important for this article. All the tests are available in the repository, they use the Jetpack Compose testing framework.\n\nThe most important part is that for testing the TimestampProvider is replaced with a stub in order to avoid test flakiness. )\n}\n\n\nStopwatch pause\n\n@Test\nfun pausingAStopwatchPreventsItFromUpdatingTheTime() {\n timestampProviderStub.currentMilliseconds = 0\n stopwatchScreenRobot.clickAddButton()\n stopwatchScreenVerifier.confirmStopwatchForTaskHasTime(\n taskName = FIRST_TASK_NAME,\n timestamp = \"01:00:000\"\n )\n}\n\nFrom what I experienced when creating Compose UI tests is that they are flaky, probably because the Jetpack Compose testing framework is still under development. However, I'm sure that with time it will be more reliable.\n\n\nSummary\n\nI tried my best to keep the process of adding support for multiple stopwatches simple and hope that it was at least somewhat insightful.\n\nThe current implementation only keeps the stopwatch in the memory, meaning that if the app is closed/killed the stopwatches are gone. In the future, I'm planning on creating an Android Service which will keep the stopwatch running in the background. "Step-by-step guide how I implemented a Kotlin/JS app with Redux and Material UI", "published_at": "2020-06-21T15:20:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Preface\n\nAfter struggling for a couple of days with setting up a Kotlin React app in my Kotlin multiplatform project. I thought I would share my experience and provide a set-up that worked for me. I have created a repository Kotlin/JS React example which can be used as a starting point or reference when creating React applications in Kotlin.\n\nKotlin/JS with React is still not that well documented unfortunately, most of the information I used in this project comes from open source projects or discussions. This is why I thought that creating such an example app can help some people set up their projects faster.\n\nIn this blog post I will go more in-depth about the app and its inner-workings. A brief overview can be found in the repository README.\n\n\nThe app\n\n\nDependencies\n\nFor this project I used Gradle Kotlin DSL, but Groovy can be used as well. Make sure to check Muirwik compatibility with the Kotlin wrapper versions. The current 0.5.1 is compatible with the pre.104-kotlin version of wrappers.\n\nplugins {\n id(\"org.jetbrains.kotlin.js\") version \"1.3.72\"\n}\n\nrepositories {\n mavenCentral()\n maven(\"https://kotlin.bintray.com/kotlin-js-wrappers/\")\n maven(\"https://dl.bintray.com/cfraser/muirwik\")\n jcenter()\n}\n\ndependencies {\n implementation(kotlin(\"stdlib-js\"))\n\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains.kotlinx:kotlinx-html-js:0.7.1\")\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains:kotlin-react:16.13.1-pre.104-kotlin-1.3.72\")\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains:kotlin-react-dom:16.13.1-pre.104-kotlin-1.3.72\")\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains:kotlin-react-redux:5.0.7-pre.104-kotlin-1.3.72\")\n implementation(npm(\"react\", \"16.13.1\"))\n implementation(npm(\"react-dom\", \"16.13.1\"))\n\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains:kotlin-redux:4.0.0-pre.104-kotlin-1.3.72\")\n\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains:kotlin-styled:1.0.0-pre.104-kotlin-1.3.72\")\n implementation(\"org.jetbrains:kotlin-css-js:1.0.0-pre.104-kotlin-1.3.72\")\n implementation(\"com.ccfraser.muirwik:muirwik-components:0.5.1\")\n implementation(npm(\"styled-components\"))\n implementation(npm(\"inline-style-prefixer\"))\n implementation(npm(\"@material-ui/core\", \"^4.9.14\"))\n\n testImplementation(kotlin(\"test-js\"))\n}\n\nkotlin.target.browser { }\n\n\nReact\n\n\nContainer class components\n\nI treat containers as Redux entry points into React, they probably should also contain the React state of the components, but I like to use state hooks for that. I wasn’t able to get rConnect working with functional components unfortunately.\n\ninterface TaskListConnectedProps : RProps {\n var isLoading: Boolean\n var tasks: Array\n var fetchTasks: () -> Unit\n var addTask: (task: Task) -> Unit\n var removeTask: (task: Task) -> Unit\n}\n\nprivate interface StateProps : RProps {\n var isLoading: Boolean\n var tasks: Array\n}\n\nprivate interface DispatchProps : RProps {\n var fetchTasks: () -> Unit\n var addTask: (task: Task) -> Unit\n var removeTask: (task: Task) -> Unit\n}\n\nprivate class TaskListContainer(props: TaskListConnectedProps) \n : RComponent(props) {\n\n override fun componentDidMount() {\n props.fetchTasks()\n }\n\n override fun RBuilder.render() {\n child(taskList) {\n attrs.isLoading = props.isLoading\n attrs.tasks = props.tasks\n attrs.addTask = props.addTask\n attrs.removeTask = props.removeTask\n }\n }\n}\n\nval taskListContainer: RClass =\n rConnect(\n { state, _ ->\n tasks = state.taskListState.tasks\n isLoading = state.taskListState.isLoading\n },\n { dispatch, _ ->\n fetchTasks = { dispatch(TaskListSlice.fetchTasks()) }\n addTask = { task -> dispatch(TaskListSlice.AddTask(task)) }\n removeTask = { task -> dispatch(TaskListSlice.RemoveTask(task)) }\n }\n )(TaskListContainer::class.js.unsafeCast>())\n\n\nFunctional components\n\nThey are the UI representation of the component with optional local react state provided by hooks. All components prefixed with m like mList, mButton are provided by Muirwik.\n\ninterface TaskListProps : RProps {\n var isLoading: Boolean\n var tasks: Array\n var addTask: (task: Task) -> Unit\n var removeTask: (task: Task) -> Unit\n}\n\nprivate object TaskListClasses : StyleSheet(\"TaskList\", isStatic = true) {\n val loading by css {\n minHeight = LinearDimension(\"200px\")\n display = Display.flex\n justifyContent = JustifyContent.spaceAround\n alignItems = Align.center\n }\n val actions by css {\n display = Display.flex\n justifyContent = JustifyContent.spaceAround\n alignItems = Align.center\n\n children {\n firstChild {\n margin = \"5px 10px 0 0\"\n }\n }\n }\n val task by css {\n margin = \"10px 0\"\n }\n}\n\nval taskList = functionalComponent { props ->\n val (taskName, setTaskName) = useState(\"\")\n val (isTaskNameInvalid, setIsTaskNameInvalid) = useState(false)\n\n styledDiv {\n if (props.isLoading) {\n css(loading)\n mCircularProgress { }\n } else {\n styledDiv {\n css(TaskListClasses.actions)\n mTextField(\n label = \"Task\",\n variant = MFormControlVariant.outlined,\n value = taskName,\n error = isTaskNameInvalid,\n fullWidth = true,\n onChange = {\n setIsTaskNameInvalid(false)\n setTaskName(it.targetInputValue)\n }\n )\n mButton(\n caption = \"Add\",\n variant = MButtonVariant.outlined,\n onClick = {\n if(taskName.isBlank()){\n setIsTaskNameInvalid(true)\n } else {\n props.addTask(Task(taskName))\n setTaskName(\"\")\n }\n }\n )\n }\n mList {\n props.tasks.forEach { task ->\n mCard {\n css(TaskListClasses.task)\n mListItem(button = true, onClick = { props.removeTask(task) }) {\n + task.name\n }\n }\n }\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\n\nRedux\n\nThe state for the whole app, every reducer state will have their own property in this class.\n\ndata class AppState(\n val taskListState: TaskListSlice.State = TaskListSlice.State()\n)\n\nI like the idea which is used in Redux Toolkit where a single feature resides in a single slice. This slice contains all the information needed for the Redux reducer.\n\nobject TaskListSlice {\n data class State(\n val tasks: Array = emptyArray(),\n val isLoading: Boolean = true\n )\n\n private val fetchTaskThunk = FetchTaskThunk()\n\n fun fetchTasks(): RThunk = fetchTaskThunk\n\n data class SetIsLoading(val isLoading: Boolean): RAction\n\n data class SetTasks(val tasks: Array): RAction\n\n data class AddTask(val task: Task): RAction\n\n data class RemoveTask(val task: Task): RAction\n\n fun reducer(state: State = State(), action: RAction): State {\n return when (action) {\n is SetIsLoading -> state.copy(isLoading = action.isLoading)\n is SetTasks -> state.copy(tasks = action.tasks)\n is AddTask -> state.copy(tasks = state.tasks + action.task)\n is RemoveTask -> state.copy(tasks = state.tasks.filterNot { task -> task == action.task }.toTypedArray())\n else -> state\n }\n }\n}\n\nReducers are combined using this function\n\nfun combinedReducers() = combineReducersInferred(\n mapOf(\n AppState::taskListState to TaskListSlice::reducer\n )\n)\n\n// Credit https://github.com/JetBrains/kotlin-wrappers/blob/master/kotlin-redux/README.md\nfun combineReducersInferred(reducers: Map, Reducer<*, A>>): Reducer {\n return combineReducers(reducers.mapKeys { it.key.name })\n}\n\nAnd the store is created with the createStore function, note that the last argument of the compose function enables Redux dev tools.\n\nval myStore = createStore(\n combinedReducers(),\n AppState(),\n compose(\n rThunk(),\n rEnhancer(),\n js(\"if(window.__REDUX_DEVTOOLS_EXTENSION__ )window.__REDUX_DEVTOOLS_EXTENSION__ ();else(function(f){return f;});\")\n )\n)\n\n\nRedux thunk\n\nThis is the setup that I use for thunks\n\ninterface RThunk : RAction {\n operator fun invoke(\n dispatch: (RAction) -> WrapperAction,\n getState: () -> AppState\n ): WrapperAction\n}\n\n// Credit https://github.com/AltmanEA/KotlinExamples\nfun rThunk() =\n applyMiddleware(\n { store ->\n { next ->\n { action ->\n if (action is RThunk)\n action(store::dispatch, store::getState)\n else\n next(action)\n }\n }\n }\n )\n\nval nullAction = js {}.unsafeCast()\n\nHere’s one dummy thunk defined in this example app, after a timeout it dispatches a list of tasks to the reducer.\n\nclass FetchTaskThunk: RThunk {\n override fun invoke(dispatch: (RAction) -> WrapperAction, getState: () -> AppState): WrapperAction {\n dispatch(TaskListSlice.SetIsLoading(true))\n window.setTimeout({\n val tasks = listOf(\n Task(\"Kotlin\"),\n Task(\"is\"),\n Task(\"awesome\")\n )\n dispatch(TaskListSlice.SetTasks(tasks.toTypedArray()))\n dispatch(TaskListSlice.SetIsLoading(false))\n }, 2000)\n\n return nullAction\n }\n}\n\n\nWebpack\n\nTo create a custom webpack config, there has to be a directory named webpack.config.d in the project root. In this directory any file with the extension config.js will be treated as the webpack config. Here’s the config of the example app:\n\nconfig.devServer = Object.assign(\n {},\n config.devServer || {},\n {\n open: false,\n // port: 8080\n }\n)\n\nNote that I only override the fields that I need, and the rest is used from the default config. Overriding the whole config can and probably will break some things.\n\n\nMy experience and some tips\n\n * Use the —continuous argument for the gradle run command. It doesn’t work perfectly, but it is better than nothing. Usually the second page refresh contains the desired changes.\n * The error When accessing module declarations from UMD, they must be marked by both @JsModule and @JsNonModule is usually caused by the fact the @JsModule doesn't also have the @JsNonModule annotation. This can be fixed by creating a wrapper for the imports. Just like ReduxImportsWrapper.kt in this project.\n * If you’re using coroutines, the scope can be passed into the thunk as a constructor parameter, thanks to this you can cancel it if there is a need.\n * The Muirwik demo app has a great showcase of the available components and how to use them.\n\nHere’s a list of resources that helped me immensely:\n\n * Building Web Applications with React and Kotlin/JS\n * React in Kotlin/JS, What I learned by Jolanrensen\n * Kotlin React Redux PoC (A great resource for learning Redux in Kotlin)\n * KotlinExamples (I used it to implement Redux thunk)", Redux and Material UI"}, {"title": "Using Bash to build and install Android flavors", "custom_excerpt": "A Bash script which allows for easy building, installing and launching Android flavors ", "published_at": "2020-04-25T14:51:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "At my current work, we heavily rely on Android flavors. Unfortunately installing them with Android studio is problematic because of the scale of the project. Because of that we mostly use the gradle wrapper and the default tasks provided by gradle for building/installing the app.\n\nMy typical workflow was as follows:\n\n * run “./gradlew assemble{Variant}{BuildType}”\n * find the path of the apk\n * run “adb install -r {PathToApk}”\n\nI know there is an install{Variant}{BuildType} task, but I find it is not so reliable. There is an added problem that this task assembles the app first, so if you just want to install the apk you have to wait for the app to be rebuilt.\n\nThe variants in the project I’m working with, sometimes have more complex naming schemes which cause typos when running from the command line. The typos and the amount of manual steps motivated me to create a bash script which could make this process a lot more streamlined.\n\nThe first version of this script only installed the apk, it only needed the name or shortcut of the app to do that. After some feedback from the team I got a request to expand the functionality of the script. The current version can build, install and even open the app on the phone or emulator.\n\n\nThe app\n\nFor the purpose of this blog post I have made a basic app, which has flavors that somewhat reflect the variants in my work project. Here’s the companion project repository\n\nThe gradle variants are declared as following:\n\nandroid {\n flavorDimensions \"flavor\", \"api\"\n productFlavors {\n apple {\n dimension \"flavor\"\n applicationIdSuffix \".apple\"\n }\n orange {\n dimension \"flavor\"\n applicationIdSuffix \".orange\"\n }\n appleCarrotLime {\n dimension \"flavor\"\n applicationIdSuffix \".appleCarrotLime\"\n }\n mock {\n dimension \"api\"\n applicationIdSuffix \".mock\"\n }\n production {\n dimension \"api\"\n applicationIdSuffix \".production\"\n }\n }\n}\n\nBasically this app has 3 juice flavors all of which also have 2 variants depending on if the API should be production or mock, this gives a total of 6 flavors. Building the app from the command line using the default gradle tasks would look something like this:\n\n$ ./gradlew assembleOrangeProductionDebug\n$ ./gradlew assembleAppleMockDebug\n$ ./gradlew assembleAppleCarrotLimeProductionDebug\n\nAfter the build finishes an apk file is created. To install the app on the device, a path to the apk has to be provided:\n\n$ adb install -r ./app/build/outputs/apk/appleCarrotLimeProduction/debug/app-appleCarrotLime-production-debug.apk\n\nOverall this process is not complicated, but a little time-consuming. Fortunately the gradle tasks and build folders have the flavor names in them, which drastically simplified the script creation.\n\n\nThe script\n\nI will break down the script to a list of steps and explain them, if you want the full code click here.\n\nWhat the script can do:\n\n\n- Show a help menu\n- Install an app with the possibility to:\n - Build it before installing\n - Open it after the install\n\n\nFor this script to work with all the capabilities, adb and aapt have to be added to $PATH. The full script has a comment on the top with a little more information.\n\nGetting the command line flags is achieved with getopts:\n\napp=\"\"\nisMock=false\nbuildTheApp=false\nopenTheApp=false\nshowHelp=false\n\nwhile getopts \"a:mboh\" flag; do\n case \"${flag}\" in\n a) app=\"${OPTARG}\" ;;\n m) isMock=true ;;\n b) buildTheApp=true ;;\n o) openTheApp=true ;;\n h) showHelp=true ;;\n esac\ndone\n\nHere are some examples:text\n\n$ ./script.sh -h\nsets the showHelp flag to true\n\n$ ./script.sh -a apple -b\nsets the app variable to \"apple\" and the buildTheApp flag to true\n\nIf the showHelp flag is set to true it simply echos some info, and stops the execution:\n\nif [[ \"$showHelp\" == true ]]\nthen\n echo \"Available flags:\"\n echo \"'-a app' what app to install\"\n echo \"'-m' use the mock version\"\n echo \"'-b' build the app\"\n echo \"'-o' open the app (only works if you add aapt to path)\"\n exit 0\nfi\n\nHere are the flavor declarations, and a helper setAppFolderName function to check if the provided app/flavor name or shortcut is correct:\n\ndeclare -a appMap=(\n \"ap=apple\"\n \"or=orange\"\n \"acl=appleCarrotLime\"\n)\n\nsetAppFolderName(){\n #make the user app $1 input lowercase\n appNameArgument=$(echo \"$1\" | tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]')\n for i in \"${!appMap[@]}\"; do\n #split appMap element with \"=\" and assign them\n appShortcut=$(echo ${appMap[$i]} | cut -d'=' -f1)\n appFullName=$(echo ${appMap[$i]} | cut -d'=' -f2)\n if [[ \"$appNameArgument\" == \"$appShortcut\" ||\n \"$appNameArgument\" == \"$appFullName\" ]]\n then\n #set the return variable $2 (folderPrefix)\n eval \"$2=$appFullName\"\n break 1\n fi\n done\n}\n\nThe way I return a variable from this script is with an eval assigment. $2 corresponds to the second variable passed in to this function. If the app name is correct, then the full name of the flavor is “returned” by assigning it to $2.\n\nWhen the app is incorrect then setAppFolderName doesn’t return anything, when that happens the script asks for a correct name in a loop:\n\nfolderPrefix=\"\"\nsetAppFolderName $app folderPrefix\n\nwhile [ -z \"$folderPrefix\" ]; do\n for i in \"${!appMap[@]}\"; do\n echo ${appMap[$i]}\n done\n read -p \"Enter a shortcut or full name: \" input\n setAppFolderName $input folderPrefix\ndone\n\nSetting the API flavor is done with a simple if assignment. By default, the Production flavor is chosen:\n\nif [[ \"$isMock\" == true ]]\nthen\n type=\"Mock\"\nelse\n type=\"Production\"\nfi\n\nIf the -b flag was present, then the app is built before the installation:\n\nif [[ \"$buildTheApp\" == true ]]\nthen\n appNameCapitalized=\"$(tr '[:lower:]' '[:upper:]' <<< ${folderPrefix:0:1})${folderPrefix:1}\"\n buildCommand=\"./gradlew assemble${appNameCapitalized}${type}Debug\"\n echo \"running: $buildCommand\"\n eval \"$buildCommand\"\nfi\n\nInstalling the app is a little more complicated, first the apk file has to exist before it is installed. If the apk exists, what happens if there are more than one apk file in the build folder? This shouldn’t happen, but if it does, then the apk with the most recent date is used:\n\nappFolderPath=\"app/build/outputs/apk/$folderPrefix$type\"\nif [[ ! -d \"$appFolderPath\" ]]\nthen\n echo \"$appFolderPath doesn't exist\"\n exit 1\nfi\n\n#take the first apk from ls sorted with -t\nappDebugFolder=\"$appFolderPath/debug\"\nfullFilePath=$(ls -t $appDebugFolder/*.apk | head -1)\n\n#execute adb install -r on apk\necho \"Installing $fullFilePath\"\neval \"adb install -r $fullFilePath\"\n\nOpening the app is as easy as copying the solution from stack overflow:\n\nif [[ \"$openTheApp\" == true ]]\nthen\n pkg=$(aapt dump badging $fullFilePath|awk -F\" \" '/package/ {print $2}'|awk -F\"'\" '/name=/ {print $2}')\n act=$(aapt dump badging $fullFilePath|awk -F\" \" '/launchable-activity/ {print $2}'|awk -F\"'\" '/name=/ {print $2}')\n eval \"adb shell am start -n $pkg/$act\"\nfi\n\nThe benefit of opening the app this way is that no activities have to be specified. Even if the flavors have a different entry point, this script always opens the app using the main launcher activity.\n\nI have been using this script for a couple of weeks now, and it made working with flavor’s a lot easier.\n\nHere’s the link to the Juice app repository, and the script.\n\nThe script in action:", "tags": [{"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de748a", "name": "Android", "slug": "android", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#67b54b", "url": "/tag/android/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de748f", "name": "Bash", "slug": "bash", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": null, "url": "/tag/bash/"}], "url": "/using-bash-to-build-and-install-android-flavors/", "excerpt": "A Bash script which allows for easy building, installing and launching Android flavors "}, {"title": "Using Dagger 2 Multibindings to Avoid Coupling in a Multi Flavor Android Project", "custom_excerpt": "Adding flavor dependent features on a shared screen can be troublesome. The problem gets even worse when you don't want to couple app flavors to features they don't use", "published_at": "2021-04-16T10:29:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Introduction\nAdding flavor dependent features on a shared screen can be troublesome. The\nproblem gets even worse when you don't want to couple app flavors to features\nthey don't use. In this article I'll show you how we do in FootballCo\n[https://www.linkedin.com/company/footballco] using Dagger 2 Multibinding\n[https://dagger.dev/dev-guide/multibindings.html], AdapterDelegates\n[https://github.com/sockeqwe/AdapterDelegates] and some abstractions.\n\nThis example will focus on injecting feature specific RecyclerView items and a\nconcept of \"customOrDefault\" that we use in our codebase. Multibindings however\nhave a lot more uses than shown here. If you know of other use cases please feel\nfree to share them in the comments below. \n\nThe apps\nThere will be two app flavors: a basic and a premium one.\n\nThe basic flavor shows 3 football matchesThe premium flavor has additional 3\nbasketball matchesI tried to keep the app simple while still tackling the\nAndroid flavor coupling problem.\n\nGradle modules\nA diagram of the project gradle modules and their dependenciesThe app module\nContains the Activity, RecyclerView Adapter and most importantly it contains the\nentry point for the dagger modules.\n\nAdditionally, the premium package contains an implementation which is used to\noverride the default app implementation using the customOrDefault concept (which\nwill be explained later).\n\nThe gradle dependencies for the app module are as follows:\n\nimplementation project(\":framework\")\nimplementation project(\":dependency:football\")\n\npremiumImplementation project(\":dependency:bastketball\")\n\n\n\nThe Football and Basketball modules\nContain:\n\n * their corresponding match data structure\n * an AdapterDelegate which are used for extending the capabilities of the\n RecyclerView Adapter (shown later)\n * a MatchProvider which just returns a list of matches either Football or\n Basketball depending on the module.\n\nTo keep the example simple the modules implementations are mirrored with slight\nchanges. But multibindings can be used in much more complicated situations where\nthe flavor features are much different.\n\nThe Framework module\nThis module includes classes which are not implementation details but rather the\nthings that are used by multiple modules, for example:\n\n * Interfaces and other abstractions\n * Data structures\n * Utilities\n\nFlavor based RecyclerView extension without coupling modules\nIn the first part of this section I will be showing how the AdapterDelegates\n[https://github.com/sockeqwe/AdapterDelegates] library works. If that does not\ninterest you please read the summary below and navigate to the second part\n\n> Summary:\nAdapterDelegates are mini RecyclerView Adapters which can be added to the Main\nAdapter in order to extend the supported types.\n\nIn the first solution the Main Adapter is extended by injecting a concrete\nclass. This means that changing the supported types of the Main Adapter requires\nchanges in the constructor. Multibinding solves the problem by providing an\nabstraction in the form of a set. Thanks to this set, the constructor requires\nno changes when a type is added/removed/changed.\nHow AdapterDelegates work\n> \"Favor composition over inheritance\" for RecyclerView Adapters\nAdapterDelegates could be thought of like mini RecyclerView Adapters which can\nbe plugged in into the main Adapter in order to support more item types. This\nmeans that adding an additional type to a list only requires an additional\nAdapterDelegate without needing to add extra logic to the main Adapter.\n\nThe AdapterDelegate for football matches in this project:\n\nThe data structure:\n\ndata class FootballMatch(\n val homeTeamName: String,\n val awayTeamName: String,\n) : DisplayableItem\n\n\n\nDisplayableItem is just a marker interface to indicate that this data structure\nis used as a RecyclerView Adapter item\n\ninterface DisplayableItem\n\n\n\nTaking all of this into account, an AdapterDelegate for a football match looks\nlike this:\n\ninternal class FootballMatchAdapterDelegate @Inject constructor() :\n\tAdapterDelegate>() {\n\n override fun isForViewType(items: List, position: Int): Boolean =\n items[position] is FootballMatch\n\n override fun onCreateViewHolder(parent: ViewGroup): RecyclerView.ViewHolder =\n FootballMatchViewHolder(\n parent.inflateLayout(R.layout.item_football_match)\n )\n\n override fun onBindViewHolder(\n items: List,\n position: Int,\n holder: RecyclerView.ViewHolder,\n payloads: MutableList\n ) {\n val match = items[position] as FootballMatch\n (holder as FootballMatchViewHolder).bind(match)\n }\n}\n\nThe FootballMatchAdapterDelegate will be responsible for showing any\nFootballMatch that is present in the DisplayableItem list. The ViewHolder\nimplemented in the same way as any other ViewHolder, so I won't show it. The\nbasketball implementation is just a mirror to this implementation is also\nomitted.\n\nThe main RecyclerView Adapter for the FootballMatchAdapterDelegate:\n\nclass MatchListAdapter(\n footballMatchAdapterDelegate: FootballMatchAdapterDelegate\n) : RecyclerView.Adapter() {\n\n private val delegatesManager = AdapterDelegatesManager>()\n private var items = listOf()\n\n init {\n delegatesManager.addDelegate(footballMatchAdapterDelegate)\n }\n\n override fun getItemViewType(position: Int): Int {\n return delegatesManager.getItemViewType(items, position)\n }\n\n override fun onCreateViewHolder(\n \tparent: ViewGroup, \n viewType: Int\n ): RecyclerView.ViewHolder =\n delegatesManager.onCreateViewHolder(parent, viewType)\n\n override fun onBindViewHolder(holder: RecyclerView.ViewHolder, position: Int) {\n delegatesManager.onBindViewHolder(items, position, holder)\n }\n\n override fun getItemCount(): Int = items.count()\n\n fun setItems(newItems: List) {\n items = newItems\n }\n}\n\nThe main Adapter basically delegates all of its calls to the registered\nAdapterDelegates. If a suitable delegate is found then it will handle the item,\nin the case no appropriate delegate is found, a RunTimeException is thrown.\n\nSide note: Behind the scenes I used an additional abstraction for the\nAdapterDelegate superclass to make injections more readable:\n\nabstract class MatchAdapterDelegate : AdapterDelegate>()\n\n\n\nUsing Dagger 2 Multibinds to extend RecyclerView Adapter functionality\nExtending the solution from the previous section would require adding an extra\nconstructor parameter along with its addition to the delegatesManager property.\nTo make matters worse, it uses the concrete AdapterDelegate implementation\ninstead of an abstraction. This means that adding a basketball AdapterDelegate\nwould require a dependency on the basketball module for both the basic flavor\nand the premium flavor, even though the basic flavor will not use it.\n\nInjecting a common supertype (AdapterDelegate or the MatchAdapterDelegate\nmentioned earlier) and the help of Multibinds allows extending the main Adapter\nwithout the need to change the code inside it. The added bonus is that the basic\nflavor does not need a dependency on the basketball module.\n\nThe dagger module for adding the FootballMatchAdapterDelegate into the\ndependency graph:\n\n@Module\nabstract class FootballModule {\n\n @Binds\n @IntoSet\n internal abstract fun bindFootballMatchAdapterDelegate(\n footballMatchAdapterDelegate: FootballMatchAdapterDelegate\n ): MatchAdapterDelegate\n}\n\nThanks to the @IntoSet annotation the AdapterDelegate is not bound as a single\nclass in the dagger graph, but as Set. If that sounds\nconfusing I encourage you to read more about dagger multibindings in the\nofficial documentation [https://dagger.dev/dev-guide/multibindings.html].\n\nIn order for the Set to work correctly, a @Multibinds declaration needs to exist\nin a module that both flavors use:\n\n@Multibinds\nabstract fun multibindMatchAdapterDelegate(): Set\n\n\n\nAnd now instead of injecting a concrete implementation into the main Adapter,\nthe multibound Set can be injected instead:\n\nclass MatchListAdapter(\n matchAdapterDelegates: Set<@JvmSuppressWildcards MatchAdapterDelegate>\n) : RecyclerView.Adapter() {\n\t//...\n init {\n matchAdapterDelegates.forEach { delegate ->\n delegatesManager.addDelegate(delegate)\n }\n }\n //...\n}\n\nAdding another RecyclerView type now only requires creating an AdapterDelegate\nand adding it to the dagger dependency graph as a set. The @JvmSuppressWildcards \nis used because of the way that Kotlin defines Set. Sometimes without the\nannotation dagger won't recognize the generic type.\n\nExtending the main Adapter with the basketball type, only requires a dagger\ndeclaration with an @IntoSet annotation:\n\n@Binds\n@IntoSet\ninternal abstract fun bindBasketballMatchAdapterDelegate(\n\tbasketballMatchAdapterDelegate: BasketballMatchAdapterDelegate\n): MatchAdapterDelegate\n\n\n\nFlavor based custom implementation\nIn our project we commonly encounter a situation where we need to implement\ncustom behavior in one of the flavors, but the rest should remain as is. To\nallow this we use a function called customOrDefault:\n\nfun customOrDefault(\n defaultImplementation: Type, \n customImplementationsSet: Set\n): Type {\n require(customImplementationsSet.size <= 1) {\n \"Multiple (${customImplementationsSet.size}) custom implementations bound in $customImplementationsSet\"\n }\n return customImplementationsSet.firstOrNull() ?: defaultImplementation\n}\n\nThe basic idea is that if there is one custom implementation in the set then the\ncustom implementation is used, otherwise the default implementation is used.\n\nProviding the default implementation\nThe basic flavor only provides a list of football matches\n\nA list of only football matchesThese three items come from the default (basic\nflavor) implementation of the MatchesProvider interface:\n\ninterface MatchesProvider {\n\n fun get(): List\n}\n\n\nclass FootballMatchesProvider @Inject constructor() : MatchesProvider {\n\n override fun get(): List {\n return listOf(\n FootballMatch(\"Burnley\", \"Leicester\"),\n FootballMatch(\"Leipzig\", \"Wolfsburg\"),\n FootballMatch(\"Chelsea\", \"Atlético Madrid\"),\n )\n }\n}\n\n\n\nThis MatchesProvider interface is used to provide items to the main Adapter:\n\n@Inject\nlateinit var matchesProvider: MatchesProvider\n//...\n\tadapter.setItems(matchesProvider.get())\n//...\n\n\n\nThe MatchesProvider interface is introduced to the dagger dependency graph in\nthe following way:\n\n@Provides\nfun provideDefaultOrCustomMatchesProvider(\n\tdefault: FootballMatchesProvider,\n\tcustom: Set<@JvmSuppressWildcards MatchesProvider>\n): MatchesProvider =\n\tcustomOrDefault(default, custom)\n \n\nIf the Set does not exist in the dagger graph then the default\nFootballMatchesProvider is used. Because the basic flavor does not introduce any\nadditional MatchesProvider it uses the default implementation.\n\nSide note: This declaration resides in the main package of the app module,\nmeaning that both flavors use it.\n\nProviding a custom implementation\nThe basketball module provides a MatchesProvider for basketball matches:\n\nclass BasketballMatchesProvider @Inject constructor() : MatchesProvider {\n\n override fun get(): List {\n return listOf(\n BasketballMatch(\"Denver Nuggets\", \"Boston Celtics\"),\n BasketballMatch(\"Atlanta Hawks\", \"Charlotte Hornets\"),\n BasketballMatch(\"Orlando Magic\", \"Washington Wizards\"),\n )\n }\n}\n\nHowever binding this as a Set with @IntoSet would mean that this implementation\nis the custom one thus making the premium flavor only show basketball matches.\n\nTo overcome this issue an additional MatchesProvider is defined in the app\nmodule within the package of the premium flavor:\n\nclass ConcatenatedMatchesProvider @Inject constructor(\n private val footballMatchesProvider: FootballMatchesProvider,\n private val basketballMatchesProvider: BasketballMatchesProvider,\n): MatchesProvider {\n\n override fun get(): List {\n return footballMatchesProvider.get()\n .zip(basketballMatchesProvider.get()) { a, b ->\n listOf(a, b)\n }.flatten()\n }\n}\n\nIt basically merges the football and basketball matches together and returns the\nresult.  This implementation is bound in the following way:\n\n@Binds\n@IntoSet\nabstract fun bindMatchesProvider(\n\tconcatenatedMatchesProvider: ConcatenatedMatchesProvider\n): MatchesProvider\n\n\n\n\nBecause the ConcatenatedMatchesProvider implementation is bound as a set, it\nwill be used as the custom implementation in the customOrDefault function:\n\n@Provides\nfun provideDefaultOrCustomMatchesProvider(\n\tdefault: FootballMatchesProvider,\n\tcustom: Set<@JvmSuppressWildcards MatchesProvider>\n): MatchesProvider =\n\tcustomOrDefault(default, custom)\n \n\nThe list of matches from ConcatenatedMatchesProviderSummary\nDagger2 Multibindings add even more complexity to the dependency injection in\nAndroid, but they can be used to solve even more complex problems. The example\nproject for this article is available on GitHub\n[https://github.com/AKJAW/flavor-multibindings], I hope it can be used as\nlearning material for using Dagger 2 Multibindings in Android apps with multiple\nflavors.\n\nSome things we used Multibindings for:\n\n * New RecyclerView types\n * Additional screens (e.g. Authentication)\n * Analytics\n * Custom views\n\nIf you have any questions, or you want to share how you used Multibindings to\nsolve a complex problem please write about it below.\n\nMany thanks to Michał Olszak [https://www.linkedin.com/in/micha%C5%82-olszak/], \nArtur Poplawski [https://www.linkedin.com/in/apoplawski96/] and Piotr Sulej\n[https://www.linkedin.com/in/piotr-sulej-4a4958105/] for the help on this\nArticle.", "tags": [{"id":
