The problem gets even worse when you don't want to couple app flavors to features they don't use"}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform For Android and the Web - Part 1 Introduction and the Server", "custom_excerpt": "The first part of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. This part contains the project introduction and the Ktor server", "published_at": "2021-08-16T18:50:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "This article series contains parts of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. The\nthesis is based on my Fuller Stack project\n[https://github.com/AKJAW/fuller-stack-kotlin-multiplatform] which consists of\nan Android App, a Kotlin React.JS app and a Ktor server. The full thesis pdf can\nbe found here\n[https://akjaw.com/assets/blog/thesis-kotlin-multiplatform/Kotlin_Multiplatform.pdf]\n.\n\n> Part 1 Introduction and the Server (This part)\nPart 2 The shared module\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-2-the-shared-module/]\nPart 3 The Web App\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-3-the-web-app/]\nPart 4 The Android App\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-4-the-android-app/]\nPart 5 The Summary\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-5-the-summary/]\nThe application allows for taking and saving notes. The notes are persisted\nlocally and on the server, which allows for offline use. In order to use the app\nevery user has to be authenticated. Thanks to this authentication the notes can\nbe synchronized between multiple devices. When the user opens the app\nsimultaneously on different devices and has internet access, the synchronization\nhappens in real-time. This means that adding a new note on one devices is\nvisible on other devices after a short delay. There are also operations which do\nnot interact with the API or the database such as filtering, searching the notes\nand changing their date format representation.\n\nProject structure\nThe project is divided into 4 main modules:\n\nModule division with dependency arrowsThe React and Android module contain code\nfor the UI of their respective platforms (Web and Android) and all other\nplatforms specific code. Both of these modules depend on the Shared module,\nwhich is a Kotlin Multiplatform module. The shared module contains all of the\ndata structures used in this project along with logic for the shared app\nfeatures i.e. user input validation, database synchronization, networking, note\noperations.\n\nThe Ktor module contains the back-end (interchangeably called the server) that\nboth of the client apps (Android and React.JS) use. It has a dependency on the\nShared module but uses only the data structures from that module, no other logic\nis used. Unfortunately, even though this module does not need anything besides\nthe data structures, it still needs to compile the whole Shared module. The data\nstructures could have been duplicated between the Shared and Ktor module, which\ncould cause problems when either of them gets out of sync. The Shared dependency\nin the Ktor module guarantees that all of the modules have up to date data\nstructures.\n\nGitHub Actions\nGitHub Actions are a CI/CD (Continuous Integration / Continuous Delivery) tool\nthat is inte- grated into GitHub repositories. GitHub Actions use so called\nworkflows which could be used as CI, CD or both.\n\nContinuous Integration is a practice of making frequent and small code changes.\nThe workflows provided by GitHub Actions enable a way for automating the process\nof checking if the code is correct. These check usually consist of compiling the\ncode, running tests and other actions. Thanks to this automation any errors or\nbugs can be caught early into the development cycle.\n\nContinuous Delivery automates the deployment/release process. This project does\nnot use CD so it will not be discussed in details. This MSc projects GitHub\nActions have the following workflows:\n\n * Build all of the platforms in order to verify that there have been no\n breaking changes intro-\n duced;\n * Running tests for the Android app and the Shared code;\n * Check the coding standards with the help of static code analysis tools.\n\nThese workflows are run every time a code change occurs in the GitHub repository\nensuring that if something breaks it is caught early.\n\nStatic code analysis\nIt is an analysis which is performed without executing the code. Usually it is\nperformed by an automated tool. This MSc project uses two static analysis tools,\ni.e.:\n\n * Ktlint - checks the formatting of Kotlin code and notifies of any\n discrepancies in the coding standard. Additionally it has a built in\n formatter which can be used to automatically format Kotlin files;\n * Detekt - checks the complexity of the code, for example it detects long\n function names or large blocks of code and then suggests what can be done in\n order to reduce the complexity.\n\nDependency Injection\nIn Object Oriented Programming paradigm dependency injection is the act of\npassing dependencies (classes) to clients (other classes). Usually these\ndependencies contain behavior which is used to extend the capability of the\nclient. This results in using the composition over inheritance pattern. In\nbrief, instead of inheriting behavior it should be contained in separate classes\nwhich are used by the client. Dependency injection also divides the\nresponsibilities of classes, either a class creates other classes, or it\ncontains a behavior logic and/or a state. Without this separation classes would\nbe responsible for both of these things, which is contradictory to the single\nresponsibility principle. This principle says that a class should only have on\nresponsibility or just one reason to change.\n\nDependency Injection can be done manually by creating classes whose behavior\nconsist of creating other classes or it can be done by using\nframeworks/libraries. On Android the most popular framework for dependency\ninjection is Dagger 2. It eliminates the need for manually creating classes,\nbecause all of the creation classes are automatically generated during\ncompilation. Unfortunately, Dagger 2 is only available on the JVM, which\ndisqualifies it from this project because it reduces the capabilities of code\nsharing.\n\nThe cross-platform solution used in this project is Kodein. It is not a\ndependency injection framework but a service locator. It still creates the\ndependencies but in a different way. To put it simply, dependency injection\nframeworks give the dependencies and service locators provide dependencies when\nasked by a client.\n\nHere is the general idea of how dependency injection is done with Kodein:\n\nclass Database() {\n // ...\n}\n\nclass GetNotes(private val database: Database) {\n // ...\n}\n\nclass Client(private val getNotes: GetNotes) {\n // ...\n}\n\nval module = DI.Module(\"Module\") {\n bind() from singleton { Database() }\n bind() from singleton { GetNotes(instance()) }\n bind() from singleton { Client(instance()) }\n}\n\nThe module takes care of creating the classes and the GetNotes and Client \nclasses just use what is injected in the constructor. Most of the dependencies\ncan be injected through the constructor like in the example above, but sometimes\nthe developer is not in charge of creating the classes e.g. an Android screen\nwhich is created by the system. In these cases the injection cannot happen in\nthe constructor so it happens in the class properties by calling a Kodein\nfunction:\n\nclass Screen : DIAware {\n private val getNotes: GetNotes by instance()\n}\n\nThe DIAware interface enables the use of Kodein in the Screen class. Dependency\ninjection happens through the instance function which searches through the\ndependency graph until it finds the correct class and provides it to the Screen \nclass.\n\nKtor server\nThe server enables the apps to be fully cross-platform, as a user can use\nmultiple devices without losing access to their data. The Ktor server was build\nfor the JVM environment meaning that it leverages Kotlin/JVM.\n\nPostgreSQL Database\nThis database, later called a remote database, contains the users notes. The\nnotes kept in this database are a reflection of the notes stored in the\napplications local database. When a note is added locally it is also added\nremotely to this database etc. This allows the user to change devices and have\nup to date notes in their applications.\n\nNote Schema\nThis data structure is used for the remote database and also for the network\ncalls in both of the apps. These are the most important fields:\n\n * Title - the title of the note;\n * Content - the body of the note;\n * LastModificationTimestamp - the unix timestamp of last modification. This is\n used for comparing which version of the note should be kept during\n synchronization. The newer LastModificationTimestamp always overwrites the\n older one;\n * CreationTimestamp - the unix timestamp of the creation time. This is used as\n the unique note identifier when synchornizing the notes locally. Normally\n this kind of an identifier is discouraged but for this project it will\n suffice;\n * WasDeleted - a flag which indicates if the note was soft-deleted. The note\n still exists in the remote database but from the user's perspective it is\n deleted because they cannot see it.\n\nAdditionally, the remote database also has a field used for user identification.\n\nAuthentication\nAuthentication is handled by a third-party platform called Auth0. The platform\nprovides integration for multiple platforms including Android and React.JS.\nDelegating user authentication to a third-party means that the developer has\nfewer things to be concerned about like developing their own integration and\nsecurity concerns.\n\nAuth0 in the client apps allow the user to sign in using their email or Google\naccount. The authentication flow is as follows for both platforms:\n\n * User clicks the sign-in button in the app.\n * A new website is open (redirected in case of the web app). This website\n allows the user to input their credentials and sign into the app.\n * If the credentials are correct, the user is authenticated and redirected back\n to the app.\n\nThis whole flow is handled by Auth0, which means that the Ktor back-end is not\ninvolved. However, every call to the back-end after the authentication requires\na subsequent authentication because every note is tied to a user and the server\nneeds to differentiate between users notes.\n\nWhen the apps are sending requests to the back-end they also send a JSON Web\nToken (JWT) which is used by the server to obtain the user ID. This token is\nprovided by the Auth0 libraries used on the client side. JWTs are widely used\nfor authentication. Ktor provides an extra package for their support which means\nit does not need Auth0 or  any other third-party dependencies for user\nauthentication.\n\nWebsocket\nWebsockets are a computer communications protocol allowing two-way communication\nbetween the client and the server. This protocol is used to give the user\nreal-time updates about their notes. Ktor provides an additional package to\ncreate websocket sessions.\n\nClient \nEvery client app at a startup connects to the socket endpoint and sends the\nuser's ID in order to identify and send updates to the correct user. In addition\nto the user ID, cookies are used to differentiate users sessions. As an example,\nif a user opens two browser windows, changes from one window are sent to the\nother window and the same occurs for other devices.\n\nServer\nThe back-end listens to socket connections and when a connection is established,\nthe client sends the user identifier which is the only thing the client sends\nthrough the websocket. If the user is valid the connection is saved along with\nthe session. After the client makes a change in the notes, the websocket\nnotifies the clients but only the sessions which did not make the change. This\nprevents  unnecessary data transmission because the source of the changes does\nnot need to be notified of them.\n\nTechnologies used\n * Ktor with (Server core, Server netty, Auth JWT, Websockets, Serialization)\n * Kodein - cross-platform dependency injection;\n * Klock - cross-platform Date and time library;\n * Kotlin coroutines - enables coroutines support in Kotlin;\n * Exposed - allows for the use of databases;\n * PostgreSQL JDBC Driver - enables the server to connect to a PostgreSQL\n database;\n * H2 Database - database engine used for running an in-memory database for\n development purposes.\n\nHosting\nBack-end hosting is provided by Heroku which is a platform for building and\ndeploying applications. It supports multiple programming languages and\nframeworks, including Java and Kotlin applications such as Ktor. Besides the\nback-end Heroku also hosts the remote PostgreSQL database.\n\nHeroku has a great documentation and a big community which makes finding help\neasier. It also offers a generous free plan which is perfect for side-projects\nor university projects like the one for this MSc thesis. The only requirement is\nan account which can be created without any payment.\n\nSource code\nThe source code for the Ktor server can be found in the project repository\n[https://github.com/AKJAW/fuller-stack-kotlin-multiplatform/tree/master/ktor].\nKeep in mind that I don't dabble with back-end development professionally so the\ncode probably doesn't comply to best practises.", "tags": [{"id": "610422a85e5e944f4dab9a01", "name": "Multiplatform", "slug": "multiplatform", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#7b4fff", "url": "/tag/multiplatform/"}, {"id": "6112cab25e5e944f4dab9b3a", "name": "Ktor", "slug": "ktor", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": null, "url": "/tag/ktor/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-1/", "excerpt": "The first part of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. This part contains the project introduction and the Ktor server"}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform For Android and the Web - Part 3 The Web App", "custom_excerpt": "This part explores Kotlin/JS and shows the React.JS app of the Kotlin Multiplatform project.", "published_at": "2021-08-30T05:25:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "This article series contains parts of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. The\nthesis is based on my Fuller Stack project\n[https://github.com/AKJAW/fuller-stack-kotlin-multiplatform] which consists of\nan Android App, a Kotlin React.JS app and a Ktor server. The full thesis pdf can\nbe found here\n[https://akjaw.com/assets/blog/thesis-kotlin-multiplatform/Kotlin_Multiplatform.pdf]\n.\n\n> Part 1 Introduction and the Server\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-1/]\nPart 2 The shared module\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-2-the-shared-module/]\nPart 3 The Web App (This part)\nPart 4 The Android App\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-4-the-android-app/]\nPart 5 The Summary\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-5-the-summary/]\nThe React.JS app is fully written in Kotlin thanks to Kotlin.JS\n[https://kotlinlang.org/docs/js-overview.html] and the React.JS Kotlin wrapper\n[https://github.com/JetBrains/kotlin-wrappers].\n\nThe app\nSign in screen - the only screen an unauthenticated user sees. Clicking the\nbutton navigates the user to the Auth0 sign in pageThe Auth0 signing screen -\nthe user can sign in using a Google account or email and passwordHome screen -\nthe main screen of the app. It features the notes list and a note editor which\ncan be opened and closedSettings screen - this screen allows the user to sign\nout or change their date formatting preferences.Kotlin/JS\nBesides the Kotlin/JS standard library, Kotlin leverages the existing JavaScript\necosystem and allows for the use of its libraries. A lot of popular libraries\nhave a corresponding \"Kotlin wrapper\" which allows for a type safe and easier\nuse from Kotlin. JetBrains (with the help of the community) also provides a set\nof wrappers. These wrappers i.a. include React.JS with helper libraries and\nRedux, both of which are used in this project.\n\nCalling JavaScript from Kotlin\nGiven the alert function in JavaScript which shows a pop-up with a message, one\nway of calling this function in Kotlin is inlining JavaScript:\n\nfun alert(message: String?) {\n return js(\"alert(message)\")\n}\n\nOne thing to note here is that the js function returns a dynamic type. Kotlin is\na statically typed language but JavaScript is not and does not have any concept\nof types. This difference requires Kotlin to support cases where the type is\nunknown or just unexpected and this is what the dynamic type is used for. This\ndynamic type basically disables Kotlins type checker, which in hand removes\ncompile-time safety. For example: JavaScript offers a function for calculating\nthe minimum of the numbers. In Kotlin it could look like:\n\nfun min(a: Int, b: Int): dynamic {\n return js(\"Math.max(a, b)\")\n}\n\nIn order to use the result of the min function this result needs to be casted\nfrom a dynamic type to a number type. This cast can only happen at run-time,\nwhich prevents the compiler from failing if there is a problem with  types.\n\nAnother problem with inlining JavaScript is the fact that the execution is also\nnot compile-time safe. In the alert example above, changing the message \nparameter to text is still a valid Kotlin code which compiles even if the string\ninside the js function remains the same. At run-time, however, the application\nwould not work as expected and executing the alert function would result in an\nerror: \"Uncaught ReferenceError: message is not defined\". This is because Kotlin\nexecutes the JavaScript just as it is defined in the js function but the message \nvariable does not exist anymore because it was replaced with text in Kotlin.\n\nThe safer option is to define a contract using the external keyword:\n\nexternal fun alert(message: String?)\n\nThis contract assumes that the alert function is declared externally in the\nJavaScript world. This more or less suffers from the same dynamic problem as\ninlining JavaScript, but it leverages the Kotlin type system more. Changing the\nparameter names does not break the application at run-time.\n\nUsing JavaScript libraries\nAs it was mentioned before, Kotlin leverages the JavaScript ecosystem and a lot\nof Kotlin wrappers are provided for popular JavaScript libraries. These wrappers\nallow Kotlin to call those libraries out of the box. Unfortunately, the less\npopular libraries do not have such wrappers. In such case there is a possibility\nto write these wrappers by hand using the external keyword mentioned in the\nprevious section. Doing this by hand can be tedious and error prone, fortunately\nthere is an experimental solution for this problem called Dukat. This tool uses\nexisting TypeScript declaration files in order to generate matching Kotlin\nexternal definitions.\n\nGiven a TypeScript declaration file like follows:\n\nexport function doSomething(): number;\n\nGenerating a Kotlin file with Dukat gives a result similar to this:\n\nimport kotlin.js.*\nimport org.khronos.webgl.*\nimport org.w3c.dom.*\nimport org.w3c.dom.events.*\nimport org.w3c.dom.parsing.*\nimport org.w3c.dom.svg.*\nimport org.w3c.dom.url.*\nimport org.w3c.fetch.*\nimport org.w3c.files.*\nimport org.w3c.notifications.*\nimport org.w3c.performance.*\nimport org.w3c.workers.*\nimport org.w3c.xhr.*\n\nexternal fun doSomething(): Number\n\nDukat adds a lot of additional imports which are not always needed. Fortunately,\nthe generated Kotlin files can be modified to contain only what is needed. \n\nIn this project this tool has been used to generate Kotlin files for two\nlibraries, one is for authentication and the other is for a database.\n\nKotlin React.JS\nThis section will show some JavaScript React.JS examples and how they can be\nachieved in Kotlin.\n\nThe first example: An input for typing with a buttonclass TextInputExample extends React.Component {\n render() {\n return (\n
\n {\n const newVal = event.target.value;\n console.log(newVal);\n }}\n />\n \n
\n );\n }\n}\n\nReactDOM.render(, document.getElementById(\"root\"));\n\nReact.JS uses JSX which is an extenstion for JavaScript and allows for writing\ncode in an HTML-like syntax. In the example above TextInputExample is a class\nreact component and it is used just as other HTML elements in React.JS (line\n20).\n\nclass TextInputExample : RComponent() {\n override fun RBuilder.render() {\n input(InputType.text) {\n attrs.onChangeFunction = { event ->\n val newVal = (event.target as HTMLInputElement).value\n console.log(newVal)\n }\n }\n button {\n + \"A button\"\n attrs {\n onClickFunction = { console.log(\"Button clicked\") }\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\nfun main() {\n window.onload = {\n render(document.getElementById(\"root\")) {\n child(TextInputExample::class)\n }\n }\n}\n\nThe Kotlin wrapper for React.JS does not use JSX but it uses a custom DSL for\nit. Instead of using HTML-like tags, Kotlin uses predefined functions which\ncorrespond to a given HTML tag. Since using React components differs from the\nJSX usage, an additional child function (line 21) needs to be used in order to\nplace the React component.\n\nThe second example does not use a class component but a functional component\nwhich has better performance and involves less boilerplate code. Additionally,\nthe component uses props and state. Props could be thought of arguments which\nare passed in from the parent component. When a prop value changes, the\ncomponent is re-rendered in order to reflect the new value. While props are\npassed in from the parent, the state is self contained in the component and\noptionally passed to its children components. Both props and state changes cause\nthe component to re-render.\n\nThe second example: A text label which value is based on a passed in prop and a\ncheckbox which value is based on the components statefunction CheckBoxExample(props) {\n const [isChecked, setIsChecked] = useState(false);\n\n return (\n \n );\n}\n\nReactDOM.render(\n ,\n document.getElementById(\"root\")\n);\n\ninterface ExampleProps : RProps {\n var name: String\n}\n\nval checkboxExample = functionalComponent { props ->\n val (isChecked, setIsChecked) = useState(false)\n\n div {\n label {\n + props.name\n input(InputType.checkBox) {\n attrs.checked = isChecked\n attrs.onChangeFunction = {\n setIsChecked(!isChecked)\n }\n }\n\n }\n }\n}\n\nfun main() {\n window.onload = {\n render(document.getElementById(\"root\")) {\n child(checkboxExample) {\n attrs.name = \"React.JS\"\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\nBecause Kotlin is a static language, the props contract is defined in the form\nof an interface (line 1). Both JSX and Kotlin allow for easy extensions in the\nform of extracting existing logic into standalone components. But I personally\nthink that modifying and creating functions in Kotlin is easier and safer than\nin JSX thanks to types and a less verbose syntax.\n\nModules\nThe main web app module consist of the React module along with its dependency\nmodules.\n\n * Authentication - provides user authentication functionality using the Auth0\n library;\n * Persistence - provides database access thanks to the Dexie.js library;\n * Shared - provides shared functionality.\n\nBoth the libraries for authentication and persistence were generated using\nDukat.\n\nArchitecture\nRedux\nRedux is not an integral part of the whole project, so it will be briefly\nexplained with a simple checkbox example.\n\nThe state of the app resides in a single state container branch (i.e a branch\ncould relate to a screen or a feature) in this case App state. This state is\nonly changed by the reducer, which is invoked when an action is dispatched.\nBecause React.JS components do not ask for state but only react to state changes\nin combination with Redux this creates a unidirectional data flow:\n\nView layer\nThe view layer is responsible for showing the user interface and reacting to\nuser input. In React.JS the view is composed from components which could be\nthought of as small building bricks which are joined together in order to create\nsomething bigger like the notes list:\n\nThe whole notes list consists of an action row component and a number of note\nitems. The Action row is composed from a button component and two icon \ncomponents.\n\nSome components hold their own state and some are derived from Redux. Usually\nthings that relate to outside sources like the users notes are held in the Redux\nstate. \n\nPresentation layer\nThe presentation layer mainly revolves around Redux, since it is an integral\npart of almost every main application part like the notes list or the note\neditor. The Redux usage was inspired by Redux-toolkit and its convention of\nusing \"slices\".\n\nSlices can be thought of containers for related Redux classes and functions. The\nmain building blocks of Redux are actions, reducers and states. Slices are the\nclasses which hold these main building blocks. Referring back to the checkbox\nexample, here's the Kotlin Redux code which could be used for this:\n\nobject CheckboxSlice {\n data class State(\n val checkboxValue: Boolean = false\n )\n\n data class SetCheckboxValue(val newValue: Boolean) : RAction\n\n fun reducer(state: State = State(), action: RAction): State {\n return when (action) {\n is SetCheckboxValue -> {\n state.copy(checkboxValue = action.newValue)\n }\n else -> state\n }\n }\n}\n\nThis slice class contains the classes for the state, actions and a reducer\nfunction which calculates the new state.\n\nFor asynchronous actions like reaching out to the database, Redux thunk is used.\nThe basic idea is such \"Thunks\"  provide a callback function which can be\ninvoked after an asynchronous task ends. This callback takes in an action which\nis sent to the reducer in order to modify the state.\n\nSummary\nWith all of this in mind the web app architecture for the View and Presentation\nlayer can be summed like this:\n\nThe React.JS components dispatch actions usually as a result of a user input.\nThese actions are sent to a reducer function which changes the state. Because\nReact.JS components are reactive, any relevant state changes cause the\ncomponents to re-render based on the new state.\n\nTechnologies used\nMost of these libraries make use of Kotlin wrappers, but I have omitted the ones\nwhich come from the official JetBrains repository.\n\nLibraries related to UI:\n\n * React.JS - allows for composing the user interface using small self contained\n components;\n * Styled components - provides easier styling for React.JS components;\n * Material UI - provides predefined React.JS components which adhere to\n material designs;\n * Muirwik - the Kotlin wrapper for Material UI.\n\nLibraries related to logic:\n\n * React router - provides declarative navigation to React.JS apps;\n * Redux - a state container for JavaScript apps;\n * Redux thunk - an extension for Redux which allows for asynchronous actions;\n * Auth0 - allows for an easy integration of authentication in React.JS;\n * Kotlin Coroutines - provides asynchronous programming features to the Kotlin\n language. It is used in the React presentation layer;\n * Klock - library used for operating with notes timestamps and formatting them\n to dates on the notes list;\n * Kotlin Serialization - allows for an easy JSON serialization for API requests\n and responses;\n * Kodein - allows for injecting dependencies and retrieving them from the react\n module;\n * Dexie - the JavaScript library for the database. The Kotlin files were\n generated using Dukat.", "tags": [{"id": "610422a85e5e944f4dab9a01", "name": "Multiplatform", "slug": "multiplatform", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#7b4fff", "url": "/tag/multiplatform/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7484", "name": "React.JS", "slug": "react-js", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": null, "url": "/tag/react-js/"}, {"id": "60428caf1df95a30f3de7483", "name": "Kotlin", "slug": "kotlin", "description": null, "feature_image": null, "visibility": "public", "og_image": null, "og_title": null, "og_description": null, "twitter_image": null, "twitter_title": null, "twitter_description": null, "meta_title": null, "meta_description": null, "codeinjection_head": null, "codeinjection_foot": null, "canonical_url": null, "accent_color": "#e38fff", "url": "/tag/kotlin/"}], "url": "/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-3-the-web-app/", "excerpt": "This part explores Kotlin/JS and shows the React.JS app of the Kotlin Multiplatform project."}, {"title": "Kotlin Multiplatform For Android and the Web - Part 4 The Android App", "custom_excerpt": "This part focuses on the Android app of the Kotlin Multiplatform project", "published_at": "2021-09-06T17:18:03.000+00:00", "plaintext": "This article series contains parts of my thesis about Kotlin Multiplatform. The\nthesis is based on my Fuller Stack project\n[https://github.com/AKJAW/fuller-stack-kotlin-multiplatform] which consists of\nan Android App, a Kotlin React.JS app and a Ktor server. The full thesis pdf can\nbe found here\n[https://akjaw.com/assets/blog/thesis-kotlin-multiplatform/Kotlin_Multiplatform.pdf]\n.\n\n> Part 1 Introduction and the Server\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-1/]\nPart 2 The shared module\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-2-the-shared-module/]\nPart 3 The Web App\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-3-the-web-app/]\nPart 4 The Android App (This part)\nPart 5 The Summary\n[https://akjaw.com/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-5-the-summary/]\nOriginally this part was supposed to be the last part of the article series,\nhowever the resulting article was just to big so is split it into two parts.\n\nThe app\nAndroid app authentication screenThe Auth0 website used for authentication in\nthe Android appThe main screen of the Android appThe Android screen for editing\nnotesThe user profile screen for the Android AppThe settings screen for the\nAndroid AppKotlin/JVM\nKotlin started out as a language for the Java Virtual Machine. This means that\nKotlin targeting the JVM is the most used and refined part of the Kotlin\necosystem. Kotlin offers 100% interoperability with Java. Consequently, Kotlin\ncan be introduced gradually to a Java codebase.\n\nKotlin/JVM can be used for building applications in every environment that Java\ncan be used in, which includes:\n\n * Desktop applications;\n * Android apps;\n * Servers.\n\nIn this MSc project Kotlin/JVM is used on the Ktor server and in the Android\napp.\n\nModules\nThe main Android module uses three sub-modules:\n\n * Shared - provides shared functionality;\n * Authentication - provides user authentication functionality using the Auth0\n library;\n * Framework - contains shared Android specific classes and interfaces for the\n main and Authentication sub-module.\n\nArchitecture\nModel-View-Viewmodel\nThe Android architecture in this MSc project makes use of the official Android\nArchitecture Components which recommend using the MVVM (Model-View-ViewModel)\narchitecture. The Model in MVVM should represent business logic which in this\nproject resides in the domain layer in the form of use cases discussed before.\n\nView layer\nWithout going into details, this MSc project uses Android Fragments as the View.\nFragments are reusable portions of the UI but in this project they always\nrepresent a single screen.\n\nAndroid app mock-up with three screensAll three screens are represented by a\nfragment and the bottom buttons change which fragment is shown. The Android app\nfor this MSc works in the same way.\n\nBriefly speaking fragments are  responsible for showing the UI and reacting to a\nuser input. Sometimes they do more than that, but I will omit such discussion to\nkeep the explanation concise. In this MSc project they work in the same way that\nReact.JS components do. They react on data changes and send user events to the\npresentation layer.\n\nPresentation layer\nThe ViewModel is the heart of the presentation layer and is a mediator between\nthe View and the Domain layer.\n\nThe data for the View is exposed through so called LiveData which basically is\nan implementation of the Observer pattern. It allows the View to listen to\nLiveData data changes but it is only emitting changes if the fragment is in\ncorrect state (i.e. the fragment is shown and not closing).\n\nUser interaction usually revolves around calling ViewModel functions based on\nthe View callbacks. Most of the time the ViewModel functions reach out to the\nDomain layer and then update the corresponding LiveData which updates the UI.\n\nOn more complicated screens like the notes list or the note editor all of the UI\nrelated data are encapsulated in a single class. Thanks to this the View is only\nconcerned with one LiveData which holds all the necessary View data. This\npattern is often called \"Single View States\". These Single View states are\neither represented by data classes or sealed classes:\n\ndata class NoteEditorState(\n val note: ParcelableNote? = null,\n val titleError: String? = null\n)\n\nIn this case note holds the current user input in the editor and titleError \nholds the error message in case the user gives an incorrect title. Both of these\nstates can happen simultaneously and that is why a data class is used for this\nstate. \n\nsealed class NotesListState {\n object Loading : NotesListState()\n data class ShowingList(val notes: List) : NotesListState()\n}\n\nIn this case the the state can be in one of two states, either loading or\nshowing a list. Both of the states cannot be shown at the same time, and that is\nwhy a sealed class was used for this state.\n\nArchitecture summary\nThe Android app architecture for the View and Presentation layersTesting\nThe Android module only contains unit tests for the presentation layer and most\nof it are tests for ViewModels. The framework used for writing these tests is\ncalled JUnit which is the standard for writing JVM tests. The particular version\nused is JUnit 5.\n\nBecause the shared module already contains fakes for the database and the API,\nthe Android module makes use of them. This means that the UseCases in the\nViewModels are not mocked, their real implementation is used but in place of the\ndatabase and the API a fake is used. Using real implementations in unit tests\ninstead of test doubles could be perceived as integration tests.\n\nTechnologies used\nA lot of libraries coming from the androidx package were omitted in order not to\nmake this list too exhaustive. The list below contains the most notable ones:\n\n * Android ViewModel - the official implementation of the ViewModel in Android;\n * Android LiveData - an observer wrappper which is aware of the Android life\n cycle;\n * Room  - persistence library;\n * Auth0  - provides an easy authentication integration in Android;\n * Simple Stack - a library for navigation between screens;\n * Retrofit  - a type safe HTTP client;\n * OkHttp  - a networking library;\n * Coil  - provides fast image loading;\n * Kotlin Coroutines  - allows for using asynchronous programming\n * Kotlin features. It is mostly used in the Android presentation layer.;\n * Klock  - library used for formatting the notes timestamps;\n * Kotlin Serialization  - allows for retrofit to automatically serialize\n * JSON responses and requests;\n * Kodein  - provides dependency injection from the Android module.\n\nTesting libraries:\n\n * JUnit5  - a testing framework for the JVM;\n * Mockk  - allows for mocking in Kotlin using a DSL;\n * Coroutines test - contains utilities for easier coroutines testing.", "tags": [{"id": "610422a85e5e944f4dab9a01", "name": "Multiplatform", "slug": "multiplatform", "/kotlin-multiplatform-for-android-and-the-web-part-4-the-android-app/", "excerpt": "This part focuses on the Android app of the Kotlin Multiplatform project"}, {"title": "Deploying a Gatsby website to an FTP server with CircleCi", "custom_excerpt": "How I implemented a CI/CD workflow to deploy a Gatsby website hosted on a server with FTP access", "published_at": "2020-04-19T18:52:00.000+00:00", "plaintext": "Preface\nThe last version of my website was made with Gatsby [https://www.gatsbyjs.org/].\nDeploying a Gatsby website is as easy as running one command (gatsby build) and\nplacing the created folder on a server.\n\nMy current hosting service gives me access to my server through FTP and SSH.\nUnfortunately I do not have access to the sudo command which means that I can’t\nInstall anything from the command line. This limitation forces me to upload a\nnew version of my site with the use of FTP. By trail and error I learned to\nalways save the previous version of my site if I ever needed a rollback.\n\nAs you can guess, my site deployment strategy requires a lot of manual steps:\n\n\n1. Build the site with **gatsby build**\n2. Connect to the FTP server\n3. Upload the new build\n4. Rename the current production for backup\n5. Make the uploaded build the new production\n\n\nIt might not seem like a lot, but it will add up fast. I thought about\nautomating this process, the official Gatsby documentation\n[https://www.gatsbyjs.org/docs/deploying-and-hosting/]lists a lot of option, but\nnone suited my needs, because I wanted to keep my current hosting service\nwithout moving my domain.\n\nI tried out Netlify [https://www.netlify.com/]with the idea of doing a reverse\nproxy [https://www.nginx.com/resources/glossary/reverse-proxy-server/], but I\nsoon realized that I lack the experience needed for this, and found out that\nNetlify doesn’t really support\n[https://community.netlify.com/t/trying-to-set-up-a-nginx-reverse-proxy-fails/4542/2]\nthis type of proxy.\n\nAfter some more digging around I stumbled upon the idea of using a CI/CD\npipeline for automatic build deployment.\n\nCircleCi\nCircleCi [https://circleci.com/] is a continuous integration and delivery\nservice which can be hosted in the cloud for free. I didn't want to host my own\nCI/CD because of the maintenance and additional manual work.\n\nAfter connecting my repository to CircleCI and providing the starter config, I\nalso needed to set 3 environment variables to my project. The FTP address, \nusername and password. These variables are encrypted and only visible from\ninside the CircleCi workflow.\n\nCircleCi listens to repository changes and runs the workflow based on the config\nfile placed in the repository. This means that to change the config there has to\nbe a new commit that to triggers the workflow start. Because of this, testing it\na little cumbersome, to avoid unnecessary commits I had to resort to a lot of\nforced commits.\n\nHere’s the latest and I hope the final version of the config:\n\nversion: 2.1\norbs:\n node: circleci/node@2.0.2\n\njobs:\n build-deploy:\n executor:\n name: node/default\n tag: '12.16.1'\n steps:\n - checkout\n - node/install-packages\n - run: npm run build\n - run:\n name: Deploying\n command: node ./ftp-deploy.js $FTP_ADDRESS $FTP_USER $FTP_PASSWORD\n\nworkflows:\n version: 2\n main:\n jobs:\n - build-deploy:\n filters:\n branches:\n only: master\n\nWithout delving into a lot of details, the build-deploy job is only called on\nnew commits in the master branch which means deployment only happens on that\nbranch. The executor checkouts to the master branch and installs all required\nnode packages with node/install-packages. After that it builds the project and\nruns the ftp-deploy.js with the 3 environment variables passed in as command\nline arguments. This allows the script to connect to the FTP server without\nhard-coding any credentials.\n\nThis is my first time using such a service, so my solution is probably far from\nperfect, but as of now I’m happy with it.\n\nNode.js\nThe ftp-deploy.js is the star of the show, it is responsible for all the logic\nrelated to the deployment. The script is a little complicated, but I will try to\nsplit it into digestible slices. If you want to see the whole script click here\n\nStarting from the top of the source file, here are the dependencies and\nconstants:\n\nconst FtpDeploy = require(\"ftp-deploy\")\nconst ftp = require(\"basic-ftp\")\nconst basicFtpClient = new ftp.Client()\nconst https = require('https');\n\nconst LOCAL_BUILD_DIRECTORY = \"public\"\nconst DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME = \"temp\"\nconst PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME = \"master\"\nconst BROKEN_BUILD = \"broken\"\nconst PRODUCTION_URL = \"https://akjaw.com/\"\n\nconst [ host, user, password ] = process.argv.slice(2)\n\ndependencies\n * ftp-deploy is used for uploading the files to the FTP server. I tried to do\n it with other libraries, but this one offered me the most flexibility for\n this particular task.\n * basic-ftp is used for listing renaming directories on the server, take a note\n that a global instance of its client is created.\n * https is for checking if my website up (200 OK) after the deployment.\n\nconstants\n * LOCAL_BUILD_DIRECTORY - directory that is sent to the server.\n * DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME - name of the uploaded directory on the server.\n * PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME - directory for the live site.\n * BROKEN_BUILD - directory name for a build which doesn’t work.\n * PRODUCTION_URL - the url for my site.\n\nhost, user, password correspond to the environment variables passed in through\nthe command line\n\nHere’s the function that directs the deployment flow:\n\nfunction main() {\n return uploadBuildDirectory()\n .catch(error => onError(\"Upload\", error))\n .then(() => renameFtpDirectories())\n .catch(error => onError(\"Rename\", error))\n .then(async backupDirectoryName => {\n const isLive = await isProductionLive()\n if(!isLive) return rollBackProduction(backupDirectoryName)\n })\n .catch(error => onError(\"Rollback\", error))\n .then(() => {\n console.log(\"Deployment FINISHED\")\n basicFtpClient.close()\n process.exit(0)\n })\n}\n\nAfter every deployment step there is a catch block which stops the execution of\nthe script, because a partially uploaded build or badly renamed folders can\nbreak the live site. Along with the script exit, the global ftp resource is also\ncleared. I’m not sure if this is necessary, but it’s better to be safe than\nsorry.\n\nfunction onError(name, error){\n console.log(`${name} FAILED`)\n console.log(error)\n fail()\n}\n\nfunction fail() {\n basicFtpClient.close()\n process.exit(1)\n}\n\nprocess.exit takes in a number parameter which refers to the exit code. An exit\ncode of 0 means that everything went fine, but a positive number means an error.\nIn this case an exit code of 1 is an Uncaught Fatal Exception.\n[https://node.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api/process/]\n\nHere’s the break-down of the script flow without any errors:\n\n\n1. Upload the build directory to the FTP server\n2. Rename the current production directory and then the uploaded directory, at the end return the name of the old renamed production directory \n3. Check if the new build works on production, if not, then rollback the changes by reversing the second step\n4. Finish the deployment by closing any resources and exiting with a normal exit code.\n\n\nUploading the build directory\nfunction uploadBuildDirectory(){\n const config = {\n host,\n user,\n password,\n localRoot: `./${LOCAL_BUILD_DIRECTORY}`,\n remoteRoot: `./${DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME}`,\n include: [\"*\", \"**/*\"]\n }\n\n const ftpDeploy = new FtpDeploy()\n\n ftpDeploy.on(\"uploading\", data => {\n const { totalFilesCount, transferredFileCount, filename } = data\n console.log(`${transferredFileCount} out of ${totalFilesCount} ${filename}`)\n })\n\n ftpDeploy.on(\"upload-error\", data => {\n console.log(data.err)\n throw new Error(\"Upload FAILED\")\n })\n\n return ftpDeploy\n .deploy(config)\n .then(() => console.log(`Upload COMPLETED`))\n}\n\nAll the heavy lifting is done by ftp-deploy, I only specify the credentials,\ndirectories and log some information about the files during the upload. Even If\none file is not uploaded correctly it will probably break the site, so an error\nis thrown to stop the deployment.\n\nRenaming both of the directories\nasync function renameFtpDirectories(){\n await basicFtpClient.access({ host, user, password })\n\n const directories = await basicFtpClient.list(\".\")\n const backupDirectoryName = createBackupDirectoryName(directories)\n console.log(`Backup folder name: ${backupDirectoryName}`)\n\n await basicFtpClient.rename(PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME, backupDirectoryName)\n await basicFtpClient.rename(DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME, PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME)\n console.log(\"Renaming COMPLETED\")\n\n return backupDirectoryName\n}\n\nThis function uses the global basicFtpClient, which is closed on any failure\nduring the deployment flow. The only reason I made it global is to not\novercomplicate the code and pass it as a parameter everywhere.\n\nOn my hosting, I keep the backups of all previous site versions, their directory\nname refers to their upload date in this format “YYYY.MM.DD”. But what happens\nif there is already a directory with that name? createBackupDirectoryName solves\nthis issue:\n\nfunction createBackupDirectoryName(directories) {\n const productionDirectory = directories\n .find(fileInfo => fileInfo.name === PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME)\n\n const formattedDate = formatDate(productionDirectory.modifiedAt)\n\n const numberOfDateOccurrences = directories\n .filter(fileInfo => fileInfo.name.includes(formattedDate))\n .length\n\n if(numberOfDateOccurrences === 0){\n return formattedDate\n } else {\n return `${formattedDate}_${numberOfDateOccurrences + 1}`\n }\n}\n\nfunction formatDate(date) {\n const month = date.getMonth() + 1\n const paddedMonth = month.toString().padStart(2, \"0\")\n const paddedDay = date.getDate().toString().padStart(2, \"0\")\n\n return `${date.getFullYear()}.${paddedMonth}.${paddedDay}`\n}\n\nThis function gets a list of all the directories on the FTP server as a\nparameter. To correctly make a backup of the production directory  I need its\nmodification date. I use the find function to iterate the list and find the\nproduction directory, the modification date is then formatted to the correct\nformat. A simple glimpse in the formatDate shows how intuitive it is to work\nwith dates in plain JavaScript :). Once I have the correct directory name I need\nto check if there are already some directories with the same name/date. If this\nis the case, I add a postfix as to not overwrite anything.\n\nThe rest is as easy as swapping the production directory and returning the \nbackupDirectoryName for the possible rollback:\n\nawait basicFtpClient.rename(PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME, backupDirectoryName)\nawait basicFtpClient.rename(DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME, PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME)\nconsole.log(\"Renaming COMPLETED\")\n\nreturn backupDirectoryName\n\nChecking the production website\nJust to be sure everything went as planned, I check if my site is live:\n\n...\n.then(async backupDirectoryName => {\n const isLive = await isProductionLive()\n if(!isLive) return rollBackProduction(backupDirectoryName)\n})\n...\n\nfunction isProductionLive() {\n return new Promise((resolve) => {\n https\n .get(PRODUCTION_URL, res => {\n console.log(`Production status code ${res.statusCode}`)\n resolve(res.statusCode === 200)\n })\n .on(\"error\", _ => {\n resolve(false)\n })\n })\n}\n\nasync function rollBackProduction(backupDirectoryName){\n await basicFtpClient.rename(PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME, BROKEN_BUILD)\n await basicFtpClient.rename(backupDirectoryName, PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME)\n console.log(`Rollback COMPLETED`)\n fail()\n}\n\nI simply connect to my website and check the status code\n[https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Status]it returns, if there\nare no errors it should be 200 OK. I know this is far from perfect, but at least\nit lets me sleep at night. If the website returns a different status code it\nmight mean my server is down or something is wrong with the build. In either\ncase I roll back the build by simply renaming the directories again. I also call\nthe fail method to notify CircleCi that something went wrong.\n\nConclusion\nI know that my way of deployment is far from perfect, and that there are\nprobably better ways of doing it. Nonetheless, I’m happy with the result and the\nexperience I gained along the way. The first version of this post will be\nuploaded with CircleCi, then I will provide some screenshots from the panel.\n\nScript logs in CircleCiCircleCi workflowAs you can see this workflow takes quite\na bit of time, but I don’t think I will ever exceed the free CircleCi limit with\nit. By my calculation I can have about 20+ deployments weekly, so even if I add\nsome tests to the workflow, I still don’t have to worry about it.\n\nThe whole script or a gist\n[https://gist.github.com/AKJAW/9d1f16c6d08f760cc2bc52b29577aeb5#file-ftp-deploy-js]\n:\nconst FtpDeploy = require(\"ftp-deploy\")\nconst ftp = require(\"basic-ftp\")\nconst basicFtpClient = new ftp.Client()\nconst https = require('https');\n\nconst LOCAL_BUILD_DIRECTORY = \"public\"\nconst DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME = \"temp\"\nconst PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME = \"master\"\nconst BROKEN_BUILD = \"broken\"\nconst PRODUCTION_URL = \"https://akjaw.com/\"\n\nconst [ host, user, password ] = process.argv.slice(2)\n\nfunction main() {\n\n return uploadBuildDirectory()\n .catch(error => onError(\"Upload\", error))\n .then(() => renameFtpDirectories())\n .catch(error => onError(\"Rename\", error))\n .then(async backupDirectoryName => {\n const isLive = await isProductionLive()\n if(!isLive) return rollBackProduction(backupDirectoryName)\n })\n .catch(error => onError(\"Rollback\", error))\n .then(() => {\n console.log(\"Deploy FINISHED\")\n basicFtpClient.close()\n process.exit(0)\n })\n}\n\nfunction uploadBuildDirectory(){\n const config = {\n host,\n user,\n password,\n localRoot: `./${LOCAL_BUILD_DIRECTORY}`,\n remoteRoot: `./${DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME}`,\n include: [\"*\", \"**/*\"]\n }\n\n const ftpDeploy = new FtpDeploy()\n\n ftpDeploy.on(\"uploading\", data => {\n const { totalFilesCount, transferredFileCount, filename } = data\n console.log(`${transferredFileCount} out of ${totalFilesCount} ${filename}`)\n })\n\n ftpDeploy.on(\"upload-error\", data => {\n console.log(data.err)\n throw new Error(\"Upload FAILED\")\n })\n\n return ftpDeploy\n .deploy(config)\n .then(() => console.log(`Upload COMPLETED`))\n}\n\nasync function renameFtpDirectories(){\n await basicFtpClient.access({ host, user, password })\n\n const directories = await basicFtpClient.list(\".\")\n const backupDirectoryName = createBackupDirectoryName(directories)\n console.log(`Backup folder name: ${backupDirectoryName}`)\n\n await basicFtpClient.rename(PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME, backupDirectoryName)\n await basicFtpClient.rename(DEPLOY_DIRECTORY_NAME, PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME)\n console.log(\"Renaming COMPLETED\")\n\n return backupDirectoryName\n}\n\nfunction createBackupDirectoryName(directories) {\n const productionDirectory = directories\n .find(fileInfo => fileInfo.name === PRODUCTION_DIRECTORY_NAME)\n\n const formattedDate = formatDate(productionDirectory.modifiedAt)\n\n const numberOfDateOccurrences = directories\n .filter(fileInfo => fileInfo.name.includes(formattedDate))\n .length\n\n if(numberOfDateOccurrences === 0){\n return formattedDate\n } else {\n return `${formattedDate}_${numberOfDateOccurrences + 1}`\n }\n}\n\nfunction formatDate(date) {\n const month = date.getMonth() + 1\n const paddedMonth = month.toString().padStart(2, \"0\")\n const paddedDay = date.getDate().toString().padStart(2, \"0\")\n\n return `${date.getFullYear()}.${paddedMonth}.${paddedDay}`\n}\n\nfunction isProductionLive() {\n return new Promise((resolve) => {\n https\n .get(PRODUCTION_URL, res => {\n console.log(`Production status code ${res.statusCode}`)\n resolve(res.statusCode === 200)\n })\n .on(\"error\", _ => {\n resolve(false)\n })\n })\n}\n\nasync function rollBackProduction(backupDirectoryName){\n await
